* Accredited Entities should provide summary information in the proposal with cross-references to annexes for detailed information, where appropriate.
* Accredited Entities should ensure that annexes provided are consistent with the details provided in the funding proposal. Updates to the funding proposal and/or annexes must be reflected accordingly.

## A.21. Executive summary information
(may, 750 words; approximately 1.5 pages)

Climate change problem and its causes are well known and widely acknowledged for their biodiversity importance and are essential for achieving the objectives of the global climate agenda. Due to the large above- and below-ground biomass in West Kalimantan, Indonesia, the province has been declared an "Exceeding Forest" (EF). EFs are areas that have been declared a REDD+ priority region by the Ministry of Environment and Forestry (MoEF). Despite government efforts at the national level to reduce deforestation and forest degradation, the forests in West Kalimantan continue to be threatened ecosystems of West Kalimantan. West Kalimantan is the 3rd largest province in Indonesia, with an area of 14.6 million hectares, which accounts for approximately 20% of the total land area of Indonesia.

Since 1995, the forests in West Kalimantan have declined by 27%, from 7.5 million hectares to 5.4 million hectares in 2020. The rate of deforestation has been increasing over time: from an average annual deforestation rate of 386,000 hectares during 1995-2000; to 500,000 hectares between 2000 and 2005; to 740,000 hectares in the period from 2005 to 2010; to 836,000 hectares during 2010-2015. # GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE A

## Indonesia, Malaysia, Papua New Guinea, Solomon Islands, and Timor-Leste, over the last four decades. Flooding in agricultural areas led to various setbacks, including damage to farmyards, decreased crop yields, and challenges in accessing food due to the destruction of infrastructure. The impact on agriculture has been significant, with a 30% reduction in rice yield documented. The average reduction in crop yields due to waterlogging generally reached 32%, depending on the duration of flooding.

## Against this background, the project aims to support the Government and people of West Kalimantan in reducing GHG emissions from deforestation and degradation, enhancing forest carbon stocks in degraded areas, and improving livelihoods. The overall objective is to reduce GHG emissions from deforestation and degradation, enhance forest carbon stocks in degraded areas, and improve livelihoods.

## The project will cover the whole area of West Kalimantan and implementation on the ground will focus specifically on three components: (Component 1) Landscape management and planning; (Component 2) Sustainable commodity production and social forestry; and (Component 3) Management, protection, and restoration of degraded areas.

## Climate change results and benefits. The project overall impact is expected to result in annual emission reductions of 2.3 million tonnes CO2e by 2030. In addition, the project will contribute towards the achievement of resilience of approx. 586,000 people (at least 50% women) who reside in the target villages by strengthening their awareness of climate change risk and risk reduction measures. The project will also contribute to sustainable land management through capacity building for FMGs on agriculture and forestry practices. An estimated 14,000 smallholder farmers are expected to adopt low-emission resilient agricultural practices. In addition, approx. 25,000 ha of degraded areas under Component 3 will benefit from sustainable forest management under the social forestry schemes supported under Component 3. Additionally, approx. 67,000 ha of high conservation value (HCV) and HCVCS forests will be established in priority areas of West Kalimantan. In addition, approx. 100,000 ha of degraded land will be restored through the establishment of wildlife corridors and HCVHCs High Conservation Value / High Carbon Stocks area of 100 ha on degraded lands. The project will also contribute to the protection of approx. 420,000 ha of priority areas in West Kalimantan.

## As a contribution to USP target-3 (Ecosystems), 67,000 ha of ecosystems will be under sustainable management including through capacity building for FMGs to improve their livelihoods and reduce GHG emissions. In addition, the project will contribute towards USP target-2 (locally led adaptation) by providing support to PROKLIM communities.

B. PROJECT/PROGRAMME INFORMATION

### B.1 Context (max. 1000 words, approximately 2 pages)

#### B.1.1 Context

- **Deforestation and forest degradation remain a major threat to the last intact ecosystems on the island of Borneo**.
- The region has lost more than half its primary forests in just two decades. West Kalimantan (WK) is Indonesia's third largest province, with a land area of 14.6 million ha (see Annex A). It is one of the most biodiverse regions on earth and home to some of the last remaining intact ecosystems in Indonesia.
- Poor rural communities are increasingly threatened by the impacts of climate change. WK experienced an increase in rainfall intensity from 2005–2014, with a 37% increase in total annual precipitation over this period. The number of days with heavy rain increased by 27%, from 7.5 million to 9.4 million ha.
- **Average deforestation and forest degradation rates of 68,247 ha per year were recorded between 1990–2013** (see Annex B). The largest proportion of the loss in WK is due to conversion of forests into oil palm plantations (5.5 million ha), followed by logging (3.5 million ha) and mining (3.0 million ha).
- **The remaining forest area, covering 8.2 million hectares**, is designated as forest areas.
- The largest part of the forest area is allocated for forest production (4.4 million hectares), which further categorized into permanent forests (1.9 million hectares), temporary forests (0.5 million hectares) and protected forests (0.2 million hectares).
- **Protection forests, nature conservation areas and strict nature reserves cover over 2.3 million hectares**.
- The remaining forest area is divided between large-scale oil palm plantations (3.5 million ha), smallholder oil palm plantations (1.7 million ha) and other land uses such as agriculture (0.9 million ha).
- According to the latest forest map from 2020, **5.5 million hectares of WK are still covered with natural forest (37% of total land area)**.
- These remaining forests provide critical ecosystem services for local communities in terms of food and water security, nutrient cycling among others.

#### B.1.2 Forests

- **Forests are critical for communities who depend on natural resources for their livelihoods**. Although the forestry sector has been a major contributor to Indonesia's economy over the past decades, it is now facing increasing pressure from large-scale land use conversion.
- Local communities have been managing land and forest resources using their own customary laws and practices for centuries. These traditional systems of resource management are often referred to as "indigenous forest governance" with 572,199 hectares covered by 224 licenses as of September 2023.

#### B.1.3 Forests and Livelihoods

- **Forests provide a wide range of ecosystem services** such as the provision of food and water, nutrient cycling among others.
- The livelihoods of approximately 2.1 million people (50% male and 50% female) are directly dependent on forests in WK. These communities depend heavily on forest resources for their livelihoods, which is mainly inhabited by local indigenous peoples. Around 47% of the people in WK are experiencing poverty**, if we accept the World Bank's definition of poverty as a daily income below $1.90. The majority of these poor people live in remote areas and depend heavily on forest resources for their livelihoods.
- **The Government of West Kalimantan (GKW)** has set an ambitious goal to achieve a 60% reduction in greenhouse gas emissions, equivalent to 5 million metric tons CO2 per year from deforestation alone by 2030.

B

### 2. Climate Change Impacts on Forests in West Kalimantan

**8. WKT is affected by climate change due to changes in both temperature and rainfall. Generally, rainfall changes across Indonesia show an increase in intensity, particularly during the wet season, and a decrease in rainfall intensity, especially in the dry season. The average annual precipitation has increased from 1970-2015.**

**9. Historical temperature conditions in West Kalimantan generally show that the areas with the highest temperatures are located in the western part of the province, while the lowest temperatures are found in the eastern region. Apart from being influenced by altitude, temperature conditions in WKT are also greatly influenced by rainfall and wind direction. The average annual temperature ranges from 25°C to 30°C, with a temperature range from 29°C–32°C, with the average ranging from 24°C to 28°C. The period from 1981 to 2015, all regions experienced an increase in average annual precipitation and a decrease in average annual temperature. In the minimum temperature, most areas experience a lower trend until now.**

**10. Temperatures are projected to increase significantly in the future, as a result of increased greenhouse gas emissions. The average annual temperature is expected to increase by 2.5°C–3.4°C with maximum temperatures greater than the 99th percentile will increase by 7% from current conditions. The increase in average annual precipitation is projected to be between 10% and 20%.**

**11. The spatial distribution of average annual precipitation shows that the areas with the highest precipitation are located in the western part of the province, while the lowest precipitation is found in the eastern region. When divided into decades, from 1976-1985 to 1996-2005 and 2006-2015, there was a relatively lower average annual precipitation. However, the intensity level of annual precipitation varies across regions. The period from 1981 to 2015, all regions experienced an increase in average annual precipitation during the period from 2011-2012, where there was an overall increase in average annual precipitation at all regions.**

**12. Annual rainfall is expected to decrease by up to 30% compared with historical conditions in the Berau and Sambas regencies are expected to have a decrease in annual rainfall. The annual rainfall rise in Kapuas Hulu (Pahang) and West Kalimantan is expected to occur during the peak of the rainy season (JJA).**

**13. The duration of the wet season in WKT is expected to be reduced by up to 60% compared with historical conditions. Furthermore, some regions may experience a decrease in annual rainfall due to an increase in temperature and summer droughts. Both forest and peat fires are major hazards in WKT. The occurrence of fires caused serious damage in many areas during the dry season (DJF) and wet season (JJA).**

**14. Future fire risk is expected to be higher than historical conditions, especially in the Berau and Sambas regencies. Both forest and peat fires are major hazards in WKT. The occurrence of fires caused serious damage in many areas during the dry season (DJF) and wet season (JJA).**

**15. Over the last five years, total forest loss due to wildfires reached 300,000 ha.**

**16. The increase in temperature will lead to an increase in extreme weather events, with impacts felt by many people and sectors. The increase of temperature due to human activities that induced climate change would bring more frequent and intense extreme climate events. Reports from the National Disaster Agency (BNPB) from 1998-2015 show that the number of deaths caused by natural disasters has increased significantly year after year, but the impacts of climate change will increase the risk of more severe and deadly events.**

## B

### Climate Change and Its Impacts on West Kalimantan

#### 1.0 Introduction
2018 showed floods, strong winds, and land fires were the most common hazards that occurred in many locations in WK. Landslides and droughts also occurred but not as often as the other three hazards.

#### 1.1 Overview of Climate Change Impacts on West Kalimantan
Climate change is projected to strongly impact WK, where it will have a disproportionate impact on particularly vulnerable groups including poor households, agricultural and forest-dependent communities, indigenous peoples, women, children, and older people. These groups are already highly exposed to climate-related hazards in their ecosystems and sectors and ecosystems, making them particularly vulnerable to the rapid acceleration of climate-related hazards and associated impacts.

B.1 Mitigation potential and needs

20. The forests of WK are globally acknowledged for their biodiversity importance and are essential for achieving the objectives of the global climate agenda, and the UNFCCC Paris Agreement. Due to the large above- and below-ground biomass, WK is considered as one of the most important carbon sinks in Indonesia* and a REDD+ prioritized region by the Indonesian Ministry of Environment and Forestry (MoEF).

21. The forests of WK have experienced significant deforestation and forest degradation over the last decades*. A major driver for this change was the MiDEF land cover and land use map from 1990 to 2003 shows an annual deforestation of 8.3 thousand hectares. The province shows a distinct trend in annual deforestation compared to national trends, with a peak around 1995–1996 followed by a decline until the mid-2000s and then increasing during that period. The annual deforestation in WK from 1990–2003 was around 71 thousand hectares per year, which is more than double of national average (34 thousand hectares). The deforestation rate has decreased since the period 2010 to 2020. Overall, forests in WK declined by 27%, from 7.5 million hectares in 1990 to 5.4 million hectares in 2020.

23. The forests in the five provinces are severely fragmented and isolated. In West Kalimantan, 27.1 thousand hectares of logging, 8.8 thousand hectares in Riau, 6.8 thousand hectares in North Sumatra, 5.9 thousand hectares in East Aceh Regency, 4.3 thousand hectares in Central Sulawesi and 0.8 thousand hectares in South Sulawesi.

24. In terms of forest cover, West Kalimantan has the largest area of forest on mineral soils (61%), followed by Riau with 57%, North Sumatra with 55%, East Aceh Regency with 39% and Central Sulawesi with 38%. Out of five provinces in Indonesia WK is the fifth largest area of forest emissions on mineral soils and the third largest area among peatlands. The province has a high potential for carbon sequestration through afforestation, which is highly relevant for achieving the national NDC goals. In addition, forest loss in WKS watersheds increases the vulnerability to climate change.

26. Based on the revised reference level of WR (2nd FRL), the reference level since the period of 2006-2030 was 8.6 million hectares and the reference level for the period after 2031 is 7.5 million hectares. The difference between the 2nd FREL and 2nd FRL is not fully comparable, due to different scope (inclusion/exclusion emissions sources) and emission factors used by the two reference levels. The 2nd FRL includes emissions from forest fires, peat fires, land use change, deforestation, conversion of forest into other uses, biomass burning and peat decomposition. The annual removal from enhancement of forest carbon stock is estimated at 10 million tonnes CO2e per year (2006-2030) and 9 million tonnes CO2e per year after 2031 from peat decomposition. This baseline is expected to be the level of emissions with business-as-usual scenario until 2050, which is also the reference level for calculating the emission reduction from the period of 2021 to 2050.

B.1.4 Project overview and implementation area

27. Significant hurdles remain that limit progress on strengthening the resilience of local communities and ecosystems in WK. These include lack of capacity, limited access to finance, and weak institutional arrangements. There is a need for cross-cutting climate action in the agriculture, forest and other land use sector (AFOLU) in WK to strengthen climate resilience and reduce greenhouse gas emissions from deforestation and forest degradation.

## Figure 2: Scenario without project

### Institutional and regulatory frameworks of provincial and regency level cannot be timely amended

- **Institutional framework**: The institutional framework for the implementation of climate change adaptation policies is weak, and there are no clear guidelines on how to integrate climate change into existing development plans.
- **Regulatory framework**: Regulatory frameworks for forests with HCVFs in non-forest areas (APLs) are not strengthened and remain forest-based at all levels. The lack of a regulatory framework for the use of land in these areas is also a challenge.

### Overview of challenges for the adoption and scaling of cross-cutting climate action in WRI

#### Institutional and regulatory frameworks of provincial and regency level cannot be timely amended
- **Institutional framework**: The institutional framework for the implementation of climate change adaptation policies is weak, and there are no clear guidelines on how to integrate climate change into existing development plans.
- **Regulatory framework**: Regulatory frameworks for forests with HCVFs in non-forest areas (APLs) are not strengthened and remain forest-based at all levels. The lack of a regulatory framework for the use of land in these areas is also a challenge.

#### Existing, unsustainable land and forest business models are not replaced because investors are not willing to invest in improved agricultural practices or forest management systems that are more sustainable.
- **Investment**: Investors continue to invest in conventional rather than in climate-resilient agriculture and forest land and forest business models.
- **Innovation**: There is a lack of innovation in the development of new technologies, products, and services for climate change adaptation.

#### Most PAM units cannot fulfill their criteria of effective PAM organizations and do not review the authority to utilize forests in unforested areas. This results in deforestation and degradation, including illegal logging.
- **Micromanagement**: Micromanagement plans of PAM units do not reflect current national and provincial climate mitigation strategies and action plans and are not aligned with the national forest policy.
- **Limited resources**: Most PAMs cannot fulfill their criteria of effective PAM organizations and do not review the authority to utilize forests in unforested areas. This results in deforestation and degradation, including illegal logging.

#### Information and data exchanges with other government offices at provincial and central level
- **Information sharing**: There is a lack of information and data exchange between government offices.
- **Social inclusion**: There is no social inclusion or empowerment for local officers.

### Forest Management Units (FMUs) do not have the resources and capacity to fully implement sustainable forest management, including:
- **Micromanagement plans**: Micromanagement plans of PAM units do not reflect current national and provincial climate mitigation strategies and action plans.
- **Limited resources**: Most PAMs cannot fulfill their criteria of effective PAM organizations and do not review the authority to utilize forests in unforested areas. This results in deforestation and degradation, including illegal logging.

#### Information and data exchanges with other government offices at provincial and central level
- **Information sharing**: There is a lack of information and data exchange between government offices.
- **Social inclusion**: There is no social inclusion or empowerment for local officers.

## B

### Theory of change narrative and diagram (max. 1500 words, approximately 3 pages plus diagram)

#### Background
The project will address in the WK region, clustered into four categories including governance, technical and institutional capacities, financial markets, and Table 2 provides an overview and brief description of the measures that will be implemented under each category. The table also indicates which sub-national level or sector each measure falls within. Each sub-national level or sector is further divided by the following broad categories: climate change issues, land use and land cover changes, water resources, food security, and droughts. Strengthening development plans to incorporate climate adaptation and mitigation measures is essential. Coordinating with relevant stakeholders at all levels will be critical for success. The project aims to enhance adaptation policies. Gaps in forest and land governance at the sub-national level hinder the implementation of forest programs. Lack of institutional capacity, particularly among local communities in land and forest management is insufficient, leading to social conflicts and hindered participation in social forest programs.

#### Climate change issues
Climate change issues link integration into Regional Development Plans at provincial and national levels, including water resources, food security, droughts, and droughts. Strengthening development plans to incorporate climate adaptation and mitigation measures is essential.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 10

## B

### Project Barrier related to Planning and Administration

**Government (i) lack of land use planning is resulting in overlapping claims and challenges in protecting High Conservation Value and High Carbon Stock areas within concessions. Inconsistent policies exacerbate the issue, allowing multiple actors to claim the same area.**

Despite the establishment of multi-stakeholder forums, coordination among them remains weak, hindering effective management of forest resources at both national and sub-national levels.

At the same time, attention needs to be paid to ensure policies, strategies and programs are gender-sensitive and inclusive of climate change on IPRs, especially women, and strengthen Gender Equality and Social Inclusion (GESI).

### Barrier Category 2: Institutional and Technical Capacities

Institutional capacity is weak in terms of the implementation of sustainable practices, particularly at the local government level. The current coordination institution faces challenges due to authority barriers, lack of capacity, and insufficient resources.

Lack of technical capacities and support for communities hampers the implementation of social forest initiatives. Communities require more effective and timely access to responsive technical assistance, and access to finance is essential for implementing sustainable business models.

### Barrier Category 3: Financial

Limited financial mechanisms impede the mobilization of investments for sustainable forest land and management. Operational challenges exist within the national climate change funding mechanism, which involves multiple actors and institutions.

Insufficient finance hinders the implementation of social forests with limitations on private sector contributions and access to credit. The lack of a clear roadmap for scaling up social forests and limited availability of technology need in progress to close the access gap and channel funding towards sustainable practices. Related technical assistance is also lacking, which further impedes the development of social forest initiatives.

Inadequate institutional budgets for forest management organizations limit their capacity to oversee forest land use planning and implementation. The COVID-19 pandemic hindered their ability to implement mechanisms and support sustainable forest management.

### Barrier Category 4: Business Models

Lack of sustainable business models and value chain linkages hinders the adoption of sustainable agricultural practices, particularly among smallholder farmers. This is exacerbated by a lack of access to finance for smallholders and SMEs, including women-led SMEs, highlighting the need for proven and scalable business models that can be scaled up.

### Alignment of proposed outcomes, outputs and activities with identified barriers

The table below provides an alignment between the proposed project outcomes, outputs, and activities. Table 2 provides a summary of the linkages between barriers addressed and corresponding activities.

### Project Goal and Theory of Change

**Goal:** This project's overall objective is to reduce GHG emissions from deforestation and degradation, enhance forest carbon stocks through reforestation and forest land rehabilitation, improve good agricultural practices and natural resource management (NRM) systems, increase the resilience of communities in West Kalimantan, Indonesia, by increasing forest and agro-forestry land use due to increased temperatures and droughts and it reduced agricultural production due to lack of water. The project will also reduce GHG emissions from deforestation and degradation through enhanced carbon sequestration at provincial level, which are representative of the national scale. These are represented as a series of milestones leading up to success in strengthening the long-term resilience of vulnerable communities on the island of Borneo against the impacts of climate change on men and women and ensuring the mainstreaming of measures to strengthen GES.

**Theory of Change (ToC):** The project will be successful if it is able to create a market for sustainable products, which will attract public and private investments and provide incentives for sustainable practices within communities. This will lead to increased forest cover and improved livelihoods in West Kalimantan, Indonesia.

The ToC diagram, goal and goal statement respond to a series of activities, outputs and outcomes (described in detail in the project proposal).

The following diagram (Figure 3) shows the ToC for the project, which illustrates the change pathway and investments at different stages of the intervention logic proposed by the project. Based on the inputs in the figure diagram, the project has established an integrated self-supporting approach, addressing the identified barriers.

### Project Co-benefits
The project will generate several co-benefits, the main co-benefits can be resumed to:
- Increased community security and increased income
- Enhanced conservation of biodiversity and water resources
- Sustainable livelihoods and development potential

### Assumptions and Risks
Assumptions: The project design is based on the following assumptions (assumptions are further described in sections E.3 and E.5 for each relevant project indicator).
Risks: These risks have been identified as being most likely to impact the project.

1. **Increased financial and technical support for IPs**: This will provide vulnerable IPs, including women, with access to land and rights as incentive to manage forests and landscapes sustainably.
2. **Enhanced conservation of biodiversity and water resources**: The EU and other economies, including the UK and US, are currently implementing or exploring regulatory demand-side measures to limit their deforestation footprint (see Annex 7a). This will provide IPs with an additional market for their products and services, which can be used as a tool to support forest conservation.
3. **Strengthened governance, accountability, and transparency in forest management**: The project will contribute to the implementation of the Forest Law Enforcement, Governance and Trade (FLEGT) Action Plan, which aims at reducing illegal logging and promoting sustainable forest management practices.

### B.3 Project programme description (max. 2500 words, approximately 5 pages)

The project aims to support the Government and people of WK in the transition to sustainable and climate resilient management practices through the implementation of three main components.

1. The implementation period will be seven years and consists of a phased approach.
2. Outputs and results are expected from activities built on proven approaches including: (i) Community Forest Management (CFM), (ii) Gender Mainstreaming, (iii) Land Tenure Security, namely: GIZ, Solidaridad and MuGII as being active in WK for many years; Gender Mainstreaming will be practiced across all components of the project. The following are some examples of activities that will be implemented under this component: FORCLIME and SASSO projects.
3. Some measures are mentioned below and in Chapter G2, while the detailed action plan is presented in Annex 5.

## B

Climate Change (CC) is a cross-sectoral initiative. It is well placed to coordinate cross-cutting climate change measures across sectors and the new Provincial Body on Climate Change will take over a leading role. Transitioning towards low-carbon development, including adaptation to climate change, is also addressed by this output. High Biodiversity and Carbon Areas is also addressed by this output. Existing entities, such as provincial working groups on biodiversity and carbon areas, will be integrated into the new Provincial Body on Climate Change for mainstreaming into adaptation and forest management plans (see Gender Action Plan, Annex B8 – GAP).

### 4.9 The Output Supports Creation of Mechanisms to Address the Implementation of Adaptation Measures

The output supports creation of mechanisms to address the implementation of adaptation measures at the provincial level. A dedicated grant mechanism will be set up on IP to address lack finance and technical capacity for adaptation, as well as to support the development of a monitoring system for adaptation. The output also provides for a screening process for projects. Finally, this output supports creation of mechanisms that extend beyond the project’s lifespan.

### 4.9.1 Adaptation Mainstreaming in Mid-Term, Spatial, and Other Regional Development Plans

All fully-conceived provincial development plans will be updated to include adaptation measures. The (sub)actions will focus on the concrete development of adaptation policies that are not in place yet. Capacity building measures will support provinces in developing their own adaptation strategies and mainstreaming them into their development plans at the level they see fit.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 15

## Sub-activity 1.1.2.2: Mitigation of impacts into provincial development plans (DEP - GEI)

This sub-activity will ensure that mitigation measures are integrated in the Provincial Development Plans (PDPs) and other relevant documents, including the National Climate Change Action Plan (NCCAP), to achieve the GHG emission reduction targets set by the ENDC. The GHG emissions accounting method used at the sub-national level, and aligned with national mitigation action plans, will be applied in the PDPs.

## Sub-activity 1.1.2.3: Support implementation of enabling conditions for mitigation activities (GEI - GEI)

This sub-activity will implement the enabling conditions required to support the development and implementation of mitigation activities at the provincial level. This includes:

* **Supporting the development of a provincial GHG inventory:** The provincial GHG inventory will be developed in accordance with the national GHG inventory methodology, taking into account the specific context and data availability.
* **Developing enabling conditions for mitigation activities (GEI):** This sub-activity will support the development of enabling conditions necessary to implement mitigation activities at the provincial level. These conditions may include:

  * Expansion of climate change resilience climate change research and innovation (to develop a model for REDD+ and GHG Hot Spot Zone 2030 mitigation activities)
  * Upgrading the national map post-2015 (to update the national map post-2015 in accordance with the Paris Agreement)
  * Development of enabling conditions to support the development of a transparent and accountable reporting system for mitigation activities at the provincial level

## Sub-activity 1.1.2.4: Support implementation of enabling conditions for adaptation activities (GEI - GEI)

This sub-activity will implement the enabling conditions required to support the development and implementation of adaptation activities at the provincial level.

* **Supporting the development of a provincial GHG inventory:** The provincial GHG inventory will be developed in accordance with the national GHG inventory methodology, taking into account the specific context and data availability.
* **Developing enabling conditions for adaptation activities (GEI):** This sub-activity will support the development of enabling conditions necessary to implement adaptation activities at the provincial level. These conditions may include:

  * Expansion of climate change resilience climate change research and innovation (to develop a model for REDD+ and GHG Hot Spot Zone 2030 mitigation activities)
  * Upgrading the national map post-2015 (to update the national map post-2015 in accordance with the Paris Agreement)
  * Development of enabling conditions to support the development of a transparent and accountable reporting system for adaptation activities at the provincial level

## Output 1.2: Developed land use plans which consider climate change and identified HCVF/HCS areas

This output will be developed through the following activities:

* **Activity 1.2.1 Strengthening the legal and regulatory framework and implementation of High Biodiversity and High Carbon Stock (HCVF/HCS) areas with the support of national regulatory frameworks such as EEAC or Provincial Forest Law:** The government will ensure that HCVF/HCS areas are protected under existing laws, including the EEAC Act. The government will also ensure that HCVF/HCS protection in private sector concessions, particularly on non-state forests.

## Activity 1.2.1 Strengthening the legal and regulatory framework and implementation of High Biodiversity and High Carbon Stock (HCVF/HCS) areas with the support of national regulatory frameworks such as EEAC or Provincial Forest Law

This activity aims to ensure:

* **Identification of High Biodiversity and Carbon Areas:** Stakeholders will be involved in identifying HCVF/HCS areas, including local communities.
* **Stakeholder legal standing on existing laws:** The government will ensure that stakeholders have the necessary legal standing to participate in decision-making processes related to HCVF/HCS areas.

The following activities will be undertaken:

1. Review and update of relevant legislation (EEAC Act) to include provisions for HCVF/HCS areas.
2. Capacity building on existing laws, including EEAC Act, for stakeholders involved in the identification and management of HCVF/HCS areas.
3. Support from the government (through Governor's and乙方的Decisions) and secure support from the government (through Regional Councils) to ensure that HCVF/HCS areas are protected under existing laws.

The expected results will be:

* **Identification of HCVF/HCS areas:** The identification process will involve local communities, stakeholders, and relevant authorities.
* **Stakeholder legal standing on existing laws:** Stakeholders involved in the identification and management of HCVF/HCS areas will have the necessary legal standing to participate in decision-making processes related to these areas.

The project results include:

1. (i) Review and update of relevant legislation (EEAC Act).
2. (ii) Capacity building for stakeholders on existing laws, including EEAC Act.
3. (iii) Support from the government (through Governor's and乙方的Decisions) and secure support from the government (through Regional Councils) to ensure that HCVF/HCS areas are protected under existing laws.

The project will be implemented in collaboration with relevant stakeholders, including local communities, provincial authorities, and international partners. The project will also involve capacity building activities for stakeholders involved in the identification and management of HCVF/HCS areas.

### Output 1.3: Established and implemented dedicated grant mechanism provides adequate financing and management of IPs and other conservation activities in West Kalimantan

The activity seeks to emphasize and guarantee the inclusion and active participation of Indigenous Peoples (IPs), recognizing their rights as holders of traditional knowledge and custodians of biodiversity, and ensuring that IPs are involved in all stages of development efforts. The activity builds on sub-consultations and engagement with IPs throughout the project cycle, including for land use planning, forest management, and other conservation activities. This is achieved through a dedicated grant mechanism to support IPs' capacity building (including for human rights), empowering them to sustainably govern and protect their traditional lands. Furthermore, by facilitating IPs' access to finance and resources, the activity aims to ensure that IPs have greater control over their land use decisions and access to private sector resources, fostering collaboration for sustainable development.

An important outcome of this activity is the establishment of a dedicated grant mechanism to support IPs in managing their traditional territories according to their ancestral laws and customs. This ensures that IPs are recognized for their ecological, cultural, social significance, and the management practices often emphasize sustainable use and conservation.

## Activity 2.1. Scaling up sustainable land and forest-based business model for West Kalimantan

This Activity provides support in designing and expanding a sustainable land and forest-based business model to systematically scale-up the implementation of REDD+ activities, including afforestation/reforestation, agroforestry, and other ecosystem services. The objective is to facilitate and promote the engagement of investors, developers, and companies in purchasing from and investing in sustainable land and forest-based products and services. This will be achieved by providing them with the necessary assistance and resources. By creating an environment that encourages such investments, it is expected that the scale-up of REDD+ activities will increase significantly.

### Table 3: Incentives for private sector and smallholder producers

| Private sector | Smallholder Producers |
| --- | --- |
| • Compliance with national policies and international sustainability standards | • Access to Markets and Finance |
| • Secure sourcing | • Increase from Mills and Markets such as Sinar Mas, PT. Tiga Benua |
| • Access to sustainable products (alignment with shifting markets) | • Improved timber legality status |
| • Access to market with the implementation of no deforestation, no peatland degradation, no conversion of high carbon stock areas, and no human rights violations | • Increased ecosystem resilience and reduced vulnerability |
| • Access to innovative green investment | • Productivity improvements |

### Activity 2.1.3. Implementing and up-scaling the adoption of proven approaches for reducing emissions and enhancing carbon stocks in West Kalimantan (including agroforestry)

The sub-activity will support the implementation of measures to reduce the direct deforestation and forest degradation, reduce CO2e emissions and protect natural resources by introducing and scaling sustainable smallholder forest management practices. The sub-activity will also work closely with government agencies at local, Regency and province level, through capacity building and technical assistance in order to increase their capacity for implementing REDD+ activities or by engaging them in the Sustainable Forest Management (SFM) approach to increase their transformational capacities towards sustainable land use.

The sub-activity will support the implementation of Solandara’s plans to engage with and support 12,000 smallholder farmers in sustainable agriculture. Based on Solandara's experience during trainings in the field a dropout rate of 20% is expected.

## Activity 4: Enhancing multi-stakeholder dialogue and platforms for low-emission and climate-resilient agriculture and private sector investment

### Sub-activity 2.1.1: Improved capacity in agroforestry and sustainable smallholder farming (IEF - Subsidized, BPSLs)

This sub-activity will focus on the establishment of a multi-stakeholder platform for agroforestry and sustainable smallholder farming (MSF) at the Regency level to promote investments in low-emission and climate-resilient agriculture by the Regencies' private sector. The establishment of Regency-level MSFs will be based on the successful model established under the Sub-activity 2.1.1.1: Improved capacity in agroforestry and sustainable smallholder farming (IEF - Subsidized, BPSLs). # Activity 3.14 GRASS - Greening Agricultural Smallholder Supply Chains in Kapuas Hulu (on BMCF)

This activity promotes sustainable agriculture based on a jurisdictional approach in the Kapuas Hulu biosphere reserve, which combines improved agricultural production methods with participatory land use planning. The objective is to increase the resilience of smallholder farmers and their families by improving the diversification of crops and field structures. Protected natural areas are identified in planning processes and excluded from agricultural activities. This activity also aims at increasing the economic and social resilience of smallholder farmers at the base of global supply chains in Kapuas Hulu region. In addition, it is expected to increase the number of women involved in agriculture (as a result of 1,100 smallholders, including 30% women), and that the agricultural area on which smallholders are engaged will be reduced by 25%. The activity will also contribute to increasing the resilience of smallholder farmers against climate change.

### Sub-activity 3.14.1 - GRASS: Greening Agricultural Smallholder Supply Chains in Kapuas Hulu (on BMCF)

The following steps will be taken:

* Establishing a multi-stakeholder platform for sustainable agricultural supply chains
* Providing and procuring best practices of sustainable supply chains and resilient weatherfront farmer to farmer exchange
* Developing and implementing an action plan for the greening of agricultural smallholder supply chains in Kapuas Hulu region

### Activity 3.15 Improving sustainable landscape management and smallholder palm oil market inclusion (on BMCF)

The National Initiatives for Sustainable and Climate-Smart Oil Palm Smallholders (NI-SCOPS II) initiatives joint venture with the Indonesian government, private sector, and civil society organizations is a project that is capable of improving the lives of the smallholder farmers who work those lands. The development of these key partners will be supported by the following activities:

* Sub-activity 3.15.1 - NI-SCOPS II: Sustainable palm oil production in Indonesia

This sub-activity aims to increase the productivity and profitability of smallholders through growth in environmental sustainability requirements compliant palm-oil supply, by working with all smallholder farmers in the project area (approximately 20,000) to improve their livelihoods. The project area is similar to Europe. With smallholders already providing one-third of the global oil palm production, NI-SCOPS Phase II has the potential to significantly increase Indonesia's share of sustainable palm oil production.

* Sub-activity 3.15.2 - Sustainable palm oil market inclusion in Indonesia

This sub-activity aims at increasing the productivity and profitability of smallholders through growth in environmental sustainability requirements compliant palm-oil supply, by working with all smallholder farmers in the project area (approximately 20,000) to improve their livelihoods.

* Sub-activity 3.15.3 - Sustainable palm oil production in Indonesia

This sub-activity aims at increasing the productivity and profitability of smallholders through growth in environmental sustainability requirements compliant palm-oil supply, by working with all smallholder farmers in the project area (approximately 20,000) to improve their livelihoods.

* Sub-activity 3.15.4 - Sustainable palm oil market inclusion in Indonesia

This sub-activity aims at increasing the productivity and profitability of smallholders through growth in environmental sustainability requirements compliant palm-oil supply, by working with all smallholder farmers in the project area (approximately 20,000) to improve their livelihoods.

This project will support the FMU (WPHP, KPHL and KPHK) system and develop their capacity for implementing diverse activities, including: SFM, Forest Land Rehabilitation, Ecosystem-based Adaptation (EBA), protected areas management, and forest carbon accounting. ### Outputs 63-65: Supporting Forest Management Units (FMUs) in implementing climate informed protection and sustainable management of forest and peat ecosystems

**Output 63:** This output focuses on overcoming the new regulatory challenge with the emission of EUROC 2020 (now EUROPA 2021) and its impact on the FMU’s ability to manage their forests in a sustainable manner. The sub-activity will support planning, organizing, monitoring and controlling. Forest utilization activities can only be carried out by forest enterprises or organizations that have obtained an authorization from the Ministry of Environment (MoE). This output is also aimed at supporting the development capacity of FMU organizations and other forest managers to manage their business model in a sustainable manner.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND GRANT PROPOSAL V3.0 PAGE 22

## Activity 3.1.1 Advancing social forest management including building awareness of local communities on forest access and medium- to long-term planning up until 2030.

The project supports the development of social forestry management plans as a cornerstone of inclusive, community-based forest management.

### Sub-activity 3.1.1.1 Developing and implementing SF management plans and support new SF permit proposals for local communities (GEF_GCF_BPSH)

This activity aims at developing implementable activities on the ground that will restore degraded ecosystems, increase resilience to climate change, and improve livelihoods of forest-dependent communities in a sustainable manner.

The sub-activity includes:

* **Sub-activity 3.1.1.1.1 Developing SF management plans:** This sub-activity seeks to develop sufficient and balanced information about SF scheme forms and permits, funding processes for the community to represent themselves and how to make decisions on forest access and use, as well as a clear understanding of the SF permit application process.

* **Sub-activity 3.1.1.1.2 Implementing SF management plans:** This sub-activity seeks to implement SF management plans in a participatory manner and link them directly to village land-use planning (VLP) and technical forest certification from the government to ensure that SF management plans are not only developed but also implemented.

* **Sub-activity 3.1.1.1.3 Supporting new SF permit proposals:** This sub-activity seeks to support the development of new SF management plans (PWP). Up to 100 ha of new SF will be supported by this project and another 100 ha out of existing SF permits will be converted into new SF permits.

### Sub-activity 3.1.2.1 Capacity building for SF permit holders (GEF_GCF_BPSH)

This activity will enable all the rural VLPs to have similar capacity in terms of forest management and access rights, as well as a better understanding of their role in the SF scheme. The sub-activity seeks to achieve this by:

* **Sub-activity 3.1.2.1.1 Capacity building for SF permit holders:** This sub-activity will provide training on GEF_GCF_BPSH and related edited community products, including the creation of VLPs models and capital resources (GEF_GCF_BPSH). The sub-activity seeks to enable all rural VLPs to have similar capacity in terms of forest management and access rights as well as a better understanding of their role in the SF scheme.

* **Sub-activity 3.1.2.1.2 Capacity building for SF permit holders:** This sub-activity will provide training on GEF_GCF_BPSH and related edited community products, including the creation of VLPs models and capital resources (GEF_GCF_BPSH). The sub-activity seeks to enable all rural VLPs to have similar capacity in terms of forest management and access rights as well as a better understanding of their role in the SF scheme.

### B.4.1 Project management, implementation and execution structure

#### Role of GIZ as Accredited Entity (AE)
68. The German Agency for International Cooperation (Gesellschaft für Internationale Zusammenarbeit) (GIZ) GmbH is a German-based private limited liability, non-profit company owned by the Federal Republic of Germany; it is the Accredited Entity (AE); of this project. GIZ will be responsible for the implementation and execution of the project (see also Section B.4.2 in the next section). To avoid conflicts of interest, these two functions - accreditation and implementation function - are split between two different entities.

69. In its capacity as AE, GIZ will assume oversight responsibility of the project, as defined in the Accreditation Master Agreement (AMA) with the Green Climate Fund (GCF), including ensuring that all project activities comply with the AMA. GIZ will provide oversight, guidance, and quality assurance of Badan Penyelindung Dana Lingkungan Hidup (BPDLH) and SNAL, as well as other entities involved in the project implementation, to ensure compliance with the AMA, monitoring and ensure regular reporting. In addition, GIZ will also be member of the project steering committee.

70. In its capacity as implementing entity, GIZ will perform the following functions (see Section B.4.2 for details):
- The commissions by the German Federal Ministry for Economic Cooperation and Development (BMZ) to implement the project.
- The Funded Activity Agreement (FAA) between GCF and GIZ as basis for the transfer of GCF proceeds to GIZ.
- The Project Implementation Agreement (PIA) with BPDLH and SNAL, as Implementing Entities based on GIZ’s standard operating procedures for grant agreements. The grant agreement with SNAL will also include its co-financing agreement with YSNIL.
- The sub-grant agreements between GIZ and the yet-to-be-defined entity as well as YSNIL respectively.

71. All project activities are to be carried out in accordance with the terms of the PIA, FAA, and sub-grants. Project activities already agreed between GIZ and the two EEIs, YSNIL and the yet-to-be-defined entity do not have any direct financial implications for the GCF. The GCF will only provide funds to GIZ as per the terms of its budget; all such disbursements via SNAL and BPDLH, respectively. The yet-to-be-defined entity/via by BPDHL and YSNIL.

## GIZ as Executing Entity (EE)

72. GIZ has been operating in Indonesia since 1975 and currently employs approximately 419 staff members, most of them based in Jakarta.

Specifically, GIZ Indonesia has been working on climate change, forestry, and agricultural supply chains in Indonesia since the 1980s and current technical assistance in the green economy sector is part of this long-term commitment to Indonesia.

73. In its capacity as EE, GIZ will implement its assigned activities with due diligence and efficiency. It will be responsible for:

- Managing the project budget assigned to GIZ as EE.
- Coordinating project implementation with other entities involved in the project.
- Reporting to BMZ regarding Germany's financial contribution to the project, as well as the overall progress of project implementation.
- Coordinating project implementation with the other EE, co-financing partners, counterparts, other relevant donors, and implementing partners (IPs) for the project.
- Liaising with and reporting to the Project Steering Committee (PSC).
- Using the funds assigned to GIZ as EE for the implementation of the project, as well as the NDA.
- Supporting the establishment and management of the Project Management Unit (PMU).

## B.4 Other Executing Entities

75. GIZ, as the AE, conducted a thorough enhanced due diligence assessment with all Executing Entities which we recommend to the BMZ for approval. This ensured that the Executing Entities have the legal personality of the Executing Entities to enter into a legally binding agreement with GIZ and their ability to receive and properly manage funds.

76. Besides GIZ, the Project will have three additional Executing Entities:

- Kementerian Lingkungan Hidup dan Kehutanan (KLHK)
- Indonesian Ministry of Environment and Forestry (MoEF)
- Badan Pengelola Dana Lingkungan Hidup (BPDH)

### B.4.1 Executive Entity – Badan Pengelola Dana Lingkungan Hidup (BPDHL)

77. The Badan Pengelola Dana Lingkungan Hidup (BPDHL) is a public service agency (a BLU in Indonesia), a non-entities unit accountable to the Ministry of Finance (MoF) which was established in October 2015 under Presidential Regulation No. 34/2015. The BPDHL has been given the mandate and authority to manage its operations and is not dependent on the state budget (APBN), although it can source funds from it.

This gives the BPDHL a high degree of operational independence, which allows it to operate in line with its mandate, without being constrained by the state budget. The BPDHL has been given the authority to align with, or overrule, the government's financial and climate commitments; the specific benefits include: BPDHL will act as the main implementing entity for IPs that are not part of the Ministry of Environment and Forestry (MoEF) or other institutions responsible to implement parts of the funded activity such as DLHK, LIM, Perhutani, DLH Provinsi DKI Jakarta, DLH Provinsi Jawa Barat, DLH Provinsi Sumatera Utara, DLH Provinsi Sulawesi Selatan, or through implementing agencies.

78. Under BPDHL, the IPs will use the dedicated grant mechanism for IPs which will follow standard operational procedures of the TERRA Fund (in line with GCF and COP regulations) already in place within BPDHL. The IPs will be responsible for:

- Coordinating with the project implementation partners (provincial government institutions, NGOs/CSOs, and village communities) to ensure that their activities are aligned with the project.
- Participate in both the Steering Committee and the Project Management Unit (PMU).
- Work closely with GIZ as EE on all aspects of project implementation.

Ensure that all activities promoted and developed under the dedicated grant mechanism for IPs are in accordance with the respective Grant Agreement with GIZ and the strategic guidance of the Project Steering Committee.

- Ensure that all of the information to the Project Management Unit in case any modifications or updates are desired at the operational or strategic level of the dedicated grant mechanism for IPs.

**Executing Entity – Solidaridad Network Asia Limited (SNAL)**

- SNAL is a global civil society organization that facilitates the development of fair and profitable supply chains for small-scale and family farmers.
- SNAL works in four major interlocked result areas:
  - Transforming agricultural value chains (product or produce level)
  - Building supportive business ecosystems (business level)
  - Strengthening farmer organizations
  - Stimulate demand for sustainably produced goods (market level)

**SNAL’s Approach**

- SNAL will work through seven independently supervised regional expertise centres (RECs). The Asia REC (SAREC) is located in India, oversees the operations of 51 SNLs in countries including Indonesia.
- SNAL, through its director and legal representative, Dr. Shadatu Chhatrapathy, founded and registered a Social Forestry Company called “YOSI” to implement projects in Indonesia.

**YOSI’s Role**

- YOSI will provide technical assistance to help out smallholder or community-based groups in the form of training, capacity building, and financial support.
- YOSI will also help out smallholders and communities to improve efficiency, yield, and market access while reducing negative social and environmental impacts. YOSI will work with smallholder farmers and other stakeholders such as larger companies in the form of various pledges around No Deforestation, No Peat, and No Exploitation (NPDE).
- YOSI will also provide technical assistance to help out smallholder groups in improving their production capacity within UKM Indonesia over the previous decade.

**The GCF Project**

- The project will be implemented through two sub-grantees: BPD/UN and SNAL (EE). Funds would subsequently be disbursed to YOSI from SNAL on the basis of a sub-grant.
- The implementation of the GCF project will be carried out in accordance with the terms and conditions set forth by the GCF, including the fiduciary standards that would be provided by SNAL.
- SNAL as an EE will be responsible for the implementation of activity 2.1.2 “Implementing and scaling the adoption of proven approaches for reducing emissions and enhancing the sustainability and climate resilience of smallholder agricultural systems” in collaboration with YOSI, including engaging key stakeholders dialogue platform and pilot for low-emission and climate-resilient agriculture and private sector investment; and the development of a market mechanism to support the transition from conventional to sustainable palm oil market inclusion.
- Under this activity, SNAL as an EE will ensure that all GCF and GCF fiduciary standards are followed, complying with the stipulations of the Grant Agreement. Always within the legal framework of the project (AMA, FAD).
- In addition, SNAL via YOSI will foster and coordinate with other partner projects to guarantee the integration of climate change into their activities.
- SNAL as an EE will be responsible for GCF proceeds of EUR 6,803.159.

**Executing Entity – Indonesia Ministry of Environment and Forestry (Menlhut) (formerly the Ministry of Forestry)**

- Menlhut is responsible for sustainable land management and environmental conservation in Indonesia.
- MoF Director General has high responsibility to implement Sustainable Forest Management (Pengelolaan Hutan Berkelanjutan, PHB).
- The MoF is also responsible for implementing the National Action Plan on Climate Change (Rencana Aksi Nasional Pemanasan Global, RANPG) and the National Water Management and Forest Rehabilitation (Pengelolaan Dasar Air Lahan dan Rehabilitasi Hutan, PDALRH).
- In 2015, MoF was appointed by the President as the national focal point to coordinate climate change efforts. Including the implementation of the National Action Plan on Climate Change (RANPG) and the National Council on Climate Change (Dewan Nasional Perubahan Iklim, DNPI), and the REDD+ Agency were

### B.4.3 Other Project Stakeholders

9. ### B.4.4 Project Governance and Management

9. The highest level governance will be as shown in Figure B. The highest-level governance will be overseen by a Project Steering Committee (PSC), which will serve as the principal governing body for the project. Decisions of the PSC will be made on the basis of recommendations from the Project Technical Coordination Group (PTCG) and the Indonesian national ministries and departments as well as BPDH, BMKG and GIZ. The PSC will be responsible for overall oversight and coordinating partner cooperation. The specific functions of the PSC will be to:

- Provide overall guidance for project implementation.
- Provide feedback and validation of annual work plans in consideration of annual reports and project evaluations.
- Ensure alignment with national policies and strategies.
- Stay informed of project advancement with EAS/Safeguards, Gender Action Plan, and IIP processes.

10. In addition to the PSC, a Project Technical Coordination Group (PTCG) will be established as the principal technical advisory body to the project. The PTCG will meet once a year and will consist of representatives of the Indonesian national ministries and departments as well as BPDH, BMKG and GIZ.

11. In addition to the PSC, a Project Management Unit (PMU) will be established as the principal technical advisory body to the project. The PMU will meet once a year and will consist of representatives of the Indonesian national ministries and departments as well as BPDH, BMKG and GIZ.

Further funds will be provided directly from the national government institutions incl. FMAs (West Kalimantan). Further funds will be implemented directly by GIZ Indonesia or transferred to SNAL (within respective role as AE). The Government of Indonesia through BMKG will provide co-financing.

## B.4 Regulation, taxation and insurance

### Privileges, immunities and taxation
102. The Federal Republic of Germany has concluded a Framework Agreement on Technical Cooperation under Article 35 of the Treaty of Friendship, Cooperation and Partnership with Indonesia dated April 9th, 2008, which provides for certain privileges and immunities to be applied in projects and programmes of technical cooperation, including exemptions from certain taxes. The same privileges and immunities also apply to the Project, including GCF proceeds. However, formal agreements will only enter into effect after the project is approved.

### Approvals, permits, licences and land
103. As per the Funding Proposal, GIZ is not aware of the project requiring specific approvals, permits, licences or land to allow for the implementation of planned activities.

### Currency
104. The local currency in Indonesia is the Indonesian Rupiah (code: IDR). The project's local transactions will use RUPAH. The AE will manage GCF processes in Euro. GIZ will apply its standard rates** for the conversion of currencies.

### Insurance
As the AE, we will ensure that project activities are adequately insured as per GIZ standard operating procedures and common practices. GIZ standard operating procedures require contractors to ensure required insurance coverage is provided prior to commencement of work. Upon submitting the Funding Proposal, GIZ does not anticipate additional insurance to cover special risks in this project.

### B.5. Justification for GCF funding request (max. 1000 words, approximately 2 pages)

#### 105. Why GCF Funds are required - consideration of alternative funding options and justification of grant finance

The Green Climate Fund (GCF) is implementing this project, which will be instrumental in achieving Indonesia's NDC targets and the national FOLU Net zero 2030 strategy. The enhanced NDC (2022) has increased the national target on emissions reduction from 29% to 41%, with a focus on reducing deforestation and forest degradation, increasing forest cover targets, forestry land use contribution that is 17.4% to 25.4%, by enhancing land rehabilitation (afforestation, reforestation, and sustainable management of forests), and improving the quality of existing forests. Indonesia has a large area of remaining natural forest cover. Over 3.8 million ha of forests were lost from 2001-2022, making WRI the second largest country in Southeast Asia for deforestation after Brazil. The government is committed to restore forests and there is an urgency to reduce deforestation and strengthen the resilience of forest and agricultural ecosystems through sustainable land management practices.

In the intense fire season of 2019, the entire population of WK suffered from heavy haze pollution and 10% of the total population was affected by respiratory diseases. The fires were caused by human activities such as slash-and-burn agriculture, deforestation and degradation of forest functions and have generated negative impacts on the local economy. There are also increasing concerns about the impact of climate change in Indonesia, with a projected increase in higher toll climate change will have an ecosystem, infrastructure and people's livelihoods, health and overall well-being. The government has set out its NDC targets to reduce greenhouse gas emissions by 29% below business as usual (BAU) levels by 2030, which is equivalent to 416 million tons of CO2e. This will require a total investment of USD 58 billion in the forestry sector alone and USD 75 billion for all sectors combined.

The government has set out its NDC targets to reduce greenhouse gas emissions by 29% below business as usual (BAU) levels by 2030, which is equivalent to 416 million tons of CO2e. This will require a total investment of USD 58 billion in the forestry sector alone and USD 75 billion for all sectors combined.

#### B.6. Finance

Overall, GCF has insufficient resources, funding, and technical capacities to implement its jurisdictional strategic interventions and green development objectives. In 2022 the annual budget for the Ministry of Environment and Forestry (MoEF) was approximately USD 13 billion (IDR 457 trillion), with a total budget amount to IDR 42.97 trillion (USD 2.67 million), which provides annual funding for FMUs, service units responsible for forest management, conservation, and sustainable development of forests, including the restoration of ecosystem management, forestry museum, and nurseries. Considering WRI covers over 5.5 million ha of natural forests, this is equivalent to roughly USD 0.6 per ha. While budgets over the last two years have been stable at approximately USD 13 billion (IDR 457 trillion), there has been a significant increase in the number of forest management units and service units responsible for forest management, conservation, and sustainable development of forests.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 30

and reported increase from previous years. It still remains for below needed levels to establish the necessary enabling conditions and implement urgently needed cross-cutting AFOLU measures. Overall, the fiscal transfer by the Government of Indonesia (GoI) to WFP is insufficient to cover the costs of implementing REDD+ activities. Since there are insufficient funds from the public sector:

- The GoI has committed to provide additional funding and requires further investments to establish credible investment frameworks.
- Since the 2012-commitment for REDD+, there was no dedicated funding at the national level for WFP to implement its work plan.

The current budget of USD 3.5 million is insufficient to fund the full program. The Government of Indonesia is nonetheless committed to the Project, provided that additional resources are made available.

### Results-based payments: Even with results-based payments of $103.8 million for REDD+ in Indonesia (including $27.4 million from the World Bank's Forest Carbon Partnership Facility (FCPF) and $59.6 million from Desk SK 4736/MENHKUK/Per/3-6/2023 about Investment Plan Results Based Payment), as funds will be released in phases, it is expected that the total amount of results-based payments for REDD+ in Indonesia will be USD 158.9 million by 2024.

### WFP's role: The Government of Indonesia has committed to provide additional funding and requires further investments to establish credible investment frameworks.
- **REDD+ initiatives**: Overall, RFP funds earmarked for WFP are insufficient to implement the necessary actions to overcome barriers and enable scaling up of REDD+ activities in line with the national development pathway, as described in Section B.2. Without investments to overcome the identified barriers, WFP will likely not be able to achieve its results-based payments targets.
- **Future funding**: The Government of Indonesia has committed to provide additional funding for WFP to implement investments that reduce emissions from deforestation and forest degradation (REDD+) activities in line with the national development pathway. However, funds are needed to support WFP to implement investments that reduce emissions from deforestation and forest degradation (REDD+) activities in line with the national development pathway.

### Other development partners: Several other development partners are active in other parts of Indonesia, including Central and East Kalimantan. However, as indicated in B.1, WFP has attracted comparatively little donor funding for its REDD+ work compared to other agencies. At the same time, previous activities focusing on REDD+ have had a strong focus on mitigation, and there is an urgent need to shift towards adaptation.

### Concessional loans: Conventional concessional investments provide room for REDD+ or adaptation activities that are not yet financially viable but will be in future. The GCF has committed to provide USD 10 million to financial institutions, and 35-25% for private financial institutions; with a short grace period. Much of the project activities are however co-financed by other development partners. Concessional loans are provided through government agencies focusing on setting up necessary enabling conditions to facilitate future investments for replication and scaling.

### Forest management: The GCF has committed to provide USD 10 million to support forest management, including forest management businesses. It is regulated under Permenhut K/59/Menhut/Sipen/2015. The facility aims to distribute the funds among three main categories of activities: (i) strengthening forest governance and forest management; (ii) supporting community-based forest management; and (iii) supporting private sector forest management, industrial plantation forests, or community plantation forests.

### Concessional loans: For example, as is the case for a plantation permit (HGU), see Feasibility Study for further information on different forestry permits. The GCF does not provide concessional loans to support the establishment of new plantations but does not include readiness financing and one of the main objectives of the GCF project is to create enabling conditions for private sector investment in forest management.

### Project benefits: The project will have a significant impact on climate change mitigation, adaptation options such as creating business associations in the villages, building business cases and supporting access to Social Forest Management (SFM) loans. It also aims at increasing the number of households participating in SFM loan scheme (Crab and honey projects in Kubu Raya and a fish project in Kapuas Hulu). The economic and financial benefits are expected to be significant.

### Project beneficiaries: The main direct beneficiaries of the project are approx. 680,000 persons (50% women; comprising approx. 320,000 households) who will benefit from improved livelihoods through increased access to forest resources and services. Of these, 50% working in climate resilient agriculture, approx. 120,000 smallholder households benefiting from climate-resilient agricultural practices, approx. 480,000 people living in vulnerable communities (including HHV/HCVS areas). HHV has the most of the lowest HBA in Indonesia (ranking 34 out of 38 provinces), and target villages are located within these HHV/HCVS areas.

## B

would provide new opportunities at risk of missed-keeps, given the lack of an enabling environment for cross-cutting investments in the AFOLU sector as well as the high interest rates, short grace periods and longer-term returns associated with such investments. The analysis shows that the financial viability of different funding options (e.g., for palm rubber, or unmanaged land) is highly sensitive to the assumptions made about the future price of commodities.

The analysis shows the financial viability of different funding options (incl. grant-funding, loan funding and without subsidy) in the case of a smallholder palm oil producer, where there are no established business models, the project will be financially unattractive, the high upfront costs, break-even points of over 11 years and the risk aversion of smallholder farmers requiring substantial subsidies. The analysis also shows that the financial viability is highly sensitive to assumptions about the future price of commodities (e.g., for palm rubber or unmanaged land) in the case of a smallholder palm oil producer, where there is a more established business model: the project will be financially attractive, with break-even points of less than 5 years and no risk aversion. The analysis also shows that the financial viability of different funding options (incl. grant-funding, loan funding and without subsidy) in the case of an HEPF, where there is a more established business model: the project will be financially attractive, with break-even points of less than 5 years and no risk aversion.

### Proposal for financial instruments and concessionality

Based on this analysis, grant finance from the GCF is the only viable option. Specifically, grants will provide the necessary flexibility to support a wide range of projects in the AFOLU sector, including those that are not yet financially viable or where there is no established business model. The GCF can also provide grants for pilot projects and innovative approaches at scale, which may be more cost-effective than full-scale implementation.

(BPDLH) These investments will be accompanied with tailored technical assistance and support to interested beneficiaries such as universities, government institutions etc., through inclusive and participatory processes, to the extent possible.

Ensuring that these projects are included in the GCF portfolio will provide a strong signal for the need to adopt an integrated approach to climate change mitigation and adaptation in the remaining vast forested landscape of WK. ### A. Capacity Building

The project will build on the existing framework, capacity building and technical support, this project empowers stakeholders at province level to engage in forest carbon trading and conservation activities. The project will also provide a platform for developing new methodologies for carbon market credit and in line with the national regulations. This will help to mobilize additional private sector funding and create opportunities for small-scale enterprises.

The support for the development of new methodologies for issuing carbon credits will enable forest farmer groups (FFGs) to participate in the carbon market, which is currently dominated by large landowners. The FFGs will be able to afforestation, reforestation, and agroforestry after the end of this project. This will be achieved through a contractual arrangement with the private sector. The project will also provide capacity building for forest farmers who will participate in the national carbon market more inclusive and contribute to a fair distribution of benefits between small-scale enterprises and large landowners.

The Indonesian government has shown high political willingness to reduce emissions from deforestation and forest degradation (REDD+). In addition, the existing framework (carbon registry, carbon exchange etc.) has already been established and trading of carbon credits has already started. The project will also provide capacity building for forest farmers who will participate in a managed market, because there is high political will, an advanced legal framework, and considerable opportunities to mobilize private sector funding.

The project will be implemented through the existing national REDD+ mechanism (see information paper 90: Chapter 3.4. Domestic Carbon Market).

### Technical sustainability

The technical assistance provided through the project will be designed to trigger behavioral change among key actors at different levels (e.g., households, communities, local governments) in order to promote responsive and cross-cutting climate solutions in the ACPU region. EUR 15.2 million of investments (39% of GCF proceeds for comp. 1-3) will be directed towards technical assistance activities that will support the project’s implementation (13% of GCF proceeds for comp. 1-3) to relevant partner institutions such as FNUA. Implemented measures will generate a range of co-benefits, including social and economic benefits (e.g., improved livelihoods), climate change mitigation (e.g., reducing forest fires) and environmental benefits (e.g., soil protection from agroforestry; see Section D.3). A grant of EUR 15 million (39% of GCF proceeds for comp. 1-3) will be directed towards the implementation of land tenure and cadaster licenses that can improve long-term land security and can be used to demarcate land ownership), and ensure access to land for smallholder farmers, pastoralists, and other vulnerable groups.

The project has been designed through a participatory and inclusive process, and its Stakeholder Engagement Plan (Annex 7A) includes the identification of key stakeholders. The project will also be implemented in such a way as to promote a high level of stakeholder engagement and inclusion throughout project implementation, ensuring that all relevant actors are involved at every stage of the project cycle.

C.2.3 Does your project funding finance technology development/transfer? Yes [ ] No [ ]

The project will implement a capacity development approach that builds on the successful approaches applied by GIZ projects in the region and its trusted collaboration with governmental institutions involved in Technology Development Transfer (TDT). The project will focus on three main areas of intervention to support human-resource capacity development both at the national and the sub-national level in order to improve the quality, quantity, and sustainability of agricultural production systems. These interventions are contributing to the conservation of forests. A key feature of these interventions will be digitalization including e-learning infrastructure for forestry sector at national level, project focus on strengthening the e-learning infrastructure in the forestry sector at national level, this project will focus these efforts on capacity development for the design and implementation of capacity development for the design or tailor-made interventions that will create lasting positive impacts.

The project will also support the development of climate-smart agricultural practices such as agroforestry farming to ensure sustainable food production systems and climate resilient agricultural practices. In this context, the project will focus on the development of a market information system (MIS) for agricultural commodity markets and their derivatives and facilitates timely access to market information. Agricultural research and extension services, and technology development will be enhanced as part of the climate-smart and sustainable farming.

## D.1. IMPACT POTENTIAL (MAX. 500 WORDS, APPROXIMATELY 1 PAGE)

### D.1.1. Project Description

105. The estimation applies WRI’s 2nd Forest Reference Level (FRL). It is aligned with the national 2nd FRL of Indonesia in terms of the methodology, including the activity data and emission factors. It updates the total annual emissions from deforestation and forest degradation (9.8 million tCO2e/year) to include the additionality of forest restoration (0.8 million tCO2e), and enhancement of forest carbon stock (0.6 million tCO2e per year). This results in a net emission reduction of 13.4 million tCO2e/year, which is equivalent to 57% of the total annual emissions from deforestation and forest degradation for the whole project duration until 2030. Further, it shows that GCF impact to a longer project lifespan, expects total of 63.5 million tCO2e reduction until 2040.
