### D.1.3. Impact Potential - Adaptation

The project aims to increase the resilience of 65,000 villages (50% women) who reside in target villages by strengthening their awareness of climate change risk and risk reduction practices. In addition, these villages will be involved in sustainable forest management activities under Component 2, which includes direct based adaptation including the re-establishment of peat and mangrove areas, and climate-resilient low-emission agriculture (LFA) under Component 3. The project also aims to increase the number of households that will be involved in sustainable agricultural practices under Component 2 and the rest in sustainable forest management activities under Component 3. These activities include the establishment of HCVCSs and other agroforestry systems, including the establishment of HCVICSs in addition. In addition, under Component 3 ecosystem functions will be secured through the restoration of degraded peatland areas (APL) and mangrove areas (HCVICS). The HCVIFS (High Conservation Value / High Carbon Stocks) area of 10,000 ha has no forest land (APL), which will be protected.

### D.1.4. Direct and Indirect Benefits

- **Direct beneficiaries** will benefit through:
  - Direct technical and financial support to implement suitable climate-resilient and low-emission AFU/CFU measures prioritizing the protection of biodiversity and resilience of vulnerable communities and ecosystems.
  - Development of alternative livelihood sources based on low-emission and climate-resilient sustainable land- and forest-based activities, including agroforestry systems and other ecosystem services.
  - Establishment of an enabling environment that incentivizes sustainable planning and investments, and increases transparency in monitoring/REDD+, and climate change adaptation and mitigation in WCF. In addition, the project will support the development of a framework for the implementation of suitable low-emission and climate-resilient land- and forest-based investments.
  - Engagement with local communities, including indigenous peoples, who are particularly vulnerable to climate change, to actively participate in and benefit from sustainable and resilient land- and forest-based practices.
  - Additional targeted support to enable conflict resolution, strengthen tenure security, safeguard food, health, and income security.

## D.2.1 Project Description

### D.2.1.1 Background and project scope

129. Individuals and projects will benefit 4.8 million people (approx. 50% women) excluding direct beneficiaries living in WK as a result of the adaptation co-benefits including reduced impacts of forests fires, clean air, flood protection, improved water quality, and increased food security.

130. The project will facilitate a paradigm shift in the forestry and land use sector in WK that will be sustained by national policies and institutions with strengthened capacities for mobilizing and channeling climate finance. A recent World Bank report, once completed, will provide guidance on how to address these challenges. This GCF project covers a broad range of topics that have been identified as social and environmental challenges. This GCF project covers a broad range of topics that have been identified as social and environmental challenges.

131. The project will support the development of a national forest policy, land use planning, and land-use zoning; setting land tenure; financing strategies: monitoring, evaluation, and learning; and cross-sectoral coordination.

132. The project will also support the development of a national forest policy that includes provisions for sustainable supply chains. It should align government-led, multi-stakeholder processes to external and market incentives in order to ensure that the forestry sector is not only environmentally sustainable but economically viable as well. This concept has been piloted in several provinces in India where such approaches have already been piloted and the concept has emerged as a best practice.

132. This project will further strengthen the sub-national regulatory frameworks and policies for jurisdictional land use planning, including forest zoning, and the development of sustainable supply chains. The proposed regulation will provide the legal framework for all Regencies. The five most important Regencies in term of both existing and potential forest cover are expected to be covered by this project. These Regencies have a high potential for sustainable agriculture and forest value chains. This project is expected to drive investment from private sector and business communities, including small- and medium-sized enterprises (SMEs), into the forestry sector. The project will expand the proposal's impact beyond the boundaries of the intervention area. By promoting collective learning and capacity building in the five Regencies, this project will provide a replicable model for other provinces in Indonesia.

134. The demand for a paradigm shift to the WK province socio-economic development pathway is linked with the provision of forest-based livelihoods and ecosystem services. This project will support the development of a framework that consists of developing sustainable business and livelihood models while creating an effective institutional framework for the conservation, management, and use of forests.

134. This needs to take into account that WK still is in the transition to sustainable development pathways, and private sector investment has not yet been fully mobilized. The project will support the development of a national forest policy, land-use zoning, and land tenure. Government, Private sector commitments to conservation and sustainable supply chains are still insufficient in planning and implementation activities. This project will provide a replicable model for other provinces in Indonesia.

135. To overcome path dependency, the project will:

- Address the main drivers of deforestation and forest degradation (activities 1.2 and 1.2.1).
- Build local capacities on best practices for planning, implementation, monitoring, and stakeholder engagement (including gender equality and social inclusion; cross-cutting in all activities).
- Prioritize the development of a national forest policy, land-use zoning, and land tenure critical under land use planning processes in activity 1.1.1, and the implementation of sustainable land- and forest-based investments.
- Develop financial mechanisms and mobilize public and private funds for sustainable land-based investments (activities 2.3 and 2.4).
- Implement a grant mechanism for IP and mobilization of private and public investment for IP; productive commercial activities that are coupled with positive social and environmental impacts, i.e., large carbon benefit.

## D2.2 Potential for scaling up and replication

137. The proposed project is a role model on sub-national jurisdictional approaches to shape new development patterns in Indonesia, which are currently dominated by large-scale land conversion for agricultural or industrial development that is high in Indonesia's overall provinces. The success of jurisdictional models developed in West Kalimantan (Wak) and Riau (Riau) has been recognized as a model for replication across other provinces in the REDD+ portfolio to the other 10 priority REDD+ provinces in Indonesia. Therefore, the concept of matching co-funding between government and private sector actors is being considered as an important element of this project. The financial investments can be replicated in the other 10 REDD+ prioritized provinces—in particular strengthening the FMUs in a complementary manner.

- **GIZ aims to give social forestery license holders an incentive to increase carbon stocks in their areas through a contribution-based system.** This is based on the successful experience of the P3 model in Kapuas Hulu, with a methodology, which will be submitted to the government for official recognition. If successful, so-called contribution claims may be sold to private sector actors, without any transfer of carbon titles.

D.4.1 Climate risk and vulnerability (max. 50 words, approximately 1 page)
D.4.1 Climate risk and vulnerability
In 2019, Indonesia ranked 14th out of 180 countries on German Watch's Climate Risk Index, where Indonesia ranks highest in the world for climate risks related to extreme weather events (German Watch, 2021; Klok et al., 2021). As described in Section B.1, Indonesia is exposed to diverse climate-related hazards, particularly variability and extremes of rainfall, temperature, wind speed, and sea level rise. These hazards have significant impacts on particularly vulnerable households and communities, including women, indigenous peoples, disabled persons, children, the elderly, and people with limited access to resources (Klok et al., 2021). The project will provide detailed analysis on climate risks and vulnerabilities in the project region. The project will support them to strengthen their resilience by understanding the essence of the ecosystems upon which they depend and adaptation impacts under D.1.2.

D.4.1 Sustainable social needs and gaps
158. Sustainable forest management in WNK faces different capacity constraints at various levels. Among governmental agencies such as the provincial forestry service (Dinas Kehutanan), PFMAs (Forest Management Units) and local governments, there is a lack of technical capacity to manage forests sustainably. The capacities under their mandate are still limited. These limitations range from forest planning and technical capacity in GIS and remote sensing to human resource capacity for sustainable forest management. In addition, the capacity for sustainable forest management at local level, also need capacity building, especially to implement the national social forestry policy (Kebun Rakyat Nasional - KRN) and the community-based forest management model (Hutan Lindung Desa - HLD). The project will support this by providing training on social forestry business models (Kelompok Usaha Pembiayaan Hutan Lindung - KUPHL).

159. In addition to the agricultural sector, there are various other sectors that contribute significantly towards producing the country's economic wealth; these include the production of WNK in the first quarter of 2023 was dominated by agriculture, forestry, industrial sector and mining (BPS North Sumatra). This shows the importance of palm oil and its contribution to economic growth in WK. However, sustainable practices in WNK are still not maintained, which

## D.4.2 Institutional Needs

160. **WPS** in developing province, and natural resources exploitation has been a primary development pathway to grow regional income and economic growth. However, still 40% of the villages in WPS are categorized as developing or very poor. The share of the palm oil sector in total employment is only 2%, while its contribution to total regional income is only 2.2% and plantations are 4.5%. Only 2% of the WPS government budget is allocated for the development of the palm oil sector, which is not enough to support sustainable forest management.

160a. Meanwhile, the local revenue was only USD 164 million with a poverty rate of 8.73%, GDP per capita in 2020 was USD 590 (compared to USD 1,000 for Indonesia), and the average income per household was USD 1,200 (compared to USD 2,640). The local revenue was only USD 78.269 million (USD 3,956), paying WPS 23% of its revenues. **Individual expenditure per capita in WPS** was only USD 210 (compared to USD 400 for Indonesia).

161. Therefore, the government should consider increasing the share of palm oil revenue that is allocated to WPS through: (i) co-designing and co-implementing field level interventions with the private sector to improve their sustainability performance; (ii) providing technical assistance to the private sector in developing sustainable forest management activities that strengthen their livelihoods.

162. Specifically, the government should consider supporting the following initiatives: (a) increasing their confidence in sustainable business models and investing in low-carbon and climate-resilient palm oil plantations; (b) promoting the adoption of best practices for sustainable land use and reducing resilient AFOLU emissions; and (c) designing climate-resilient, low-emission and sustainable plans for the private sector to invest in forest conservation activities. The government should also consider providing technical assistance committed to funding conservation activities in the field within and outside their concessions. Without OCF funding, WPS will not be able to provide the necessary support to the private sector.

163. In addition, GCF funding will also be used to engage with (local) financial institutions to explore the possibility of using blended finance mechanisms (such as co-financing, co-funding, co-lending, co-insurance, and guarantees). It has high potential for scalability and replication through the project interventions.

### D.4.4 Institutional Needs

160b. At the sub-national level, FMUs and the provincial forestry service often face budgetary and organizational constraints, which limit the resources they can allocate for training. They also often do not have a capacity building plan that is aligned with their strategic plans or the national forest policy. Training programs prepared often cannot be implemented optimally due to limited human resources and budgets. Hence, current capacity building efforts are often fragmented and ad hoc in nature.

160c. There is a lack of clarity regarding the role and duties of FMUs after Job Creation Law No. 11 (2020) on Job Creation and Employment Promotion. The law requires FMUs to provide training for their employees, including UPTs, and other stakeholders. Besides, the policy framework regarding capacity building needs adjustments to reflect the new roles and responsibilities of FMUs under the Job Creation Law. A new capacity building plan need to be developed or updated to reflect the need to build the needed capacity for GHG mitigation in the forestry sector.

160d. Also, the lack of localization of the Regulation of the Minister of Agriculture No. 39 of 2020 about the implementation of the National Forest Policy (RPM) and the lack of a clear roadmap for the development of palm oil growers by 2050 is one of the reasons the adoption of RSPO certification in WPS still low. Furthermore, organizational capacity building efforts are not aligned with the national forest policy and the private sector's initiatives in achieving a sustainable palm oil industry.

160e. The government should consider supporting the following initiatives: (a) developing a roadmap for the implementation of RPM 2030-2050; (b) providing technical assistance to FMUs to develop their capacity building plans that are aligned with their strategic plans and national forest policy; and (c) providing technical assistance to the private sector in developing sustainable forest management activities.

161. Forestry and agricultural education and training programs developed and supported by the project apply the Competence-Based Training (CBT) approach in accordance with the Indonesian National Qualifications Framework (Kerangka Akreditasi Nasional Indonesia - KAN). # D.2 Government of Indonesia: National Climate Strategy and commitments

169. The GoWK is member of the Government’s Climate & Forests (GCF) Task Force. The GCF Task Force offers a unique vehicle for advancing jurisdiction-wide approaches to protecting forests, reducing emissions, and enhancing resilience in Indonesia. The GoWK has also been a key player in the development of the National REDD+ Strategy, including as a member of the Steering Committee on Sustainable Agriculture in Indonesia (SCASI), a multi-stakeholder partnership that seeks to advance sustainable agricultural practices in Indonesia. SCASI is currently working with the Ministry of Forestry and the recognition of smallholder rights by formalizing recognizing production with Plantation Business Registration (PBR) and other legal instruments.

170. The GoWK has also been a key player in the development of the National REDD+ Strategy, including as a member of the Steering Committee on Sustainable Agriculture in Indonesia (SCASI), a multi-stakeholder partnership that seeks to advance sustainable agricultural practices in Indonesia. SCASI is currently working with the Ministry of Forestry and the recognition of smallholder rights by formalizing recognizing production with Plantation Business Registration (PBR) and other legal instruments.

171. This project follows the Indonesian Situation System (SIS REDD+), and develops a Stakeholder Engagement Strategy (SES REDD+) to ensure that all stakeholders are engaged in the design, implementation, reporting, monitoring, and evaluation. The strategy shall be inclusive to ensure all actors’ voices are heard and their concerns addressed.

172. The GoWK has been working with the Ministry of Forestry since 2009 aims as a transition Indonesia into a middle class income country with a fair and sustainable development. In the AFOLU sector, the RPJMN includes: (i) establishment of 3 million hectares of forest cover; (ii) enhancement of 15 million hectares of forest cover; (iii) participation of business sector in forest rehabilitation; (iv) rehabilitation of 637,000 hectares of mangrove; (v) conservation and restoration of peatland; (vi) establishment of 2.4 million hectares of protected areas; (vii) research and education; and (viii) establishment of 1.02 million hectares of high-conservation value forest. As per the RPJMN, the GoWK has been working with the Ministry of Forestry since 2009 aims as a transition Indonesia into a middle class income country with a fair and sustainable development.

173. The government of Indonesia considers climate mitigation and adaptation efforts as an integrated concept that is a part of its national development strategy. In this context, the GoWK has been working on developing and implementing a National Action Plan on Climate Change Adaptation (NAP-AR) or NAP which provides guidance for the implementation of the NDCs in the forestry sector.

174. The NDC highlights forest land use and land use change as key areas to increase ecosystem and landscape resilience. Areas identified include: (i) 20 million hectares of degraded lands; (ii) 35 million hectares of degraded peatlands; (iii) 6 million hectares of degraded watersheds; and (iv) 17 million hectares of degraded coastal ecosystems.

175. The GoWK has been working with the Ministry of Forestry since 2009 aims as a transition Indonesia to be identified to implement integrated approaches to land and forest management. With support of development partners such as Solidaridad or KfW, the GoWK has been working on developing and implementing integrated approaches.

| Between 1997 and 2015, Indonesia’s forest area was lost to BHK by 2.1 million ha/year, from 7.5 million ha/year in 1986-1996. At the time of FP formation (2003-2004), deforestation was still a major threat to the environment due to its role as a source of greenhouse gas emissions and biodiversity loss. The five target regions are vulnerable to the impacts of climate change, including increased frequency and intensity of droughts, floods, and cyclones. | Medium | The formulation of the FP will be supported by the project which will involve a more wide approach in the management of natural resources. The project will also support the development of adaptation and mitigation (e.g., within development plans, and through developing institutional arrangements for climate change adaptation) and social forestry to sustainably manage and use forest resources while promoting sustainable production of soft commodities while ensuring environmental sustainability. | The formulation of the FP will be supported by the project which will involve a more wide approach in the management of natural resources. The project will also support the development of adaptation and mitigation (e.g., within development plans, and through developing institutional arrangements for climate change adaptation) and social forestry to sustainably manage and use forest resources while promoting sustainable production of soft commodities while ensuring environmental sustainability. |

This project builds on best practices and lessons learned from projects and programs in the ACP1 sector over the last decade, both within Indonesia, West Kalimantan, and the broader region. Appendix 1 at the of the document provides a summary of these lessons learned.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 PAGE 49

## Resilience

At the time of this FP formulation (2022-2024) good practices and lessons learned from the AFOLU sector are available in the province. However, there is a need to scale up these initiatives, pilot activities, and promote them as replicable models at provincial level.

By working closely with government agencies and other stakeholders, we will ensure that agriculture and social forestry will set new standards for sustainable land management practices across all regions. We will ensure that improved policies, skills, and profitability of agroforestry systems are replicated in other regencies within the whole province. As soon as possible, the project will be scaled up to other regencies.

The provincial government of BMR will serve as a role model for sustainable land management and sustainable land practices. Additional technical and financial support is required to scale-up these activities across all provinces of Indonesia.

## Sustainability

The provincial government of BMR has a high ambition to implement the Green Climate Fund project to transform the AFOLU sector and contribute to national climate goals. The relevant agencies for the AFOLU sector are committed to scaling up their efforts in promoting the Climate Change and sustainable land management (CCSLM) agenda.

A paradigm shift would entail improved capacity of local governments, communities, and farmers to regulate and engage the private sector and local enterprises in the development of agroforestry commodity production. Smallholders have the potential to increase their income by accessing premium markets and accessing financing for climate-smart investments such as climate change mitigation and adaptation at the land-level.

The project will be implemented through a multi-stakeholder approach, with emphasis on sustainable business cases are внедрены на местном уровне. Для этого необходимо улучшить инфраструктуру для финансирования и доступ к рынкам сбыта для продовольственных товаров. Финансовые механизмы разрабатываются в сотрудничестве с частным сектором, и международные доноры (как результат проекта) будут участвовать в их реализации.

## E3. GCF Outcome Level: Reduced emissions and increased resilience (RMF core indicators 1-4, quantitative Indicators)

This includes, especially the integration of climate change, water and climate resilient AGRO and RESID eco-industry pilot projects and regional policy planning including provincial development plans, provincial and regional level adaptation action plan, as well as sectoral plans for aligning provincial RESILID with national policies. For this last item, the project will ensure that the provincial government of BMR is fully aligned with the GCF through the project.

The project will also contribute to the implementation of forest and land-based programs in line with the National REDD+ Strategy and the National Forest Policy. The project will also strengthen the regulatory framework. In close alignment with government visions and strategies, the project will ensure that measures provided by the project (including measures to strengthen GESI) are fully integrated into provincial development plans.

### E.7.1 Monitoring, reporting and evaluation arrangements

190. Monitoring, reporting and evaluations arrangements (in addition to the EMP, IPP and GAP) will comply with the relevant GCF policies, as stipulated in the AIA, including the GCF’s policy on monitoring, reporting and evaluation framework and its partners, which EEI will extend to sub-grantees. The project will apply a customized results-based monitoring and evaluation (M&E) system. The M&E system will track project impacts, inputs, outputs, activities, outputs, impacts, as well as associated financial flows across all outputs in all project provinces and regencies. This system will be based on:

- GCF’s Monitoring and Evaluation Framework (Annex 1)
- Implementation framework and logical framework
- Implementation timeline (Annex 5)
- Reporting requirements

Local and national monitoring systems

Project partners, including local governments, communities and stakeholders in WK and at national level

The oversight for M&E and reporting lies with the GIZ GCF EE. On the other hand, GIZ EE will ensure that appropriate monitoring and evaluation are performed to ensure that project outputs and impacts are achieved as planned. The information collected from the government agencies and through the dedicated GCF grant mechanism, as input to the project M&E. The information collected at the local level will be integrated into the monitoring system of the GIZ GCF EE. The GIZ GCF EE will also ensure that the project M&E is aligned with the GCF’s requirements for reporting entities. The project management committee (PMC) will be the body responsible for leading the preparation, monitoring and coordination of the Annual Operating Plan, including the project budget project, ensuring and coordinating the functioning of the project's monitoring and evaluation system, and discussing monitoring processes and results.

191. In case of successful project completion, GIZ’s GCF EE will make available one expert to oversee and coordinate the project M&E and reporting routines. As soon as GCF project process become available in WK, the project will recruit M&E specialist. The specialist will work closely together and support the


---
**Page 65**
---

management of the MAE system and provide on-the-job training for dedicated MAE experts from local partners. In addition, the experts will maintain the Cloud Database. Additional specialist needs may be required for certain technical areas (e.g., agriculture or forestry).

### E.7.3 Independent monitoring and evaluation studies

105. GIZ will conduct an independent mid-term evaluation and a final evaluation of the project. GIZ evaluators are carried out by GIZ’s independent evaluation unit. This unit is steered by a corporate unit that is separate from operational business and reports directly to the Management Board. Members of this unit are selected on a rolling basis, based on their experience in conducting evaluations according to the OECD DAC Evaluation Recommendation (2006; revised 2020) on a standard basis in project evaluations. These are based on the six evaluation criteria agreed by the Development Assistance Committee (DAC) of the Organisation for Economic Co-operation and Development (OECD) for evaluating development cooperation projects: relevance, effectiveness, efficiency, impact, coherence and sustainability. Additionally to the six OECD DAC evaluation criteria, the following five GCF criteria will be evaluated: Gender equality, climate change adaptation, environmental sustainability, social inclusion, and poverty reduction.

106. GCF’s Evaluation Principles of impartiality, objective and unbiased and relevance, use and participation: All provisions for upholding the GCF evaluation principles are applicable to this project. The independent evaluation unit is responsible for ensuring that all evaluations conducted in line with OECD DAC recommendations, evaluation team members selected by GIZ must not be directly responsible for the policy setting, design or management of an evaluation project. An internal review will be carried out at the end of each evaluation and a first Independent Evaluation Report will be submitted to the GCF as per timeline agreed in the funding activity agreement (FAA).

107. GCF’s Evaluation Methodology: The independent evaluation unit will use quantitative and qualitative methods of empirical social research in evaluations. The mix of methods is adapted to the object of the evaluation and the evaluation questions. To ensure that the evaluation methodology is appropriate, it must be agreed with the GCF before implementation. The evaluation design should be such as to be a suitable scheme. The aim of contribution analysis is to collect empirical evidence on whether and to what extent contributions to observed changes can be associated with the project.

108. Projects will be rated based on the six OECD DAC and on the GCF evaluation criteria. Each of the OECD DAC or GCF criteria is rated on a scale of 1 to 10, where 1 indicates that there are no signs of achievement and 10 indicates that all aspects have been achieved.

109. Results from the mid-term evaluation will be reflected within the project design to influence upscaling and improve overall project implementation.

### E.7.4 Data collection and frequency

108. GIZ will coordinate data collection for implemented activities through responsible executing partner in charge of leading the monitoring activities at local level. The PMU will not guide the monitoring and evaluation. The PMU will aggregate monitoring reports based on local partner inputs at least every six months and make a summary report available to GIZ’s AE unit at head office, again at least every six months.

# F. Risk Assessment and Management

## F.1. Selected risk mitigation measures (next 3 pages)

### Selective Risk Factor 1: Lack of alignment with national strategies and climate change priorities at the national level

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Governance | High | Medium |

**Description:** The project is designed in close coordination with the government of Indonesia and the provincial government of West Kalimantan, with key national development strategies and climate change policies, including short-, medium- and long-term strategies.

### Selective Risk Factor 2: Gaps in coordination between key ministries and agencies

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Governance | High | Medium |

**Description:** Misaligned coordination of different agendas of ministries and agencies could limit the effectiveness of the project to meet its goals, as the project is designed with a strong emphasis on alignment.

### Selective Risk Factor 3: Unclear or complex land tenure and/or land use rights could lead to conflicts

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Governance | High | Medium |

**Description:** Land tenure and land-use rules are often complex and unclear (e.g., overlapping claims with protected areas or customary land) could result in land grabbing, land-use conflicts or expansion of agro-business into forest plantations into protected areas or customary areas.

### Selective Risk Factor 4: Human resources and capacity to support project implementation are insufficient

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Technical and operational | High | Low |

**Description:** The project is supported by a dedicated small grants mechanism for Indigenous Peoples, supporting land tenure, addressing Gender and Age roles and other issues.

## Factor 5: Means of Finance

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Governance | Medium | Medium |

### Corruption or the misuse of funds is a potential risk to resources available under this project.

- Enhanced due diligence process has been carried out with EPCL and/or under the project funding will be provided to hire further staff at the national level.
- A new financial mechanism (IP on grant mechanisms) and be developed to directly channel funds to the local level in a transparent manner. The IP will be used for the implementation of the project activities.
- Monitoring of the use of funding will be regularly conducted to recipients or sub-recipients of award funds. Recipients of funds need to regularly report their progress against the approved budget.
- Rigorous G2E financial management finance rules and regulations and procedures set out by the project.
- Synergies will be sought with the ongoing G2E project “Competition Research in the Forestry Sector” which is closely working with the government on the development of a new IP for the forestry sector.

## Factor 6: Low demand for sustainably produced commodities (on global markets or market fluctuations affect the profitability and sustainable practices)

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Investment operations | Medium | Medium |

### Description

- Sustainably produced commodities might not grow due to a lack of demand on global markets. Also, different countries and financiers ask for different sets of requirements. Without sufficient capacity and a good strategy, the project may not be able to leverage the potential of the G2E-SGMEF project to support sustainable agriculture in the country.
- Traceability of sustainably produced commodities will enable access to premium markets for local communities.

### Description

- The project will reduce the exposure of farmers to price volatility and market fluctuations (activities 2.1.3 and 2.1.4).

## Factor 7: Natural hazards slow down or halt project implementation

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Environment | Medium | Medium |

### Description

- Landslides, tsunamis, or flood and pest fires have been significant natural hazards in the past and can even be aggravated by climate change.

### Description

- Climate risk and vulnerability assessments, including the risk of climate-related natural hazards (e.g., floods) have been conducted at the national level. The project will build on these assessments to identify potential risks associated with climate change and climate-related practices. Thus, the project is likely to help increase the resilience of vulnerable communities and the ecosystems upon which they depend.
- If this project were to leave flood-prone areas it would increase overall risk and is part of the overall design of the project.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 64

## Selective Risk Factor II: Profitable agriculture may create adverse impacts and increase further expansion and clearing of forest

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Description | Low | Medium |

If sufficient safeguards, monitoring and enforcement are not in place there will be increased clearing of forest (or mangroves for aquaculture).

### Mitigation Measures:
- Climate-informed land use planning to help clarify implementation boundaries, as well as monitoring and enforcement of land-use plans.
- Promote the adoption of climate-resilient agricultural practices that reduce emissions intensity, increase carbon sequestration, reduce deforestation and promote sustainable climate-resilient and low emission investments. This will help clearly monitor mitigation and manage risks (e.g., if land needs to be cleared for new investments).
- Develop a monitoring plan and report to a monitoring joint unit allows the provincial government to act as an improved capacity for planning, implementation and monitoring.
- The project will conduct capacity development with public servants and local communities in dedicated carbon protection prevention training (use Article 3.1a & b). This aims to foster awareness, efficient understanding of their rights and responsibilities, and enhanced skills on

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 65

## Selected Risk Factor 12: Community resilience to new practices

### Overview

Different circumstances lead to a low adoption of new sustainable practices. The traditional practices may be preferred due to various reasons, such as ease of implementation, familiarity with the practices, and/or lack of awareness about the benefits of adopting new practices.

### Technical and operational

- **Project:** The project has been developed through participatory processes, engaging diverse stakeholders. Stakeholders expressed interest and support for the project's objectives and activities.
- **Impact:** The project will have a positive impact on ecosystems. At this stage, there is no opportunity to access premium markets for sustainable products (e.g., in the EU). However, the project will contribute to building institutional capacity through training modules for corruption prevention agency (KPK) and training modules for corruption prevention trainings with FMSA, private sector, and local communities.
- **Probability:** Low
- **Impact:** Medium

### Description

Model farms will be used to encourage reluctant farmers to change towards sustainable practices and certification ("being in" is believing). In the case of model farms, the project will provide technical assistance for the implementation of good agricultural practices (GAPs) and support for improved monitoring and traceability systems. This will also provide additional support to farmers to strengthen market access and reduce transaction costs.

Previous training need assessments will be conducted prior to the implementation of trainings in good agricultural practices.

# GCF POLICIES AND STANDARDS

## G.1 Environmental and Social Risk Assessment (max. 750 words, approximately 1.5 pages)

### 20. The Environmental and Social Impact Assessment (ESIA) of the project is provided in Annex 6a. The ESIA rates the risks of the project as category B (medium risk). In general, the project will have a positive environmental and social impact on the communities involved. However, there are some potential negative impacts that may arise from the project which would mostly be site-specific but due to the nature of the interventions these impacts will be irreversible or complex in nature. The ESIA has identified 10 Environmental and Social Sustainability Indicators (ESSIs) for this project which represent a diverse set of socio-economic, cultural and environmental conditions. The high percentage of indigenous peoples living on the land where the project is located (Annex 6c) makes it important to ensure that Indigenous Peoples Policy to ensure they enjoy equitable benefit from the project and their livelihoods are adequately safeguarded. Therefore, the ESIA has identified a number of mitigation measures for each ESSI. The ESIA also provides details on Indigenous Peoples Plans (Annex 6e) were developed through ongoing stakeholder consultations with various groups at all levels of government and civil society organizations. These plans provide guidance to project staff in developing specific protocols for issues concerning SEAH (items 6b) and customary communities (Annex 6c). The project is expected to be implemented under the framework of the National Development Plan (NDP), which provides the overarching policy and regulatory framework, as outlined in Chapters D.1 - D.5.

### 20.1 Environmental and Social Sustainability Indicators (ESSIs)

#### ESS I: Cultural Tenure and Understanding
- **Risk and Impact**: The project will have a positive impact on cultural tenure and understanding of the cultural, tenure, and land use rights of communities living in the project area.
- **Mitigation measures**:
  - Ensure that all relevant stakeholders (particularly IPs) are adequately consulted throughout the project with all affected customary communities, villages, and other stakeholders.
  - Provide training for project staff on cultural sensitivity and respect for local customs and traditions.
  - Ensure that project activities do not affect facilities through trainings.

#### ESS II: Communication and Social Understanding
- **Risk and Impact**: The project will have a positive impact on communication with poor communities and social understanding, as well as increased forest clearance and land use change.
- **Mitigation measures**:
  - Provide training for project staff on cultural sensitivity and respect for local customs and traditions.

#### ESS III: Livelihoods
- **Risk and Impact**: The project will have a positive impact on livelihoods of communities living in the project area, as well as increased forest clearance and land use change.
- **Mitigation measures**:

## ESS 4 Community Livelihoods and Security
- **Potential social conflict and potentially negative impacts on indigenous peoples' rights and access to the assistance from the project:**
    - Conduct community consultation during the design of business models.
    - Project planning and implementation should be inclusive of vulnerable groups and communities in terms of their capacity to participate in the implementation.
    - Consultation and information dissemination activities will be carried out to avoid misinformation about the project.

## ESS 5 Land acquisition, resettlement and compensation
- **The loss of livelihood through rehabilitation of degraded areas is likely to lead to social conflicts:**
    - The project will provide land for relocation from Kapau Kelingu to four other target regencies.
    - Resettlement activities will be initiated and maintained throughout the lifetime of the project in order to ensure that all affected parties are adequately compensated.

## ESS 6 Biodiversity
- **The promotion of agriculture may lead to loss of habitat for endangered species of West Kalimantan such as orangutans, elephants, rhinos, tigers, and other wildlife:**
    - The project will provide the necessary information on how to identify emerging problems.
    - Ensure communication skills for land facilitators for conflict and problem resolution.

## ESS 7 Social and Cultural Practices
- **Social conflicts may arise among indigenous peoples and non-indigenous people over land and resources:**
    - All project activities will happen on land that is already under agricultural use, so there should be no need to relocate communities.
    - Ensure the compliance with existing national laws related to customary communities and their rights to access and manage natural resources.

## ESS 8 Cultural Heritage
- **The integrity of any culturally significant site or structure during development may be compromised:**
    - Protect and safeguard the integrity of any culturally, historically, archaeologically important sites or structures.
    - The project will conduct prior to designation of HCV/HVCS areas and maintained throughout the lifetime of the project.

## ESS 9 Conflict Resolution
- **In some areas, access projects may lead to land use planning and management conflicts:**
    - All affected parties should be consulted on any historical, indigenous, or cultural heritage.
    - The project will provide information on how to identify emerging problems at the landscape level throughout the project to culture heritage lands in areas of the project. Its progress as well as any changes.

## G2. Gender assessment and action plan (max. 500 words; approximately 1/ page)

### 203. A Gender Assessment Plan (GAP) was conducted and developed based on thorough consultations at various levels and is available in Annex B/a.

#### 203.1 The GAP identifies the main gender-related risks and vulnerabilities associated with climate change adaptation, mitigation, and disaster risk reduction policies and programs. It also highlights opportunities to promote gender equality and protect women’s and children’s rights (see also the IPP in Annex C/c). Gender mainstreaming commitments exist at various levels, including the national level, and are reflected in the country’s NDCs, NAPs, and climate change mitigation and adaptation regulations, not in environmental and forestry sector policies due to a lack of capacity.

#### 204. As mentioned in Section D (3), indigenous women in the project area are disproportionately vulnerable to climate change impacts because they have limited access to resources such as land, water, and other natural resources that are essential for their livelihoods. This vulnerability is further exacerbated by gender-based discrimination and lack of access to many of the services to which they have a right, and further escalates their vulnerability to climate change.

#### 205. The following actions will be taken:

- Implementing measures that help women overcome barriers to participation (e.g., planning trainings in community spaces and/or accessible venues that are convenient for women; ensuring inclusive flexible scheduling at times that are convenient for women, considering their domestic responsibilities; and ensuring effective and clear

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 69

## G.3 Financial Management

### G.3.1 Financial Management

206. The financial management of the project will follow GIZ’s internal rules and regulations. GIZ has bank accounts with the following banks: Deutsche Bank, Commerzbank, and Volksbank. These accounts are subject to audit by an independent auditor that will ensure that all funds provided are clearly identifiable and distinguishable from GIZ’s other funds by setting up separate accounts for each project. The financial statements of the project will be prepared on a monthly basis. All revenues received and expenditures incurred will be booked to the respective cost unit according to generally accepted accounting principles (GAAP). The project will be audited annually in accordance with the German Act Against Restraints of Competition (GWB) by an independent auditor. The EEs (GIZ, BPDHL and DNL) have to report progress on the project’s financial performance at least once a year. The annual financial statements prepared for each auditor will perform annual financial audits of the project in line with International Auditing Standards. At the beginning of every fiscal year, the project management team prepares an annual budget for the coming year. These annual plans need to be approved by the PMU with the leading coordination function and are divided into cost units. The monthly financial statements prepared on a monthly basis will be submitted to the auditor prepared for each implementation partner, which is paid after PMU approval by BPDHL. Each implementation partner reports its own national regulation for public procurement in accordance with the German Act Against Restraints of Competition (GWB) and the Public Service Contracts (VOB, VOLS and UVCg) when providing services, construction work and supplies. When a project is funded from the GCF, the project management team will be responsible for ensuring that all procurements from the financial contract, the external Executing Entities will observe their own national regulation for public procurement in accordance with the German Act Against Restraints of Competition (GWB) and the Public Service Contracts (VOB, VOLS and UVCg). The Procurement Guidelines shall not contradict the applicable national procurement law and/or regulations. The project management team is responsible for ensuring that all procurements from the Executing Entities can be observed; the procurement procedures mentioned in the Guidelines are obligatory and must be followed by the Executing Entities. When a project is funded with public funds, the Executing Entities should take reasonable account of economic efficiency as well as ecological sustainability when procuring goods and services. The project management team will monitor the procurement activities carried out by the third parties. The monitoring and oversight procedures will follow GIZ’s internal rules and regulations. All procurements from the project are subject to annual audits, which are internally reviewed and reviewed externally via annual audits. The project's procurement plan is available in Annex 10a and GIZ’s procurement guidelines in Annex 10b.

### G.4 Disclosure of funding proposal

- **No confidentiality information:** The accredited entity confirms that the funding proposal, including its annexes, may be disclosed in full by the GCF, as no information is being provided in confidence.
- **With confidential information:** The accredited entity declares that the funding proposal, including its annexes, may not be disclosed in full and must be treated with confidentiality. Accordingly, the accredited entity is providing to the Secretariat the following two copies of the funding proposal, including all annexes:
  - A redacted copy for internal use by the GCF together with an explanatory note regarding the said portions and the corresponding reason for confidentiality under the accredited entity's terms.
  - A non-redacted copy for disclosure on the GCF website.

The funding proposal can only be processed upon receipt of the two copies above, if containing confidential information.

## Secretariat’s assessment of FP248

Proposal name: Land-based Mitigation and Adaptation through a Jurisdictional Approach in West Kalimantan

Accredited entity: Deutsche Gesellschaft für Internationale Zusammenarbeit (GIZ) GmbH

Country: Indonesia

Project/programme size: Medium

### I. Overall assessment of the Secretariat

1. The funding proposal is presented to the Board for consideration with the following remarks:

| Strengths | Points of caution |
| --- | --- |
| The project focuses on West Kalimantan, a province rich in peatland ecosystems (holding a massive amount of below-ground carbon) and under significant pressure from commodity production, as well as geographical proximity to global shipping lanes. It includes a dedicated financing mechanism for Indigenous Peoples and Local Communities, the country’s most vulnerable populations, who are key development partners in the sustainable management of the province's ecosystems. | Care is needed during the implementation phase to retain the project focus on forest and REDD+ related interventions at local, provincial and national levels in Indonesia. |
| It champions a holistic jurisdictional approach to land use planning across one of the largest provinces in Indonesia. It combines protection, rehabilitation, sustainable management and market-based methods as well as a deforestation-free commodity supply chain, with strong potential for replication. | It is crucial to continuously and iteratively review and adapt regulatory and institutional frameworks to adapt to political changes and ensure that the project remains resilient and low-emission agricultural and forestry practices throughout the project’s lifetime. |
| The project will need to pay special attention in ensuring that the rights of Indigenous Peoples, implementing meaningful participatory land-use planning processes and building capacity are built into the project design, although these have been built-in, but will nevertheless be essential to address potential conflicts over land use rights, preventing potential conflicts and ensuring community engagement and support. | The project will need to pay special attention in ensuring that the rights of Indigenous Peoples, implementing meaningful participatory land-use planning processes and building capacity are built into the project design, although these have been built-in, but will nevertheless be essential to address potential conflicts over land use rights, preventing potential conflicts and ensuring community engagement and support. |

2. The Board may wish to consider approving this funding proposal in accordance with the term sheet agreed between the Secretariat and the accredited entity (AE), and if considered appropriate, subject to the conditions set out in annex II of document GCF/B.04/02.

### II. Summary of the Secretariat’s assessment


---
**Page 82**
---

1. **Project background**

   Indonesia, particularly West Kalimantan, faces significant vulnerability to climate change impacts such as deforestation, forest-level droughts, increased temperatures, changes in precipitation patterns, rising sea levels, and extreme weather events like floods, droughts, and forest fires. The "Land-based mitigation and adaptation through a jurisdictional approach" project aims to address these challenges by implementing a multi-stakeholder approach with the German Federal Ministry for the Environment, Nature Conservation, Building and Nuclear Safety (BMUB) and the Deutsche Gesellschaft für Internationale Zusammenarbeit (GIZ) GmbH and the Government of Indonesia. Aim is to enhance the climate resilience of West Kalimantan’s forests and landscapes.

   These regions face severe climate sensitivity and vulnerability with coastal areas particularly exposed to sea-level rise, increased frequency and intensity of extreme weather events, and biodiversity loss. This project will focus on five priority regions: Kapuas Hulu, Ketapang, Kubu Raya, Sanggau, and Pontianak.

2. **Project components**

   - The project will directly benefit approximately 680,700 people and indirectly assist an additional 4,016,293 individuals by implementing sustainable land and forest management practices, promoting agroforestry systems, and providing access to improved energy services.
   - It is expected to generate a reduction, avoidance, or sequestration of approximately 16.05 million tonnes of carbon dioxide equivalent (Mt CO₂-eq) over the project duration.

3. **The project components are divided into three main elements:**

   - Climate change adaptation, promoting sustainable forest management strategies, and developing climate-resilient landscapes.
   - Sustainable land use and ecosystem conservation, focusing on agroforestry systems and landscape restoration.
   - Forest governance and institutional strengthening, with a focus on local and national frameworks.

4. **Budget allocation across the project components is as follows:**

   - EUR 38.91 million is dedicated to Component 1, aimed at institutional and regulatory frameworks for climate change adaptation (CCA) and sustainable land use.
   - EUR 17.98 million is allocated to Component 2, focusing on sustainable commodity production and social forestry to enhance livelihoods and food systems;
   - EUR 34.05 million is dedicated to Component 3, which seeks to manage, protect and rehabilitate forest and peatland ecosystems.
   - Additional allocations include EUR 11.61 million for monitoring and evaluation (M&E), communication, and awareness-raising.

5. **The project's success is crucial for sustaining its impacts beyond its lifetime. This strategy ensures integration of initiatives into local and national frameworks, emphasizing local ownership and sustainability.**

   The project involves key government actors, strengthens policy frameworks, incorporates social advocacy, financial sustainability through innovative financing and private-sector engagement, and robust monitoring and evaluation systems.

6. **The project faces several key risks, including political challenges such as changes in government priorities and collaboration issues between different governmental bodies. These risks can lead to delays or disruptions in implementing projects that have impacts on agricultural productivity and forest health. Social risks involve potential conflicts over land use and inadequate inclusion of Indigenous and local communities. Financial risks pertain to the availability of resources, including donor funding and private-sector investments. To mitigate these risks, the project involves key government actors, strengthens policy frameworks, incorporates social advocacy, financial sustainability through innovative financing and private-sector engagement, and robust monitoring and evaluation systems.**


---
**Page 83**
---

# Component-by-component analysis

## Component 1: Institutional and regulatory frameworks (total cost: EUR 38.93 million; GCF cost: EUR 25.07 million)

### Component 1 of the project aims to strengthen the institutional and regulatory frameworks for sustainable and climate-resilient forest management in West Kalimantan – serving as a model for other provinces in Indonesia.

#### Project risks include potential political changes in social conditions that could affect commitments, delays or cancellations by government bodies and agencies that could limit project effectiveness, and complex land tenure issues that could lead to conflicts. These risks are mitigated through several strategies: the project employs participatory land-use planning processes; it establishes a multi-stakeholder platform for dialogue on forest management; strengthens regulatory and institutional frameworks to adapt to political changes; and establishes mechanisms to ensure alignment and integration of efforts across different levels of government.

## Component 2: Sustainable commodity production and social forestry (total cost: EUR 19.58 million; GCF cost: EUR 14.07 million)

### In collaboration with BMZ and Solidaridad Network Asia Local Unit (SNAL), Component 2 focuses on enhancing the livelihoods of smallholder farmers in West Kalimantan by improving climate resilience and economic stability.

#### Project risks include limited knowledge and access to finance for smallholders, potential market fluctuations, challenges in achieving certification for sustainable practices. These risks are mitigated through capacity-building activities for farmers and agriculture extension workers, the development of resilient business models, and the establishment of a multi-stakeholder platform for dialogue on forest management.

### Component 2 complements Component 1 by building on the strengthened institutional and regulatory frameworks to ensure that sustainable practices and policies are effectively implemented.


---
**Page 84**
---

# Component 3: Management, protection and rehabilitation of forest and peatland ecosystems (total cost: EUR 37.80 million; GCF cost: EUR 27.64 million)

1. **Component 3a**: Forest management, protection, and rehabilitation of forest and peatland ecosystems, with a strong emphasis on strengthening social forestry schemes and building the capacity of Forest Management Units (FMUs) to implement sustainable land use practices. This component receives particular attention due to its critical role in carbon sequestration, storing considerably more carbon than other terrestrial ecosystems.

2. **Component 3b**: Strengthening FMU capacity for forest management and ecosystem services, such as carbon sequestration and water regulation, while reducing flood and fire risks. The component focuses on improving the institutional framework for obtaining and managing social forestry permits and developing management plans, ensuring access to markets.

3. Project risks include regulatory challenges posed by the UIUC 2020 law*, which limits the role of FMUs, and the complexities of coordinating among a diverse set of stakeholders. These risks are mitigated through close collaboration with key stakeholders, providing extensive training to FMU staff, and fostering public-private partnerships for ecosystem protection.

4. This component complements Component 1 by reducing institutional frameworks for forest management and aligns with Component 2 by supporting sustainable agricultural practices in the region.

5. Additional allocation: Gender, environmental and social management framework, M&E components (total cost: EUR 4.31 million; GCF cost: EUR 2.50 million)

6. The project is classified as category B (moderate risk). Institutional arrangements involve a Memorandum of Understanding (MoU) with the government agreements between Deutsche Gesellschaft für Internationale Zusammenarbeit (GIZ) GmbH and multiple entities, including Badan Penyelenggara Jaminan Sosial (BPJS), the Ministry of Environment and Forestry, and the Government of Indonesia via the Indonesian Ministry of Environment and Forestry (MoEF). The project also involves a Memorandum of Understanding for capacity building and sustainable land management in West Kalimantan.

7. The environmental and social management plan (see footnote) proposed annexes include a gender action plan, a safety and health at work (S&HAW), and sexual harassment (SEAH), with specific procedures for risk mitigation integrated into project management, and a grievance redress mechanism to address complaints related to the project.

8. The environmental and social management plan also emphasizes the importance of free prior and informed consent and robust stakeholder engagement processes to avoid or mitigate potential adverse impacts on indigenous communities and adverse impacts on Indigenous communities. More information is provided in the relevant sections below. The costs for the safeguards are embedded in the components discussed above.

9. The project’s M&E system will comply with relevant GCF policies and agreements, using a continuous improvement approach. It will be based on the GCF’s standard operating procedures financial flows, based on GCF's standard operating procedures, project specific plans (see funding proposal annexes 5 and 113), GCF requirements, and local and national monitoring systems and procedures. The M&E system will include a baseline assessment of project implementation, while BPIUH supervises and reports on disbursements, integrating local data

### III. Assessment of performance against investment criteria

#### 3.1 Impact potential Scale: High

28. The project is expected to generate substantial impact by addressing the environmental and socio-economic issues at rural communities in West Kalimantan through integrated interventions.

29. The project, structured as a cross-cutting initiative with high emphasis on mitigation and adaptation, will be implemented over seven years (project duration) and USD 15 million (CO2 equivalent over seven years; project duration) or USD 10 million CO2 over twenty years (life span).

30. Ecosystem functions will be supported through social forestry initiatives across at least 200,000 hectares of degraded land in the region, including areas with high biodiversity values and High Conservation Value and High Carbon Stocks on non-forest land (Area Penggunaan Lahan Hutan dan Non-Hutan dengan Nilai Konservasi dan Karbon Tinggi).

31. The project will also contribute to the restoration of 200,000 hectares of degraded peatland, with approximately 420,000 hectares of large peat swamps being safeguarded.

32. The project seeks to empower communities to better withstand and recover from climate-related shocks by increasing their adaptive capacity through the establishment of climate islands, where at least 50 per cent are women, by strengthening their capacity to manage climate risks and implementing sustainable land management practices. The project will have a broad impact on approximately 480,269 indirect beneficiaries from improved livelihoods and sustainable land management practices that enhance the overall resilience of the region's ecosystems and communities.

#### 3.2 Paradigm shift potential Scale: High

25. The project is innovative at potentially a potentially for several reasons:

(a) At the jurisdictional level selected, through extensive collaboration with regency-level governments (Kabupaten), allowing for replication across the archipelago.
(b) This strategy integrates government-led multi-stakeholder processes with external actors.

*Ulama LHK is responsible for managing and implementing environmental and forestry policies at the provincial level.
*Kabupaten refers to a region or district in Indonesia, an administrative division below the provincial level.


---
**Page 86**
---

## Sustainable development potential

### Scale: High

As a cross-cutting proposal combining forests and land use, ecosystems and ecosystem services, most vulnerable people and communities and health and well-being, and food and water resources, this project has the potential to contribute significantly to the provision of other ecological services as well as livelihoods and the forest-related economy. These include improved soil fertility, reduced erosion, increased biodiversity, reduced sedimentation due to reduced deforestation and degradation, reducing threats of fires, especially in peatlands, and reduced greenhouse gas emissions. The project promotes practices which support food security and sustainable production practices, enhanced health and food security for communities, including Indigenous Peoples, enhanced business, entrepreneurial and livelihood opportunities associated with increased productivity and livelihoods, and a range of elements empowering women beneficiaries.

## Needs of the recipient

### Scale: High

While located in a middle-income country, West Kalimantan is classified as one of the poorer provinces of Indonesia, partly due to its remoteness and difficulty of access to the interior. The project area is home to the largest remaining block of lowland peat swamp forest in Indonesia. It also contains the largest population of Indigenous Dayak peoples who have historically been economically and socially marginalized, despite their custodianship of some of the most biodiverse and ecologically significant areas on earth, including the province's exceptional natural ecosystems. In order to address the specific needs of Indigenous Peoples, the project includes a dedicated Indigenous Peoples mechanism.

## Country ownership

### Scale: High

In addition to the national designated authority indicating this proposal as high priority and providing a no-objectection letter, alignment with the national REDD+ strategy of Indonesia further ensures that the project will be implemented in an efficient manner. The project also builds on the institutions and financial mechanisms developed as part of Indonesia's REDD+ readiness phase, including the development of a National Forest Monitoring System (NFMS).

## Efficiency and effectiveness

### Scale: Medium to high


---
**Page 87**
---

29\. The proposal demonstrates the efficiency and effectiveness of the project by presenting a detailed economic and financial analysis (EFA). The EFA assesses the economic and financial viability of forest land-use changes and commodity production on a total of 335,000 hectares of land targeted for conversion to oil palm plantations in West Kalimantan. The EFA compares the business-as-usual scenario with the project scenario, transitioning from unutilized forest land to sustainable agricultural practices and conservation of indigenous community forests. The data and assumptions used in the model are from a mixture of literature review, sources from the Indonesian government and stakeholder consultations on the ground.

30\. Indicators for the two types of activities mentioned above include: additional land uses and commodities produced, carbon sequestration and avoided emissions, and economic benefits. These indicators are defined by the following factors: (1) the type of activity; (2) the scale of the activity; (3) the location of the activity; and (4) the time horizon for the activity. The valuation of economic benefits has been primarily derived from the value of greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions reduced, using the World Bank data on the shadow price of carbon. Using a cost-of-carry rate of 15 per cent, the project scenario is estimated to generate revenues of EUR 723 million, with a benefit-to-cost ratio of 3:1 in the project scenario, demonstrating the project's financial viability and long-term sustainability.

31\. Financial viability in the long run and the need for the highest concessionality are demonstrated through the profitability indicators for the same two types of activities. For the commodity production activity, the internal rate of return is estimated to be 20 per cent at a discount rate of 12 per cent, with an overall financial internal rate of return of 20 per cent and net present value of EUR 35 million. The project scenario will result in a decrease in revenues and increase in costs, and will generate negative net present value only when it results in the reduction of revenue by more than 15 per cent and cost increases by more than 25 per cent with 10 years interest rate. Results are negative for some forestry activities and break-even points at 30 years or more. The project scenario is therefore financially viable, demonstrating that the option to invest in this project is viable.

32\. The project model has a total cost of EUR 185 per ha, and the adaptation cost is EUR 44 per hectare. There are EUR 7 per ha total benefit beyond the 7-year project implementation period (GCF cost). These costs are on the lower end of the GCF portfolio in forest land-use projects.

33\. The project will contribute to the achievement of the Sustainable Development Goals by reducing deforestation and promote sustainable agricultural practices and conservation measures that avoid soil degradation, enhance forest carbon stocks through reforestation and forest land management. The project will also contribute to the resilience of forest and peat landscapes in West Kalimantan. It will also provide small grants to Indigenous Peoples and local communities. The project's environmental and social co-benefits include increased biodiversity conservation measures that avoid soil degradation, promotion of sustainable agricultural practices and biodiversity conservation measures that avoid soil
