### Environmental and Social (E&S) risk category and safeguard instruments.

The project is classified as Category B by the AE which the Secretariat confirms, and it is within the AE's acceptable limits for E&S risks. The risks are generally considered to be low in terms of environmental and/or social risks and impacts that individually or cumulatively, are few, generally manageable, and can be mitigated through a combination of project design and implementation of mitigation measures. An Environmental and Social Impact Assessment (ESIA) has been conducted for the project and an Environmental and Social Management Plan (ESMP) has been prepared. The project will consist of various activities and sub-activities under the ESMP. Site-specific activities with various ESIs, including government units and communities. These site-specific activities are subject to detailed environmental and social management plans in consultation with the respective EEs.

### Compliance with GCF Environmental and Social Safeguards (ESS) standards.

The following paragraphs describe how the project complies with the standards.

#### ES1 (Assessment and Management of Environmental and Social Risks and Impacts)

The ES1 has been assessed that the project will provide technical assistance, capacity-building, and linkages to services for various government and private sector entities, including local communities and individual smallholders, to help them develop their own ESS. The ESMP provides a framework for the assessment of general risks and/or impacts, and the ESMP provides the measures to be implemented in order to mitigate these risks and/or impacts. The effectiveness of implementation will be assessed and vetted on their impacts and risky or subjecting them to environmental and social risks. The ESMP shall ensure that the ESS is followed in the implementation of the activities implemented by the PMU and other implementing entities. All relevant information and data collected will be processed through the Environmental and Social Management System of BPDL.

#### ES2 (Labour and Working Conditions)

The ES1 has assessed that the risk regarding labour and working conditions is low. The ESMP has highlighted the risk of infringement of "decent work" standards and physical harm to government and private sector entities, including local communities and individual smallholders during the enforcement of forestry regulations. The ESMP has provided measures including capacity-building of project staff, an information campaign on labour rights, medical screening of workers, provision of personal protective equipment (PPE), training in the prevention of physical harm during enforcement of regulations, measures proposed include training of FMU staff involved in forest law enforcement and community patrol activities, and mandatory use of personal protective equipment.

#### ES3 (Resource Efficiency and Pollution Prevention)

The ES1 has identified the use of pesticides as a potential environmental risk from smallholder farming and agriculture and waste from the production of fish feed as potential pollution sources. These would come from individual smallholders and local communities. The measures being proposed include: not supporting the direct procurement of agricultural inputs, including fertilizers and pesticides; providing training to smallholder farmers on alternatives to agrochemicals are widely documented (e.g., oil palm); and provide capacity-building and awareness-raising activities for smallholder farmers on the use of environmentally friendly practices and responsible use of agrochemicals/pesticides. The project will also support an integrated pest management approach. It is recommended that the project consider the potential for soil erosion from deforestation, and take appropriate measures to prevent it by implementing erosion control activities and other earth-moving works as necessary. The project should also consider


---
**Page 89**
---

potential emissions and effluents from other non-farming activities which the project may support at the level of the commodity value chains, and alternative livelihood interventions.

### ES4 (Community Health, Safety and Security)
The ESAs has identified (a) the risk of social conflict arising from misunderstanding due to "insufficient human capacities, knowledge and resources" in the project area; (b) the risk associated with the lack of trust between and within communities as well as "insufficient communication and consultation skills"; and (c) the risk associated with the potential for conflicts between and within communities due to perceived unfairness in the allocation of project benefits. The ESAs has identified a number of measures that will be implemented to address these risks, including: (i) setting up a dedicated ES team; (ii) initiation of the free, prior and informed consent (FPIC) process prior to implementation of the project activities and throughout the project work at all levels, including customary communities, villages, and other stakeholders; (iii) conducting awareness-raising campaigns about Indigenous Peoples' local community rights and responsibilities in relation to the project; (iv) training of facilitators; (v) skills training of land facilitators; (vi) provision of grievance mechanisms; and (vii) provision of a conflict resolution mechanism. These measures are described further under ES3 above.

### ES5 (Land Acquisition and Involuntary Resettlement)
The ESAs has identified possible involuntary resettlement (loss of livelihood) as one of the impacts of the planned rehabilitation of degraded areas in the AFL and peat and mangrove areas as the activity might require the relocation of communities. The project will not implement any land acquisition or resettlement plan. However, the mitigation measures proposed in the ESMP enumerate the strategies and approaches that will be implemented to avoid involuntary resettlement where such impacts are avoided. These include activities such as the Participatory Land Use Planning, the conduct of FPICs, and the provision of a grievance mechanism. The project will also not engage in any activities that would violate community rights, and if any form of involuntary expected, identified or claimed, the project will not intervene in the respective area. The FPIC process will be initiated at an early stage to ensure free, prior and informed consent from all participating villages, affected vulnerable groups and other stakeholders prior to the implementation.

### ES6 (Biodiversity Conservation and Sustainable Management of Living Natural Resources)
The climate mitigation part of the project also conserves biodiversity as it is generally recognized that the conversion of primary forest into agricultural land carries a high risk of expansion of agriculture production into primary forest and natural habitat in the implementation area. The ESMP has identified several strategies and approaches for efficient production forestry and an agriculture value chain. The ESMP has enumerated strategies and approaches to avoid negative impacts on biodiversity, including: (i) promotion of agroforestry; (ii) land expansion, monoculture farming systems, or timber plantations; the use of lands that are already degraded or abandoned; (iii) conservation areas are highly degraded; participatory land planning; capacity-building and awareness campaigns; and service provision. The project will also not engage in any activities that could have the potential to negatively impact aspects of ecological value or result in the conversion of primary forest into agricultural land.

### GCF Indigenous Peoples Policy and ES7 (Indigenous Peoples)
With the presence of Indigenous Peoples in the project area, the AE has prepared an Indigenous Peoples Plan. The AE notes that the project will not directly affect the lands and territories of the Indigenous Peoples Alliance of the Nusantara. The funding proposal envisions an on-going engagement with the Indigenous Peoples Alliance of the Nusantara (APIAN) through Stakeholder engagement both with Indigenous Peoples and local communities in the project area was undertaken in the development of the funding proposal. Free, prior and informed


---
**Page 90**
---

### Stakeholder Engagement and Grievance Redress Mechanism (GRM)

The project concept document outlines a comprehensive approach to stakeholder engagement and grievance redress mechanism (GRM). This is designed to ensure that key national government agencies such as the MoF, Ministry of Finance, the RHPUL various government departments, local communities, and villages are involved. The project has also consulted the local communities, including representatives from the Indigenous Dayak community, civil society organizations, relevant government organizations, NGOs, representatives of private sector development partners, and members of the relevant multi-stakeholders' (MoS) including the REEDs Working Group, Working Group on Water Management, and Tengkawang Network. These consultations were documented in annexes to the project concept document.

The GRM is a key component of the project's design. A Stakeholder Engagement Plan has been prepared and submitted as part of the funding proposal. The plan outlines the process of stakeholder identification, the principles of engagement, and the plan for the consultation and communication with stakeholders throughout the project lifecycle. It includes procedures, consultations, capacity-building and workshops, as well as due procedures disclosure and protocols.

### Implementation Arrangements

As related to safeguards, the project will engage a team of experts in various fields to make up an EES team that will support the development, capacity-building, training, implementation, monitoring and reporting needed to carry out the ESM. At least one member of this team should be a gender specialist who ensures that issues of sex and gender will ensure that safeguards are implemented and monitored. Other advisors and staff such as the Indigenous Peoples’ Rights Advisor, Water Management Advisor, High Carbon Stock Area Staff, Social Engagement/Liaison Adviser, High Conservation Value-High Carbon Stock Area Staff, social inclusion advisor, biodiversity specialist, gender specialist, land tenure specialist, grievance redress mechanism (GRM) specialist, and other relevant experts will be engaged as needed throughout the lifecycle of the project. The main function of the EES team will be to conduct and oversee the ESM implementation at all stages of the project.

The detailed functions of the EES team are also described in the ESM.


---
**Page 91**
---

### Gender policy

The GCF has provided a gender assessment and action plan, therefore complies with the GCF gender policy.

* Despite Indonesia’s commitment to women's empowerment and gender equality, the distribution of benefits from development initiatives remains uneven between men and women.
    * Women face several barriers to participating in activities that can lead to economic empowerment. These include access to credit, land tenure, and participation in initiatives that do not guarantee additional income and are constrained by heavy domestic workloads, with little role-sharing between men and women.
        * Their contributions and ideas are frequently undervalued or ignored, leading to the perpetuation of gender stereotypes and less resilient value chains. Additionally, specific perceptions about women cannot perform heavy work or engage in business activities are prevalent among men and women.
    * Specific assistance programmes for women's groups are lacking, and gender issues are not yet fully integrated into development planning and implementation through sustainable gender mainstreaming activities.

Based on the findings of the gender assessment, the project will focus on creating an enabling environment for gender-responsive management. This will be achieved through awareness-raising, training, and the development of gender-responsive systems and resources for various stakeholders. The project will also develop a framework to facilitate gender-responsive operational guides, regulatory frameworks, gender mainstreaming guidelines, minimum standards, and other tools. Additionally, it will establish collaboration platforms that include institutions focused on gender issues.

The project will also create opportunities for women by enhancing their skills and providing access to information and knowledge about land tenure, credit, financial instruments, business development, protection, marketing, and agro-processing. Additionally, the project will develop financial instruments and other tools to support women in accessing resources that can lead to change and benefit from land- and forest-based businesses. These interventions are expected to bring about changes in gender relations and increase women's access to resources while ensuring they share equitably while gaining relevant skills and information to sustain their involvement. ### Risks

#### 4.3.1 Overall programme assessment (low risk)

The project aims to support the Government of Indonesia and people of West Kalimantan in achieving their goals for resilient management of forests and landscapes at scale. The overall objective is to reduce GHG emissions from deforestation and degradation, enhance forest carbon stocks through reforestation and forest land rehabilitation, improve livelihoods and food security, and increase resilience of forests and landscapes in West Kalimantan. Activities will address the two main climate risks (i) increased frequency and intensity of droughts and (ii) increasing temperatures which could reduce agricultural production due to increased drought. This will enable a paradigm shift towards climate-resilient land use practices.

The project is divided into three components: (i) Component 1 - Institutional and regulatory frameworks; (ii) Component 2 - Sustainable community-based forest management, including reforestation, rehabilitation of degraded forests, and the restoration and rehabilitation of forest and peatland ecosystems. With a total cost of EUR 100.19 million, the project will be funded by EUR 59.40 million from GCF as grant, with additional co-financing totaling EUR 67.21 million (EUR 38.71 million in grants and EUR 28.50 million in co-financing). The co-financing includes funding for the Smallholder Supply Chains in Kapuas Hulu (commissioning agreement) and the KIW development project, as well as existing GCF projects.

#### 4.3.2 Accredited entity/recipient entity capable to execute the current programme (low risk)

GIZ will serve as the executing entity. GIZ has eight approved GCF projects across Asia, Latin America, and the Caribbean, with a strong track record in Indonesia, where it has been operating since 1975, working on climate change, forest and agricultural supply chains. GIZ Indonesia currently provides EUR 45 million in technical assistance in the green sector and has a total portfolio of EUR 203 million. GIZ is also implementing three other projects with three other EEs namely BLPB, MoEF and NSAL. The AI will provide oversight, guidance and quality assurance for the project implementation as well as the execution of the funded activity agreement (FAA), which is still under development. As the FAA has also provided adequate capacity to undertake the activities, GIZ has sufficient capacity to undertake the role as EE.

#### 4.3.3 Programme-specific execution risks (low risk)

Co-financing: We acknowledge the substantial co-financing totaling EUR 67.21 million (grants and in-kind), resulting in a co-financing ratio of 19.68. If this co-financing does not materialize, it could significantly impact the project's intended outcomes, including reducing GHG emissions from deforestation and degradation, increasing forest cover through improved agricultural practices, and strengthening the resilience of forest and peat landscapes in West Kalimantan. The GCF will provide technical assistance to the executing entity (EE) and recipient entity (RE) to ensure the reporting and realization of the co-financing per GCF requirements, though the FAA for the proposed project has not yet been finalized.


---
**Page 93**
---

### Compliance risk (medium risk)

The Secretariat has conducted a comprehensive compliance risk assessment of the proposal, concluding that there is a medium level of inherent risk to potential governance issues, corruption, and money laundering/terrorist financing (ML/TF) risks.

#### Primary risks identified

1. The potential for corruption, particularly in the context of large-scale development projects where transparency, accountability, and ethical governance are critical.
2. ML/TF risks, with a high probability that funds will be misused or diverted due to inadequate oversight mechanisms.
3. Potential collusion between private sector entities and public authorities.

#### Mitigation measures

To address these risks, the proposal outlines several mitigation measures for:

1. The misuse of funds: enhanced due diligence processes have been applied, and additional personnel will be recruited if necessary.
2. ML/TF risks: the decentralization of financial decision-making among Indigenous Peoples and Local Communities (IPLCs) ensures that funds are used as intended. Further transparency is ensured through screenings on the UN sanctions list to prevent ML/TF risks. Additionally, synergies with existing anti-corruption initiatives will be applied.
3. Governance issues: due to the comprehensive nature of these measures, the residual risk is significantly reduced.

#### GCF portfolio concentration risk (low risk)

In case of approval, the impact of this proposal on GCF portfolio concentrations in terms of credit exposure is deemed minimal.

### Recommendation

It is recommended that the Board consider the above factors in its decision.

# Summary risk assessment

## Rationale

The funding proposal has an overall low risk of failure based on the issues highlighted. It is recommended that GCF Board consider the above factors in its decision.

### Overall programme

- **Accession entity (AE)/executing entity (EE) capability**: Low
  - The AE/EE has a limited track record and capacity to manage large-scale projects.
  
- **Project-specific execution**: Low
  - There are concerns about the project's ability to deliver on time and within budget.

### GCF portfolio/concentration

- **Compliance**: Medium
  - Compliance risks exist, but they can be mitigated with proper oversight and monitoring.

## Fiduciary

### Financial management of the project will follow GCF internal rules and regulations. GIZ will open separate bank accounts at Deutsche Bundesbank and Commerzbank, setting up separate cost units to track GCF funds clearly and distinctly. All transactions will adhere generally accepted accounting principles and procedures approved by the German Government.

Funds will be disbursed in accordance with the project plan and budget, ensuring proper use of funds and progress reports from the executing entities (GIZ, BPDHJ and SNAL). Independent external auditors will conduct annual financial audits in line with International Auditing Standards.

At the start of each year, an annual plan will outline the activities for the year, which will be approved by GIZ. Each implementing partner will submit monthly progress reports on plans and budgets, approved and disbursed by BPDHJ. Each implementation partner reports monthly to GIZ, which then consolidates these into GIZ’s central accounting system, forming the basis for financial reporting to GIZ.

Funds are also disbursed annually to target countries implementing large forest projects. The funds will be disbursed in accordance with their project plan, which is then transferred by BPDHJ. The Indigenous Peoples and local communities grant mechanism follows BPDHJ’s procedures.

GIZ's financial management system ensures transparent and accountable use of funds, while the use of separate cost units adheres to accepted accounting principles, and regular audit procedures are in place. These measures meet GCF fiduciary requirements for an effective financial control and ensure efficient use of project funds.

## Results monitoring and reporting

The project uses a results-based M&E system grounded in GIZ standard operating procedures and a comprehensive monitoring plan to track inputs, activities, outputs, impacts, and financial performance (section B.4.3). The monitoring plan includes annual work plans, budgets and M&E system (section B.4.4). Key focus results on reducing GHG emissions, enhancing biodiversity conservation, sustainable land use, and forest management. Indicators include GHG emissions reduction, beneficiaries adopting climate-resilient practices, and new regulatory frameworks. Outputs and indicators are:

- **Increased access to renewable energy in targeted municipalities**
- **Smallholder farmers practicing climate-resilient agriculture**
- **Improvement in food security and poverty-gap indices**

These align with the theory of change and logical framework for sustainable, climate-resilient outcomes (section B.1C(2)). The theory of change outlines a shift towards sustainable

### Legal assessment

The Accreditation Master Agreement was signed with the Accredited Entity on 15 November 2017 and became effective on 18 January 2019, which was amended and restated pursuant to the GCF Board Decision No. 46/02 dated 30 June 2021, and which became effective on 18 January 2024 (the "AMA").

The Accredited Entity has provided a legal opinion or certificate confirming that it has obtained all necessary approvals, consents, permits, licenses, and authority to implement the project.

The proposed project will be implemented in the Republic of Indonesia ("Indonesia"), a country in which GCF is not provided with privileges and immunities. This means that amongst other things, GCF is subject to Indonesian laws on bankruptcy and insolvency, which risks need to be further assessed. The GCF Secretariat provided a draft agreement on privileges and immunities for the Republic of Indonesia, which was approved by the Board in 2015. It further submitted an updated P&I agreement and a background note to the Government of Indonesia.

The Heads of the Independent Redress Mechanism (IRM) and Independent Integrity Unit (IIU) have both expressed that it would not be legally feasible to undertake their redress activities against the Republic of Indonesia, as they are not provided with relevant privileges and immunities. Therefore, it is recommended that disbursements by the GCF are made in accordance with Indonesian laws on bankruptcy and insolvency and expropriation in the country, or has been provided with appropriate privileges and immunities.

To address the matters raised in this section, it is recommended that any approval by the Board be accompanied by:

(a) Signature of the fund agreement activity in a form and substance satisfactory to the GCF Secretariat within 180 days from the date of Board approval; and

(b) Completion of the legal due diligence to the satisfaction of the GCF Secretariat.

Independent Technical Advisory Panel's assessment of FP248

Proposal name: Land-based Mitigation and Adaptation through a Jurisdictional Approach in West Kalimantan

Accredited entity: Deutsche Gesellschaft für Internationale Zusammenarbeit (GIZ) GmbH

Country: Indonesia

Project/programme size: Medium


## Assessment of the independent Technical Advisory Panel

### 1.1 Overview

This is the first submission of the funding proposal titled "Land-based Mitigation and Adaptation through a Jurisdictional Approach in West Kalimantan" under the standard proposal approval process.

The accredited entity (AE) is the Deutsche Gesellschaft für Internationale Zusammenarbeit GmbH (GIZ), an international development organisation, with support from the International Environment Fund (IEF) (Badan Pengadaan Dana Lingkungan Hidup or BPDLH), Solidarity Network for Climate Change Adaptation (SNCCA), and the Indonesian Ministry of Forestry representing the Government of Indonesia.

The overall objective of this cross-sectoral project is to reduce greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions through land use change, including reforestation, integration, forest carbon stocks through reforestation and forest land rehabilitation, improve agricultural practices and ultimately strengthen climate resilience in West Kalimantan. The project will be implemented specifically in the following five regencies: Kapuas Hulu, Ketapang, Kubu Raya, Sungai dan Sintang.

The expected mitigation outcome is 16.05 million tonnes of carbon dioxide equivalent (Mt CO2e) over a period of seven years and 34.8 Mt CO2e over the project's five-year implementation period of the project.

The expected adaptation outcome encompasses GHG, LULUCF direct and indirect benefits, and focuses on three result area adaptations (ARAs): most vulnerable people and communities (ARA1), health and well-being, and food and water security (ARA2) and climate change resilience (ARA3).

The project will achieve the mitigation and adaptation outcomes by using a holistic approach structured around three components:

- Community-based land use frameworks: This is intended to attract investments into sustainable and climate-resilient agricultural and agroforestry value chains.
- Land-use planning frameworks to implement policies on sustainable and climate-resilient forest management, developing land-use plans that account for climate change, identifying high-priority areas for conservation and restoration, and creating a dedicated grant mechanism to ensure the active engagement and participation of Indigenous Peoples (IPs) in sustainable land management practices.


---
**Page 97**
---

### Component 2: Sustainable agricultural supply chains production and social forestry

This component will increase the climate resilience of vulnerable local communities as well as their capacities to engage in sustainable community production and social forestry activities. It will also contribute to the development of markets for forest-related commodities and facilitating the access to new markets for these sustainable products, while ensuring that all actors involved are able to demonstrate compliance with environmental compliance and ensures the scalability and replicability of sustainable practices.

### Component 3: Management, protection and rehabilitation of forest and peatland ecosystems

This component will focus on improving the capacity of forest management units (FMsU) in Indonesia to manage their forests sustainably. It will also contribute to the conservation and protection and sustainable management of forest and peatland ecosystems, as well as supporting local communities in obtaining land-use rights and implementing various social forestry activities.

The overall budget for this funding proposal is EUR 100.12 million, with a funding request of EUR 75.83 million to be agreed.

In its consideration of the ITAP considered all documents as submitted on 25 July 2024 as well as the answers to questions of the ITAP received on 13 August and during the online meeting on 6 August 2024.

## 1.2 Impact potential

### Scale: Medium to high

The ITAP assessment of the climate impact potential is guided by decision 833/II, which requires that all projects demonstrating the mitigation and adaptation impact potential of GCF-supported activities, as well as by other appraisal and assessment guidance that considers the relevant investment criteria and is endorsed by the Board.

### Mitigation

1. West Kalimantan is the third largest province in Indonesia and one of the country's deforestation hotspots.
2. According to the funding proposal, since 1990, the forests in West Kalimantan have declined by 27 per cent, from 7.5 million hectares (ha) to 5.4 million ha in 2020 and the annual deforestation rate increased from 78/0.1 ha/year in 1996–2000 to 95/0.52 ha/year in 2010-2020.
3. More specifically, the five priority project regencies are severely threatened by deforestation and forest degradation: Katingan Regency with 47,800 ha in Tanah Merah, 60,000 ha in Kemas Hulu, 27,000 ha in Katingan, 8,000 ha each in Kubu Raya, 2,900 ha in Sanggau and 4,700 ha in Sekadau Regency.
4. The implementation of the activities in the three project components mentioned above are expected to reduce GHG emissions from deforestation and forest degradation and enhance forest carbon stock (Ton CO₂eq).

18. The Vulnerability Sourcebook (GIZ, 2014) and the Risk Supplement (GIZ, 2017) were used as a methodological approach for preparing climate impact chains and understanding the related development risks.

29. The funding proposal identifies three key current and future climate hazards in West Kalimantan: extreme rainfall events, higher temperatures and long dry spells. These climate hazards are expected to increase the risk of forest fires, drought, loss of biodiversity, land degradation and soil erosion and reducing soil moisture in the coming decades.

30. At the level of plantations and forest ecosystems, the increasing climate hazards cause tree and crop damage, decrease soil fertility, increase water stress and the occurrence of pests and diseases. These impacts are expected to reduce yields and productivity of timber and non-timber forest products.


---
**Page 100**
---

both qualitatively and quantitatively, and induce a reduction in forest area and a migration of some crop systems to a higher altitude.

21. Smallholders and Indigenous Peoples are exposed to these climate hazards and suffer from the decreasing provision of goods and ecosystem services from agricultural and forest areas, reducing their adaptive capacity. This increases the vulnerability of local communities: low capacity and lack of coordination in the public sector; anchor crops that are not adapted to changing climatic conditions; limited access for communities in sustainable land planning; lack of finance and lack of suitable business models; and a lack of knowledge on climate change.

22. The consequence is a high risk for institutional, social and ecological systems that requires a systemic and multi-stakeholder approach towards adaptation to climate change. Without the project, the capacity of smallholders and Indigenous Peoples will be further undermined by increasing climate hazards, deforestation, forest resilience being undermined through forest degradation and loss of biodiversity.

23. As a response, the project will facilitate the integration of climate change adaptation into medium-term regional development plans, promote an institutional framework for adaptation and mitigation in the region, and support the development of a national strategy on climate change. The project will increase the adaptive capacity of 680,107 direct and 4,862,269 indirect beneficiaries.

24. In order to monitor and evaluate progress in adapting to climate change the project will support the development of an integrated monitoring system (IM) for adaptation (see annex 11.1.4). It will integrate an online platform where all required information can be uploaded for monitoring and evaluation purposes. The IM will be linked with the existing provincial monitoring system and will be developed together with the provincial monitoring system for mitigation activities. The IM will provide data to government agencies on monitoring and evaluating adaptation results and outcomes.

25. According to further clarification provided by the AE during the question and answer process, the project will also support the development of a Social Inclusion Database System (SIDS), a specialized data tool tailored towards measuring and monitoring the vulnerability of Indigenous Peoples in the region. The SIDS will be developed for the five participating regencies and will serve as these to a baseline to track future changes in adaptation capacity and sensitivity. In addition, the SIDS data will inform tailored village-level interventions that are designed to increase the adaptive capacity of smallholders. At the end of the project will allow evaluating changes in climate vulnerability and resilience attributable to the project.

26. The ITAP considers the information on climate risk as clear and the proposed response is generally sound. It also acknowledges that the funding proposal is aligned with the provisions in the National Adaptation Plan (NAP) and the NDCs, which are based on nationally determined contribution (2021). Additionally, the ITAP wishes to highlight the clarity and transparency of the methodology used for the calculation of direct beneficiaries (Annex 24), particularly regarding the calculation of direct beneficiaries.

27. However, concerns remain about how to develop the specific design of climate-resilient agricultural systems in a way that is sustainable over time; to ensure the timely provision of plant materials and technological tools for each of the selected systems. The ITAP recognizes that the AE has taken this into account.

28. In view of the above, the ITAP assesses the climate impact potential of the funding proposal as medium to high, mainly because of the (potential) overestimation of the mitigation impact potential.

# 1.3 Paradigm shift potential

## Scale: Medium to high

There are two criteria to bear in mind when assessing the paradigm shift potential of this funding proposal. First, as mentioned above, Indonesia is one of the largest REDD+ countries and as such it has already developed a strong national framework. Thus, more than upscaling, what is required is to ensure that the project can be scaled down to the level of communities at a lower scale, namely at the province and regency level. Second, replicability and scalability of REDD+ activities implemented at the local level are limited owing to their context-specific nature.

Owing to its high level of alignment with the national policies, the project can clearly contribute to both mitigation and adaptation targets. More importantly, component 1 of the project is aimed at strengthening the enabling conditions for the promotion of poverty-reducing climate-resilient REDD+ activities and components 2 and 3 both entail financial mechanisms and core capacity building in support of these activities. The proposed project design has been designed as a response to the categories of barriers identified in the funding proposal: government policies, institutional arrangements, and lack of resources.

Another positive feature of the project is the replicable use of existing tools and methodologies for both mitigation and adaptation outcomes. The methodological approach for mitigation includes the development of an emission factor database and the estimation of the mitigation impact potential of activities at the regency level, can be used in other provinces as well. Similarly, the proposed methodology for adaptation and tools can be used to understand climate risks and proposing adaptation responses. This can also be used in other regions.

However, some factors reduce the paradigm shift potential of this funding proposal. First, the complexity of the challenges regarding land tenure and land-use rights, which is far beyond the scope of the project design, may limit its impact on mitigation outcomes. Second, the high probability of occurrence of the risks mentioned in paragraph 17(a) above, especially those related to climate change and market volatility, as well as the theory of change assumes that the demand for sustainably sourced agricultural products will incentivize the private sector actors to shift their business practices. This will leverage private sector investments in sustainable land management (SLM), which is not covered by existing funds (e.g., in the European Union) or on non-binding international agreements. Although the ITAF fully recognizes that there are no guarantees of market demand and limitations for shifting production patterns during the implementation phase of this project.

In view of the above, the TAP assesses the paradigm shift potential of the funding proposal as medium to high.


# 1.4 Sustainable development potential

## Scale: High

In addition to its contribution to Sustainable Development Goal (SDG) 13 (Climate action), the implementation of this project will directly contribute to achieving SDG 2 (Zero hunger), SDG 5 (Gender equality), SDG 12 (Responsible consumption and production) and SDG 15 (Life on land).

This project can provide important institutional, social and economic co-benefits. This includes improvements in food security due to the promotion of agroforestry systems, as well as improvements in livelihoods through the diversification of income sources associated with climate-resilient agricultural, agroforestry and community forestry practices. In addition, the project can contribute to poverty reduction by providing employment opportunities.

With regard to environmental benefits, the project will identify high biodiversity hotspots and reduce deforestation and forest degradation. These activities will contribute to

## Needs of the recipient

The funding proposal includes all relevant information explaining the vulnerability and financing needs of the targeted population and ecosystems (annexes Za–Zde and za–cd).

According to the World Bank, Indonesia is the largest economy in South-East Asia and registered a GDP growth rate of 5.4 per cent in 2019. The projected economic growth for the same year was projected at 5.0 per cent over the medium term 2024-2026. The archipelagic country counts more than 7,390 islands and a population of 270.2 million as at 2020. The climate is tropical with an average temperature between 25°C to 31°C. Indonesia has been divided into 34 provinces, which are further subdivided into regencies. The Human Development Index (HDI) for the country was estimated at 0.698 in 2019 and the poverty rate was 7.2 per cent of the gross domestic product. Although the government's debt is considered safe, the country still faces a number of challenges.

Located on the island of Borneo, West Kalimantan is the third largest province of Indonesia, covering an area of 147,307 km². In 2020, the province had a population of 5.41 million people and a GDP per capita of USD 896 (World Bank). According to the Central Bureau of Statistics of Indonesia, the poverty rate in West Kalimantan was 6.72 per cent in 2022, with 305,254 households living below the poverty line. The villages are classified as least developed. In 2019, the Gini index for West Kalimantan was 0.83.

West Kalimantan is culturally diverse. The main ethnic groups are the Dayak (6 per cent), Malay (57 per cent) and Bugis (3 per cent). The Dayak and Malay ethnic groups are considered the two Indigenous People of Indonesia.

West Kalimantan's gross regional domestic product (GRDP) grew steadily from 2010 to 2021 and the province ranked 17 out of 34 provinces in terms of its GRDP in 2021. Although its GRDP growth rate was lower than the national average, the per capita gross regional domestic product per capita in 2020 was relatively low compared with the national average, placing West Kalimantan among the least developed regions.

The agriculture sector is the second contributor to the GRDP (21.42 per cent). The relative contribution of the forestry subsector has decreased recently although it remained significant at 9.58 per cent in 2020 compared with 13.76 per cent in 2019 and 14.71 per cent in 2020. Conversely, the plantation (estate crop) subsector's contribution has been rising. A significant share of the total agricultural output produced in the province is exported, with the total export value reaching EUR 1.71 billion in 2020.

Fifty-five per cent of West Kalimantan’s area is designated as forest area, covering 8.2 million hectares (ha). The main types are primary production forest (2.1 million ha), convertible production forest (0.2 million ha) and limited


---
**Page 103**
---

production forest (2.1 billion ha). Additionally, the region includes 2.3 million ha classified as protection forest, nature conservation area and strict nature reserve.

* Almost 80 per cent of the remaining forests and peatlands in West Kalimantan are in five priority regencies to be addressed by the project, where forest ecosystems provide climate, food and water security for millions of people living in poverty.
* Forest-dependent communities constitute 62 per cent of all villages in West Kalimantan.
* More than half of the population is under 18 years old and lives in rural areas.
* Although social forestry (limiting to community forestry) has a long-standing customary tradition in West Kalimantan, and has seen improvements in political and legal progress over recent years, only 39 per cent of the target total forestry licenses have been issued.
* Limited budget support and insufficient capacities remain two key barriers that must be overcome by project management units effective in achieving sustainable forest management on the ground;
* The complex land tenure and use rights legislation for forests land increasingly affects the ability to implement conservation, restoration and enhancement activities among Indigenous Peoples; and
* (d) The production of agro-forestry commodities for international markets, such as palm oil and rubber, is a major driver of deforestation and forest degradation. While these commodities generate higher economic benefits, they tend to concentrate such benefits in a small number of hands, while limiting the potential technological support and trading capacities.

* As climate change will increase overall vulnerability of social and ecological systems (as explained above), overcoming the barriers to the sustainable use of ecosystems becomes even more challenging.
* The integrated project approach used in the design of this funding proposal is an adequate response to promote a climate-resilient and low-carbon pathway that attends the needs of the population, governments and ecosystems in the regencies included in the project. Thus the IFAP assesses the needs of the recipient as high.

### 1.6 Country ownership Scale: High

* As clarified in section 1 above on impact potential, the funding proposal is fully aligned with the national development vision for West Kalimantan and the provincial government of West Kalimantan. Therefore, it is plausible to expect that the project's implementation will significantly contribute to the achievement of the national development vision.
* The IFAP acknowledges the capacities of the AE and the three EEs included in the governance structure of the project (see figure below, as provided in the funding proposal). The previous experience of these entities with forest management and climate change-related activities, as well as its recognition by key actors in the institutional climate change framework of Indonesia, are indicators of the high capacity of GIZ for AE and EE for this funding proposal.
* IEP is a public sector entity – a non-ethnic unit accountable to the Ministry of Finance, which was established in 2017 under the Government of West Kalimantan’s development vision and strategy for all funding streams to align with over time. IEP supports the Government of West Kalimantan in its role as the main implementing agency (IA) and is also the main financing vehicle for channelling and distributing the GCF funds to the beneficiaries and manage the dedicated grant mechanism for Indigenous Peoples. IEP will coordinate with the

### Source: Funding Proposal

15. Solidaridad Network Asia Ltd is part of the global network Solidaridad International, which is an international civil society organization founded in 1969 that facilitates the development of fair and profitable supply chains for small-and family farmers. The organization works with small-scale farmers to improve their agriculture and mining sectors at four levels: farm or producer, business, policy and market. Solidaridad is registered as a non-profit organization in Indonesia since 2012, working with local governments in West Kalimantan and farmers of the region. In addition, Solidaridad has been involved in social and environmental programs covering 27,858 ha. Additionally, Solidaridad has been involved in social forestry as well as collaboration with the private sector.

16. MoAF (Ministry of Agriculture) is a key actor for conserving forests and the environment in Indonesia and the support of several of its Directors General is necessary for implementing REDD+ and GCF projects. The Ministry of Environment and Forestry under the President of Indonesia at the national focal point to coordinate climate change efforts, including the climate change mitigation process at the UNFCCC. All organizations dealing with climate change, including MoAF, are now part of the Ministry of Environment and Forestry. MoAF merged within a Directorate General on Climate Change in MoAF. The Directorate General on Climate Change has been established as a focal point for all climate actions. It oversees REDD+ and GCF implementation.

Additionally, the annexes outline the strategic framework for the project, which is based on the principles of participation, respecting the priorities, rights and customs of Indigenous Peoples and local communities.

project. However, the ITAP considers some assumptions to be overly optimistic. Firstly it appears challenging for local stakeholders to self-finance the transition phase, especially when the break-even point for sustainably produced commodities is 3-11 years. This could significantly impact the project's ability to achieve its intended practices, thereby delaying the adaptation and mitigation outcomes of the project.

62. A second concern is that the long-term financial viability of the project activities relies on mechanisms for financing beyond the initial phase, including contributions from the private sector. The ITAP acknowledges the analysis included in annex 2C 'Potential Leveraging of Private and Public Sector Resources'. However, it notes that securing these resources remains challenging to secure the private sector's participation in time for the end of the 7-year implementation phase and for the 20-year lifespan of the project.

63. In view of the above, the ITAP assesses the efficiency and effectiveness of the project as medium.


## Overall remarks from the independent Technical Advisory Panel

64. The ITAP wishes to underscore the completeness, clarity and high quality of the documents included in this funding proposal. Furthermore, the ITAP considers that the methodology described for the identification of high conservation value (HCV) /high carbon standard (HCS) areas is commendable as it is essential to enhance transparency and clarity regarding adaptation outcomes and can be used in other funding proposals in the future.

65. The ITAP makes the following recommendations to the AE:

(a) Prepare, in a participative manner, a prioritization strategy for the high conservation value (HCV)/high carbon standard (HCS) areas considering potential trade-offs between conservation and development objectives;

(b) Prepare guidelines for designing context-specific climate-resilient agroforestry systems, including necessary methodologies and design tools, and make these available;

(c) Seek opportunities to include additional monitoring and evaluation of project products from the project areas to meet national and international market requirements;

(d) Identify sources to secure the budget for establishing and utilizing additional monitoring capacities.

(e) The EFA states that by the end of the third year of implementation, the project will finalize the transformation to sustainable agriculture and sustainable forest management. The ITAP recommends that the AE should prepare a detailed work plan with recommendations either to finalize the transition (if not yet completed) and/or to identify additional resources for the period until full transformation was completed, to accelerate progress in regions that started later;

(f) Consider whether the mitigation potential impact may have been overestimated, owing to the fact that the project is still at an early stage of implementation.

(g) During the second half of the implementation phase, and taking into consideration the results achieved so far, prepare a strategy to facilitate the participation of the private sector beyond the project implementation phase and initiate negotiations with relevant actors;

(h) The ITAP recommends that the Board approve this funding proposal with the following conditions:

(i) Conditions precedent to the second disbursement of the GCF Proceeds:

1. Introduction

### 1.1 Overview of the project

Gender issues are often ignored in designing development programs, including land-based mitigation and adaptation, even though women and men often get different impacts. Therefore, the use of a gender assessment (GA) is recommended to ensure that development programs are fair and equal benefits for women, men, and other marginal groups. Therefore, a Gender Assessment (GA) was conducted in five West Kalimantan districts: Kepulauan Hulu, Sintang, Sanggau, Ketapang, and Kubu Raya.

The GA aims to assess the gender impacts of land-based mitigation and adaptation through a jurisdictional approach for men and women on the ground. The GA is being conducted in five West Kalimantan districts: Kepulauan Hulu, Sintang, Kabupaten Sanggau, Kabupaten Ketapang and dan Kabupaten Kubu Raya.

### 1.2 Objectives and structure of the Gender Assessment

The objective is to conduct a GA which can be used to develop the GAP for the project “Land-based Mitigation and Adaptation in Five West Kalimantan”.

Chapter 2 examines gender equality in Indonesia and the project’s target regions (Kepulauan Hulu, Sintang, Sanggau, Ketapang, Kubu Raya). In the gender analysis, we chose two Kubu Raya sites to represent the lowland forest type and the dominant Dayak community. Chapter 3 elaborates on the gender mainstreaming approaches of the funding proposal (FP) including the FP’s stakeholder engagement plan (SEP, Annex 7a), and the feasibility study (FS, Annex 2).

This GA serves as a basis for the GAP and informs gender markers, gender indicators, and gender mainstreaming approaches of the funding proposal (FP) including the FP’s stakeholder engagement plan (SEP, Annex 7a), and the feasibility study (FS, Annex 2).

### 1.3 Methodology

#### 1.3.1 Research Location

The project location covers five West Kalimantan districts: Kepulauan Hulu, Sintang, Sanggau, Ketapang, and Kubu Raya. However, due to limited access and for the sake of resource efficiency, the location of this research is focused on two representative sample districts, namely Kubu Raya District (KR) as a representative district with a dominant Dayak community and a dominant Malay community. In contrast, the Sintang District was chosen as a representative area with a lowland forest type and the Dayak community as the dominant.

#### 1.3.2 Data Sources

The data sources for this research is primary data and secondary data. Primary data are obtained in three ways:

a. In-depth interviews with key informants, especially key informants at the village and district level.
b. Focus group discussion (FGD) with key informants at the district and provincial level.
c. Questionnaires were distributed to selected respondents at both the village and district levels.

Secondary data are obtained through documentation, such as regulations related to gender mainstreaming and land-based mitigation and adaptation.

#### 1.3.3 Key Informants

At the District Level, the informants are village officials involved in the Land-based mitigation and adaptation. Community Leaders (namely Lembaga Pengelolaan Hutan Desa (LPHD), Kelompok Usaha Perhutanan Sosial (KUPS), Kelompok Wanita Tani (KWIT), Kepala Desa,

At the district level, research informants were the participants of focus group discussions (FGD) with stakeholders from the project region (Regional Development Planning Agency (namely Bappeda), Department of Agriculture (namely Dinas Pertanian), Department of Forestry (namely Dinas Perdayaan Perumahan dan Pertumbuhan Alam), Department of the Environment (namely Dinas Lingkungan Hidup), and National Council for Women's Empowerment and Child Protection (namely Dewan Pemberdayaan Perempuan dan Perlindungan Anak), Regional Parliament (Dewan Perwakilan Rakyat Daerah (DPRD)), Non-Government Organization (NGO) Lolompok Wanita Tani (LWTT)).

In addition to informants participating in stakeholder consultations at the district level, the researchers also conducted key informant interviews with 156 male and 84 female respondents. The highest number of female informants was coming from representatives of Female Figures from higher-level government agencies such as the Ministry of Environment and Forestry (namely Kementerian Lingkungan Hidup), the National Regional Councils (the name is Dekra-Delegasi), the Regional Natural Resources Council (the name is Dekra-Dekadeg op), the Regional National Crafts Council (the name is Dekra-Dekar), Bio-Industry Assistant Bank, and others.

Apart from the aforementioned informants, the researchers also asked several key informants to complete the questionnaire. They were not part of the stakeholder consultation forums but came from various government agencies at both district and provincial levels in the region, especially as a gender mainstreaming working group and Forest Stakeholder Unit (KUTA) during the period 2019-2023.

At the national level, the informants came from the Ministry of Environment and Forestry (KLHK), the Ministry of Women's Empowerment and Child Protection (KP3PA), and others involved in land-based mitigation and adaptation through a jurisdictional approach.

1.3.4 Data Analysis

The data analysis used the Harvard model of gender analysis. The Harvard gender analysis model is based on the question "who does what" according to gender and "who has access and control" over resources.

2. Information on gender dimensions in the country or the region

2.1 Meta level (norms)

2.1.1 The Gender Gap in Indonesia

Development in Indonesia has succeeded in increasing justice and gender equality in various areas of society. However, in 2023, Indonesia's gender equality at the global level (Gender Gap Index) is ranked 78th out of 146 countries. When compared with ASEAN countries, Indonesia is in seventh position out of 10 countries.

The GGGI consists of four sub-indexes: Participation and Opportunity in the Economic Sector; Educational Attainment; Health and Survival; and Political Empowerment. In general, Indonesia's ranking compared to 156 countries in the world on the GGGI sub-indexes is as follows: 78th rank for participation and opportunity in the economic sector, 92nd rank for educational attainment, 107th on health and survival, and ranks 134th on political empowerment.

# Indonesia's Gender Development Index: Year 2021-2022

| Year | GDI | Life expectancy (in years) | Expected years of schooling (in years) | Average years of school (in years) | Per capita expenditure (in min. Rp.) / IDR |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 2021 | 91.67 | 69.73 | 12.95 | 13.22 | 8.92 | 8.17 | 15.770 | 9,050 |
| 2022 | 91.63 | 69.93 | 12.93 | 12.96 | 8.59 | 8.39 | 16.215 | 9,281 |

Source: KPPPA, 2022

Apart from using the Global Gender Gap Index indicator, gender justice and gender equality can also be explained based on the Gender Development Index (GDI). The GDI is an index that measures the differences between men and women across three aspects; its expectancy, education, and economic activity. The higher the GDI score, the better the situation of women in a year. Hence, it indicates the ratio between women's Human Development Index (HD) and men's HD.

The data depicted in Table 3 which is based on the KPPPA Report 2023. Busters a positive development in Indonesia's GDI, which increased from 91.27% to 91.63% in 2022. Gender equality in Indonesia has improved significantly since 2021 and 2022.

Gender equality in Indonesia is still low compared to other countries. Women's life expectancy was lower than men in 2021 and 2022, the average duration of schooling shows that on average women have less education than men. The average annual expenditure for women is also consistently much higher than women's 2021 and 2022 (in 2022: Rp. 16,215 million vs. Rp. 9,050 in 2021). This means that the average annual expenditure of women is only 57.24% of that of men.

## Women in Politics

Women's representation in politics can be seen in the Gender Empowerment Measure (GEM) dimension. GEM is an index that shows the active role of women in economic and political life, which is measured in three dimensions: women's representation in parliament, decision-making, and employment.

Indonesia's Gender Empowerment Measure (GEM) in 2021 was 75.57. The constituent components of GEM include:

* Women's involvement in parliament at 21.9%;
* Women as professionals at 48.6% (decreased from 53.1% in 2020);
* Women as political party members at 20.1% (increased from 17.9% in 2020).

Meanwhile, for 2022, Indonesia's GEM increased to 76.26 with the following components:

* Women's involvement in parliament at 34.5%;
* Women as professionals at 48.9% (increase from 2021);
* Women's income contribution of 37.2% (decrease from 40.1% in 2020).

The increase in women's representation in parliament and the decrease in women's income contribution increased in 2022, more is needed to guarantee that women can actively contribute to parliamentary activities. The number of women members of the People's Representative Council (DPR) of Indonesia for the 2014-2019 period, which decreased from 101 seats in 2013 to 85 seats in 2019 and increased again to 106 seats in 2020. In addition, there are still only 750 elected members. Women's participation in politics is fully guaranteed by Law Number 2 of 2008 concerning Political Parties, which states that the formation and management of political parties must be carried out with a minimum of 30% women representation.

The role of women through political parties, namely women as members of political parties, women as party leaders, and women in legislative members. The role of women does not go


---
**Page 119**
---

through political parties; namely, women can be government officials (bureaucrats), members of the Regional Representative Council (namely Dewan Perwakilan Daerah DPD), and members of the People's Consultative Assembly (namely Dewan Perwakilan Rakyat DPR).

On the Global Gender Index, Indonesia’s low score is burdened by the shallow index of women’s empowerment in the political field, which is 0.169 in 2022, below the global average. Then, Indonesia has a high score for women's education and health opportunities, as well as economic opportunities. Meanwhile, in the education and health sectors, Indonesia received a high score of at least 0.75.

Factors causing the not optimal participation of women in politics include:

1. Human Factors
   * Lack of awareness of women being active and involved in political activities.
   * Women are less conditioned to be involved in politics because they are used to being placed in domestic activities.
   * A sense of responsibility to the family so that they are reluctant to participate in politics and leave their family.

2. Social-Cultural Factors
   * People’s views are influenced by patriarchal culture, placing men in positions of leaders and decision-makers with support positions, which results in positions that determine public policy in political institutions and governments are dominated by men.
   * Women's entry into the world of politics stems from a normative division between the public sphere and private sphere.
   * Patriarchal social and cultural values that exist in society.
   * There is a perception that politics is dirty.

3. Institutional Factors
   * Participation and voter education have not been given optimally, especially in political parties; and strengthening of women’s political skills;
   * Limited training for women in the public sector task gender awareness and technical capacity related to gender (Wahyudi, 2020).

### Mobility, discrimination, and wage gap in the workforce

In Indonesia, women's roles are determined by formal and informal institutions and are exerted through various dimensions of life. Women’s participation is limited in terms of realizing their rights and opportunities in various dimensions, including education, health, and finances. In the 2022 Global Gender Gap report, it is stated that although Indonesia has achieved significant progress in women's empowerment (the gender gap index score increased by 15% compared to most other countries) experiencing a decline in labor force participation: women leaving the labor market at an older age than men (average age of retirement for men is around 60 years and women are female participation rate of 56% (aged 15-64 years), this still impacts about 7.3 million people). The gender gap in earnings is also significant, although men's scores fell disproportionately, increasing equality. In addition, the number of women working in the informal sector has increased significantly compared to scheduled workers in legislative, senior, and managerial positions have also increased from 29.7% to 32.4% (Global Gender Gap Report, 2022).

# ILO (2020)

ILO stated that based on the main findings of the Indonesia brief, it recognizes that women in Indonesia are outperforming men in educational attainment and broadening their skills in STEM fields. However, despite this progress, women remain underrepresented in the labor force.

Indonesia is the largest economy in Southeast Asia, characterized by high economic growth driven by exports and domestic consumption. Women make up 52 percent of the population and 33 percent of wage employment, respectively. The labor force numbers nearly 134 million people, with women representing about 60 percent of this number.

Women's employment has increased from 57 to 78 percent since 1991–2018. Women's employment as a share of the total workforce is now at its highest level in Indonesia (78%), compared to just over 75% for working-age men. Between 1991 and 2018, women’s employment as a share of the total workforce increased from 43% to 60%. However, women's career development is affected by gender-based attitudes, which impact recruitment and promotion, work assignments, training, and mobility.

Promoting gender diversity in the workplace is increasingly seen as an important strategy for companies. Companies that diversify as part of their core strategy; to increase productivity and profits, retain talent, create a more inclusive culture, and attract new customers. The benefits of having women in growing industries will lead them to success in business and innovation.

Indonesia is facing challenges related to the transition from the pandemic. In 2020, the government implemented measures to reduce the risk of transmission, including locking down parts of affected areas, restricting travel, and banning mass gatherings. These measures have reduced economic activity and led to a decline in consumer spending.

As a result, economic growth declined, especially in critical sectors, including tourism, manufacturing, and construction. The pandemic has also had an impact on women's employment. Women are more widely represented in temporary or part-time jobs and are more likely to be employed in the informal sector than men. This is because many workers in the service sector, which has been hit hard by restrictions to curb the spread of the virus.

Women have also faced challenges balancing work responsibilities with family life during this time. Work-from-home arrangements have become more common as a result of the pandemic, but they can exacerbate women's double burden in balancing family and work responsibilities.

Creating a gender-inclusive corporate culture and increasing business competitiveness requires eliminating the gender bias within the economy. The gender bias between women and men is influenced by social norms, stereotypes, and expectations about gender roles from their families, schools, and society. The gender bias influences women’s access to education, employment opportunities, and pay.

However, human resources systems and advocacy messages from senior and top management can help foster a gender-inclusive corporate culture.

The results of a survey of companies in Indonesia found that most of these companies agree that gender diversity is important for their business success. The majority of Indonesian reporting business results, 66 percent reported increased productivity and profitability as a result of having more women on the board or in senior management positions. Increased ability to attract and retain talent, 53 percent said their company's reputation had improved, and 46 percent reported better ability to gauge consumer interest and demand.

Having gender diversity at all levels is important for companies with diverse workforces, and a female chief executive officer (CEO) are positively associated with achieving better business results. Companies that report having a strong commitment to diversity and inclusion policies are 14% more likely to achieve better business results.

Of Indonesian companies, 50 percent said their culture was male-dominated, 34 percent said it was inclusive, and 15 percent said it was female-dominated. Analysis of the survey by gender of the respondents found that women were more likely than men to say that their company culture is inclusive, compared to just 26% of women surveyed. Most men (57 percent) agreed with a male-dominated part of women's (30 percent) report a male-dominated corporate culture (ILO, 2020).


---
**Page 121**
---

2.1.4 Gender-based Violence and SEAH in Indonesia

Building public legal awareness and being aware of Domestic Violence, which is considered taboo by many people, is a key force in eradicating domestic violence and social control efforts in eradicating domestic violence.

Violence against women causes suffering and misery for victims and their families and burdens communities. The pattern of violence that is most significant in Indonesia is violence between people known to each other. Violence against women is a social construction based on community consensus, which is rooted in cultural traditions. Domestic violence is like an iceberg phenomenon, where the number of reported cases is not proportional to its actual prevalence. In Indonesia, there are significant differences in gender roles and status related in cultural traditions. The pattern of violence that is most significant from year to year is violence which is divided into three domains, namely family or personal relationships, community or public life, and work.

Several single factors that cause violence against women are socio-cultural factors due to unequal power relations between men and women. Gender inequality can occur in several forms such as the role of men being more dominant than women in making family decisions, social life, and work matters. Gender inequality can also be caused by the lack of education for women which makes them less able to have a strong position. Men are characterized by aggressiveness and independence, while women are characterized by passivity and dependence on others. This gender role is often reinforced through habits that are manifested in the form of everyday life: if men are strong, women are weak. If men have to be dominant, then women must be submissive. These gender roles and stereotypes give rise to the actions of male authority and leads to attempts to dominate women. In their creation, women are often seen as inferior and less capable than men (Aminan, 2021; Almawati, 2021; Zamarhati, 2021; Angus, 2021). In the public sector, there is often exploitation of women. Meanwhile, men often get privileges (such as having the right to be leaders) (Aminan, 2021; Israpit, 2021).

Sexual exploitation, abuse, and harassment (SEAH) can occur in any project or workplace, regardless of whether it is a public or private sector. For example, sexual harassment, exploita- tion and harassment exist in the context of project-supported training and capacity building activities for women farmers. In addition to that, SEAH also occurs in informal relationships with small holder farmers and contacts between service provider staff and mem- bers of the community. The problem is not only limited to the private sector but also in implement- ing social forestery (see also Gender Assessment in Annex 8a). The project is not expected to exacerbate or worsen SEAH, however it is important to outline targeted measures to avoid SEAH for all staff and beneficiaries of the project. This includes developing a protocol and reporting on SEAH risk mitigation measures and any incidences/ grievances. Examples of good practices include:

• Developing a survivor-centered GRM protocol
• Ensuring consistent monitoring and reporting on SEAH incidences/ grievances
• Ensuring Project staff is assigned to oversee the implementation of SEAH risk mitigation measures
• Develop a code of conduct for all staff, with zero tolerance for SEAH
• Train staff on the code of conduct, SEAH and other important considerations for GESI and social safeguards
• Raise awareness of beneficiaries on SEAH and GRM protocol

For more detailed information on the avoidance/ risk mitigation measures and monitoring framework, refer to the Environmental and Social Impact Assessment (Annex 6a), Environmental and Social Management Plan (Annex 6b), and Gender Action Plan (Annex 8a).

**Page 122**

2.2 Macro level (policies and laws)

2.2.1 International Commitments

Gender mainstreaming commitments have been voiced globally through the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination Against Women (CEDAW), the Beijing Platform for Action (BPA) adopted in 1995, and the Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs). CEDAW is a treaty that sets out international human rights principles of equal rights between men and women in all areas of life. Meanwhile, the Beijing Platform for Action is an agenda for action to promote gender equality and women's empowerment through active participation in all public and private life areas in economic, social, cultural, and political decision-making processes at all levels. The Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs) are a set of 17 goals which is to achieve gender equality and empower all women and girls. The aim at this point is to end discrimination against women and ensure that they have equal opportunities in all aspects of life.

2.2.2 National Regulations

The Indonesian government has issued regulations related to gender integration in national development, starting with the adoption of the CEDAW which emphasized the principles of women's human rights, norms and standards of obligation, and the responsibility of the state in advancing gender equality. The government also enacted the Law Number 7 of 1984 on Gender Mainstreaming (PUG) through Resolution Number 53/2015 concerning guidelines for implementing gender mainstreaming in the development of West Kalimantan Province. This law requires all public and private sectors to integrate gender equality into their respective scopes of work.

The Indonesian government has also issued several other regulations related to gender integration, including:

- Presidential Instruction Number 8 of 2000 concerning Gender Mainstreaming in National Development
- Presidential Instruction Number 15/2003 on the Implementation of Gender Mainstreaming in National Development
- Presidential Instruction Number 9 of 2004 concerning Gender Mainstreaming in National Development

Integrating gender in development programs, among others, aims to realize gen- der equality and empowerment for women. This is done by promoting a participatory, decentralized government, sustainable development, and participatory community empowerment.

To accelerate gender mainstreaming in national development, the Indonesian government issued a Presidential Instruction Number 15/2003 on the Implementation of Gender Mainstreaming (PUG) through Gender Responsive Planning and Budgeting (PRPB). The four pillars of PRPB are: Strategic Plan for National Development (RPJMN), Medium-Term Development Plan (RTPK), Regional Development Plan (RTRW), and Local Development Plan (RTRW). The Ministry of Home Affairs, the Ministry of Finance, the Ministry of Home Affairs, and the Ministry of Home Affairs have been involved in this process.

Gender mainstreaming has been included in the national development plan through Presidential Decree Number 10/2015 on the National Medium-Term Development Plan (RPJMN) for 2020-2024, which is carried out by accelerating the implementation of gender mainstreaming. The government also issued a Presidential Instruction Number 9/2004 concerning Gender Mainstreaming in National Development, which aims to increase the role and quality of life of women in various development fields.

The Presidential Instruction Number 8 of 2000 encourages every Ministry, Governor/regent/mayor to issue regulations related to gender mainstreaming. The government has also issued several other regulations related to gender integration:

- Presidential Instruction Number 15/2003 on the Implementation of Gender Mainstreaming in National Development
- Presidential Instruction Number 9 of 2004 concerning Gender Mainstreaming in National Development

At the provincial level, gender mainstreaming in West Kalimantan has been included in four policies:

1. West Kalimantan Provincial Medium Term Development Plan for 2018-2023.
2. Governor of West Kalimantan Regulation Number 53/2015 concerning guidelines for implementing gender mainstreaming in the development of West Kalimantan Province.
3. Government of West Kalimantan Regulation Number 94/2016 on the Implementation of Gender Mainstreaming in the Development of West Kalimantan Province.
4. Government of West Kalimantan Regulation Number 58/2017 concerning guidelines for implementing gender mainstreaming in the development of West Kalimantan Province and its districts.

4. Regulation of the Governor of West Kalimantan Number 61 of 2019 concerning Regional Action Plans for West Kalimantan Province SDGs for 2019-2023.

Measures and policies related to mitigation and adaptation in West Kalimantan are manifested in six policies:

* **Regional Regulation of West Kalimantan Province Number 7 of 2018 concerning Planning for the Reduction of Greenhouse Gas Emissions in West Kalimantan Province.**
* **Governor of West Kalimantan Regulation Number 125 of 2020 concerning Regional Action Plans for Reducing Greenhouse Gas Emissions in West Kalimantan Province for 2020-2030.**
* **Decree of the Governor of West Kalimantan Number 1215/DLK/HK/2020 concerning Tariffs and Fees for Compensation Payments from Deforestation and Forest Degradation in West Kalimantan for 2020-2030.**

4. Regulation of the Governor of West Kalimantan Number 201 of 2021 concerning Guidelines for the Implementation of Community-Based Monitoring Mechanisms and Mechanisms for Data Exchange for Reducing Emission from Deforestation and Forest Degradation (REDD+ Activities).

Regional Regulation of West Kalimantan Province Number 8 of 2021 concerning the Protection and Management of Peat and Mangrove Ecosystems; and

Decree of the Governor of West Kalimantan Number 345/DLK/HK/2020 concerning the Formation of a Working Group on Reducing Emissions from Deforestation and Forest Degradation Plus (REDD+) in West Kalimantan Province.

Unintentionally, some policies on mitigation and adaptation do not specifically integrate a gender perspective.

The following gender mainstreamed policies exist in the project areas:

* Kapuas Hulu District does not yet have regulations related to gender mainstreaming.
* Berau Regency has four gender mainstreaming regulations, namely Singkawang Singkawang District Head Regulation Number 9 of 2013, concerning the Integrated Service Center for the Empowerment of Women and Children in Singkawang.
* Sanggau Regency has five gender mainstreaming regulations, namely a) Sanggau Regency Government's Medium Term Development Plan for 2019-2024; b) and c) Sanggau Regency Regulation Number 28 of 2018 concerning Technical Guidelines for the Preparation of Gender Data Information Systems.
* Ketapang Regency has one gender-related regulation, namely the Ketapang Regency Government's Medium Term Development Plan for 2019-2024.

Kubu Raya Regency is the district that has the most gender mainstreaming regulations, namely four regulations, including: a) Kubu Raya Regency Regional Long-Term Development Plan for 2018-2035; b) Sanggau Regency Regulation Number 28 of 2018 concerning Implementation of Gender Mainstreaming; c) Regulation of the Kubu Raya Regency Number 104 of 2019 concerning the Regional Action Plan for Reducing Greenhouse Gas Emissions in Kubu Raya Regency; and d) Sanggau Regency Regulation Number 50 of 2021 concerning Synchronized and Integrated Gender Data Information Systems.

For mitigation and adaptation policies, five project locations have six policies, including:

* Kapuas Hulu Regency has one regulation, namely Kapuas Hulu Regency Regulation Number 87 of 2021, concerning the Development of the Embau Green Metropolitan Area in Kapuas Hulu Regency.

# Conclusions for the Project

* **Indonesia:** Gender equality at global level has only achieved a GGGI score of 0.697, ranking it 8<sup>th</sup> globally and 7<sup>th</sup> among ASEAN countries. Despite some significant progress, the gender gap persists. Gender norms severely limit women from exploiting opportunities in education, employment, and decision-making processes, which hinders development.

* **Gender-based violence (GBV):** is a significant problem that is rooted in socio-cultural factors and the solid patriarchal structures. Building awareness can be a driving factor to eliminate GBV, including domestic violence.

* **Gender mainstreaming:** has not been fully implemented at international, national and provincial (West Kalimantan) level. The Indonesian government makes efforts to accelerate gender mainstreaming through gender responsive planning and budgeting (PRB). However, it is still in the initial stage of implementation for mitigation and adaptation to climate change at national and regional level. Likewise, it has also not been included in policies, programs or activities in the environmental and forestry sector at the DLHK because it has not been considered a priority.
