3. Gender dimensions in the sector

### 3.1 Meta level

#### 3.1.1 Women’s and Men’s Division of Labor and Access to and Control over Resources

Women and men perform different part incagriculture and plantation activities. Women perform work such as preparing land, preparing irrigation, and harvesting. Meanwhile, women perform work such as weeding, planting, and harvesting. Meanwhile, men perform work such as collecting for profit. However, regarding decision-making in agriculture and plantation work, it is done by men (Represented by representatives of the West Kalimantan Women Farmers’ Groups & Makmur Women Farmers Group in Sintang, 23 May 2023).

Even though women are allowed to participate in agriculture and plantation activities, irregularities remain. The division of labor between men and women is still influenced by people with higher education and those who are financially stable. Meanwhile, women are expected to take care of the household and children (questionnaire by respondents at the district level). Accordingly, the roles taken in activities outside the household still need to be improved and expanded. Even though the role of women has been increased after the implementation of gender-based violence has been opened, control is still in the hands of men. Reluctance to get involved due to ignorance is a barrier for women’s participation (questionnaire by respondents at the district level).

Since women are expected to fulfill additional tasks in the household, they are impeded from crossing the threshold and participating in activities outside the household such as preparing food, managing finances. Meanwhile, men play a role in land preparation and cultivation. In some cases, women are not allowed to participate because of their heavy workload (questionnaire by respondents at the district level).

#### 3.1.2 Potential Barriers to Women’s Participation

Other obstacles that prevent women from developing their potential include: 1) women are reluctant to be involved in activities that do not provide certain additional income; 2) they do not have access to land and other resources (questionnaire by respondents at the district level). There is no division of roles in managing domestic affairs between women and men who are already married. It is often done by women only (often given). 3) the lack of women’s awareness with the market that exists in society (questionnaire by respondents at the district level). 4) value chains, which exist in society that women cannot do heavy work and cannot work alone; 5) lack of specific assistance for programs or activities for women’s groups. (Represented by representatives of Plantation Companies at the Sintang District Women Farmers’ Group, 23 May 2023). 6) there is no specific assistance for programs or activities for women’s groups (Source: 1 to 8 abstracted from West Kalimantan Regional Regulation No. 3/2015 concerning implementing the Program of Gender Equality in Agriculture and Plantation Sector, 2015). 7) Catholic women’s groups were facilitated to form groups for activities in agriculture but, due to lack of land, they are unable to participate (Represented by representatives of the garden without harvesting results of interviews with women and village leaders in Kubi Rayas, May 2023).

#### 3.1.3 Prevent gender-based violence and discrimination

West Kalimantan already has Regional Regulation No. 3/2015 concerning implementing the Program of Gender Equality in Agriculture and Plantation Sector. However, accurate and updated data on violence against women in West Kalimantan is still challenging to obtain. However, that does not mean any violence against women in West Kalimantan. The media mass covers several severe cases, which often end in fatalities. Data on case records at the Commission


---
**Page 126**
---

for the Protection of Women and Children in 2019 shows 1,300 violence cases against women. Most of the data was obtained from data on cases or issues handled by 359 Religious Courts at the district level.

One of the villages in Kibu Raya District already has a Village Regulation related to Sexual Violence. There have been seven cases related to sexual violence reported to the team or task force that monitors the implementation of the regulation, and all were successfully resolved by the Court. Encouragement for women who are victims of sexual violence and members of the public—whether they are men or women—to report such cases is one of the challenges faced in this process, one of which is by picking up the ball if the team knows that a member of the public has been affected by sexual violence. The team has also received reports from some villages about victims and witnesses reporting because there was a fee being collected, even though there was no charge at the time of the incident. This is an indication that the community still needs to be sensitized in addition. In addition, the feeling of shame and worry about the views of the surrounding community may affect women who are victims of sexual violence. (results: interviews with representatives of community leaders and LPHD Kibu Raya, 20 May 2023).

Preventing and dealing with gender-based violence and discrimination that occurs in villages can be done by:

- Increasing the sensitivity of stakeholders, including project implementers;
- Making SOPs with a gender perspective;
- Demonstrating the importance of equality and its impact on people's lives;
- Involving young people from villages that are pilot projects to participate in campaigning for justice and gender equality (source: results of questionnaires at the district level, 2023);
- Carrying out actions or methods of following up with victims and witnesses related to allegations of gender-based violence and discrimination (source: results of interviews with representatives of community leaders and LPHD Kibu Raya, 20 May 2023).

### 3.1.4 Vulnerabilities faced by women and men in the context of climate change impacts

There is inequality of access to or opportunities for knowledge entering the village. This inequality reduces the speed of women's adaptation to climate change. Opportunities to obtain different information from women and men make vulnerability visible when implementing mitigation actions. Women are more vulnerable than men because they have less access to resources in the site area, where there are fewer and fewer sources of livelihood from nature. Women return to their homes after working on the farm or in the forest, and this is a problem for them as they are unable to manage the household due to drought. Women's vulnerability increases when they are forced by flooding due to mangrove logging activities. Women in vulnerable communities are usually only initially involved in decision-making processes related to climate change adaptation issues or participating in decision-making. Women will only express their opinions if asked (Source: results of interviews with representatives of community leaders and LPHD Kibu Raya, 20 May 2023).

Global climate efforts are gradually shifting towards a more balanced approach to adaptation and mitigation. Gender-responsive actions are recognized as necessary in the climate change agenda, and gender equality is considered an important element for sustainable development. The keys to the success of all multilateral agendas, including the 2030 agenda and all future climate change agreements, lie in the ability to address the complex interplay between demographic, geographical, cultural, political, and environmental factors. Therefore, different groups have different needs and priorities that are influenced by their age, sex, social status, health, wealth/poverty, and other sociocultural characteristics.

Climate change disproportionately affects vulnerable groups, especially women in rural communities. Women's vulnerability is often a result of gender inequalities within the family or community, and household decision-making processes that affect their lives. Such economic and social inequalities mean that women have fewer assets and fewer means to deal with the


---
**Page 127**
---

negative impacts of climate change. The tendency of women and girls to depend more on natural resources for their livelihoods also increases their vulnerability. Cultural norms regarding gender roles in the household structure are also a major factor in limiting women's access to resources. Women's individual and collective knowledge and experiences in natural resource management and environmental conservation at the household and community level equip them with unique skills beneficial for adaptation.

### 3.1.5 Vulnerability faced by Dayak women in the context of climate change

The position and role of Dayak women within the social structure is in the domestic sector. Apart from caring for their families, they also have to collect firewood, water, and food, preparing food for their husbands and children and then leaving for the forest and fields to work for a living or to gather wild plants and animals. In addition, they are responsible for collecting wood for cooking as well as looking for wooden branches to bring home as material for making a fire for cooking. For Dayak women in the context of climate change, this means that their vulnerability is increased because, in most cases, women do not have a voice in decision-making in the household. Women are not allowed to argue against their husbands' decisions and are often excluded from discussions within the Dayak tribe is still thick with a patriarchal system. The legitimacy of the Benawan Dayak community has yet to place women in an equal position with men in policy making; this has been proven in the last election where women were not elected as representatives.

The role of women in decision-making in the Dayak community are only seen in the construction of roles and status in the household, community, and village. Women's participation is also limited to their ability to access resources for development in West Kalimantan. Dayak women are very vulnerable to being disproportionately affected by climate change because they have less access to information about climate change, re- sources, and opportunities. Women’s workforce still needs to be belittled and paid low wages, including the lack of access to credit facilities and other financial services, which means that women's interests are often underrepresented in public spaces (Bowman, 2022; Niko, 2020).

### 3.2 Macro level (policies and laws in sectors)

Through the Ministerial regulation, the Ministry of Environment and Forestry No. 31 of 2017 on gender mainstreaming, the institution is abode with the national regulations on gender main- streaming. The National Action Plan for Gender Mainstreaming in Forest Sector for the period 2020–2024 has been established. All thirteen of its echelons have had a gender focal point under one person. In addition to this, the Ministry of Forestry and Environment also has regulations related to Gender Mainstreaming and integrating them into the regional me- dium-term development plan (RPJMD) and medium-term development plan (RKP). The inte- gration of gender in the environmental and forestry fields. As a result, the implementation of Regulation No. P-MENHUT-II/21/2017 concerning Guidelines for Gender Responsive Planning and Budgeting in Forestry Development and Ministry of Forestry Regulation Number P-MENHUT-II/23/2017 concerning Guidelines for Gender Responsive Planning and ranks of work units within the Ministry of Forestry in preparing gender-responsive planning and budgeting. The regulation was issued by the Minister of Environment and Forestry of Indonesia in 2014, In 2017, a Regulation of the Minister of Environment and Forestry of the Republic of Indonesia No. P-MENHUT-II/35/2017 concerning Guidelines for Gender Mainstream- ingation Guideline was issued Gender Mainstreaming in the Environment and Forestry Sector. This regulation was used to enhance more effective and optimal gender mainstreaming


---
**Page 128**
---

efforts in every planning, formulation, implementation, monitoring, and evaluation of policies, programs, and activities in the environmental and forest sector.

The implementation of gender mainstreaming of PUGs is an integral part of all activities—work units within the scope of the Ministry of Environment and Forestry. In addition, this regulation is a refinement of the two previous regulations mentioned above.

In addition to the Roadmap for Gender Mainstreaming in the Ministry of Environment and Forestry has created a gender mainstreaming roadmap so that the implementation of gender mainstreaming in the Ministry of Environment and Forestry can be accelerated. The Ministry of Environment and Forestry's Gender Mainstreaming Roadmap for 2020-2024 has the following objectives:

• Maximizing the impact of PUG implementation at the Ministry of Environment and Forestry.
• Identify strategic gender issues in order to accelerate PUG implementation.
• Arranging stages of PUG implementation at the Ministry of Environment and Forestry.

Meanwhile, the regulations of the Ministry of Environment and Forest related to climate change include:

1. Regulation of the Minister of Environment and Forest of the Republic of Indonesia Number 38/2020 concerning the Establishment of a Climate Change Fund (Koartha) which aims to standardize norms, criteria, and guidelines for planning, organizing, operating, monitoring, evaluating, and reporting on forest and land fire control activities at the Koartha's (stands for Forest and Land Fire Control) businesses/activities/actions for related parties, so that the effectiveness and efficiency of forest and land fire control is guaranteed.

2. Decree of the Director General of Climate Change Control Number 38/PJ/2021 concerning Guidelines for Implementing the National Action Plan on Climate Change for 2020-2024, which aims to provide guidelines regarding the implementation of climate change activities in forest and land fire control activities can run effectively and efficiently; consistent and sustainable.

3. Regulation of the Director General of Climate Change Control concerning the Organization and Work Area of Mangga Agra, which aims to provide guidelines regarding the organization and work area of Mangga Agra so that forest and land fire control activities can run effectively and efficiently; and

4. Regulation of the Minister of Environment and Forest Number P.APR/UP/PKI.6.3/2021 concerning Guidelines for Implementing the Climate Village Program, which aims to serve as directives and guidelines in implementing the climate village program.

This data shows that the Legal Foundation for promoting justice and gender equality through the General Law Number 4 of 2009 concerning Forests has been implemented by the Ministry of Environment and Forestry received the Paritai Ekopraga Award in two consecutive periods in the highest category. At the same time, at the level of the Director General of Climate Change Control, it was stated that gender was not yet the main performance indicator for the department during the implementation of policies or programs related to forestry. Therefore, the Green Climate Fund (GCF) project needs to be integrated into the existing regulations in the forest sector in the regions in accordance with existing regulations at the national level. In addition, it is necessary to prepare the preparation of a Roadmap for Gender Mainstreaming in the field of environment and forests in Indonesia (Direktorat Jenderal Hidup dan Kehutanan) in integrating a gender perspective in policies, programs or activities with relevant stakeholders.

The ministry responsible for gender in Indonesia is the Ministry of Women's Empowerment and Child Protection. Under the mandate of Presidential Instruction Number II of 2000 con-


| 15 |


---
**Page 129**
---

### Gender Mainstreaming in National Development

This ministry must encourage all ministries in Indonesia (including regional heads at the provincial and district/municipality levels) according to their respective functions and responsibilities.

To ensure the implementation of gender mainstreaming in Indonesia, the Gender Mainstreaming Director Institute (Dinas Pemberdayaan Perempuan dan Perlindungan Anak), under the Ministry of Women's Empowerment and Child Protection, together with the National Development Planning Agency (Bappenas), the Ministry of Finance, and the Ministry of Home Affairs, established a task force for Accelerating Gender Mainstreaming through Planning and Gender responsive budgeting (henceforth PUG) in 2013.

Each Ministry/Agency report must implement PUG according to their authority. In the environment and forestry sector, the Ministry of Environment and Forestry is responsible for integrating gender into its policies and programs. The ministry has developed a guideline on gender mainstreaming in the forest sector based on the Ministerial Regulation of the Ministry of Environment and Forestry of the Republic of Indonesia in 2017 concerning Guidelines for Gender Mainstreaming in Forest Sector Policies and Programs.

This institution was very innovative and finally received the highest award in implementing Gender Mainstreaming, namely the Paritasi Bie Praya awarding the highest category, namely Mentor International Award.

#### 1. GIZ FORCLIME (Forest and Climate Change Program)

The program aims to reduce greenhouse gas emissions from the forest sector while improving the livelihoods of Indonesian rural communities. In order to achieve this goal, the program team will support the Indonesian Government in the design and implementation of a national forest carbon accounting system (FCCS) aimed at the conservation and sustainable management of forests at all local, provincial, and national levels.

#### 2. CIFT (Combatting Illegal Wildlife Trade)

The project is to help the Government reduce the volume of unsustainable wildlife trade and the rate of loss of globally significant biodiversity in Indonesia and East and Southeast Asia.

#### 3. MF4P (Multi-stakeholder Forestry Programme Phase II)

A program funded by the UK Department for International Development through the Ministry of Environment and Forestry (KLHK) and the UK's Department for International Development (DFID) to strengthen forest governance and market reforms to reduce the illegal use of timber.

#### 4. FIP (Forest Investment Program)

The Forest Investment Program (FIP) supports developing countries' readiness efforts in implementing REDD+ by providing financing to improve infrastructure and institutional readiness to assist adaptation to the impacts of climate change on forests, improved forest management practices, and the development of a national forest carbon market (NFCM), which reduces emissions and protects carbon stores, as well as contributes to increasing access to the multiple benefits of forest resources, including conservation, biodiversity protection, enhancing community rights, poverty reduction, and increasing the welfare of rural communities.

### 3.0 Meso Level (Institutions in the Sector)

#### 3.1 District level institutions to empower women

The pilot project districts have implemented gender mainstreaming, including gender-responsive planning and budgeting. For example, Sintang Regency has increased farmers' welfare by 30% through forming several clusters, for example, farmer co-ops, fish clusters, etc. Communities are encouraged to maximize the processing of agricultural products in order to derive benefits


---
**Page 130**
---

to the broader community. Most of the women involved in programs in Sintang are in the environmental sector. The Sintang District Government emphasizes the role of women in environmental management and forest protection. The district government always collaborates with the Sintang District Women's Empowerment Office (abbreviated as Bappedalitbang). One of the obstacles encountered in activities carried out at the village level is lack of funds.

In addition, the district government has collaborated with universities to compile academic papers and publications on forest management. The district government also collaborates with women in villages areas. The district government and universities also collaborate to form village assisted women's groups (VAGs) which are formed by women who have been trained in various aspects of forest management.

Another initiative is the involvement of women in forest management and social forestery programs. Based on the results of FGDs in Sintang District, information was obtained that women's involvement in forest management has increased significantly since 2015 compared to before the formation of men, especially after the Village Forest Management Agency (LDPH) was suspended due to the pandemic. The increase is also due to the fact that women are more involved in managing forests, especially in farming activities. The representation of women in LDPH management, which previously did not exist, decreased from 14 to eight people. In addition, out of 50 KUPS, 27 KUPS were managed by women and 23 KUPS were managed by men.

Another example that the involvement of women and men is working together can be seen in an oil palm plantation in Sintang District. More women are working in oil palm plantations because they have more knowledge about the environment compared to men, especially in terms of land management. Likewise, women who occupy managerial positions also exist in the management of oil palm plantations (Plantation Companies, Sintang District FGD, May 23, 2023). However, there are still some obstacles that need to be overcome such as lack of synchronization regarding the provision of assistance needed at the site level. The institutions that arrive on-site do not have a clear understanding of the needs and requirements for the management of forest products, done mainly by women. This includes protecting the forest, and women's involvement in the protection of forests is still low because they are more focused on looking after and care for it so that the forest can indeed be maintained by women (LDPH, Sintang District FGD, May 23, 2023).

Likewise, women who occupy managerial positions also exist in the management of oil palm plantations (Plantation Companies, Sintang District FGD, May 23, 2023). However, there are still some obstacles that need to be overcome such as lack of synchronization regarding the provision of assistance needed at the site level. The institutions that arrive on-site do not have a clear understanding of the needs and requirements for the management of forest products, done mainly by women. This includes protecting the forest, and women's involvement in the protection of forests is still low because they are more focused on looking after and care for it so that the forest can indeed be maintained by women (LDPH, Sintang District FGD, May 23, 2023).

Another example that the involvement of women and men is working together can be seen in an oil palm plantation in Sintang District. More women are working in oil palm plantations because they have more knowledge about the environment compared to men, especially in terms of land management. ### 3.4.1 The situation of women and men in project target areas

Based on group discussions with several community members in the village, information was obtained that women and men share roles in forest and land use. Women’s groups in land use can only obtain assistance from government programs through a cooperative or association basis (Source: results of the questionnaire at district level 2023). In one Women Farmers Group, there are 14 male members and 16 female members; in another Women Farmers Group, there are 14 male members and 16 female members. Jaya Bersama Women Farmers Group is one of the Women Farmers Groups that has been working with Forest Stakeholders Units (abbreviated as KPH). The Department of Agriculture and Forestry provides training to women farmers on how to use agricultural equipment, and some are related to agroforestry programs, including working with the Peat and Marshland Management Agency. In addition, the government provides assistance in obtaining tools and assistance that are used for agroforestry activities is high. However, even though there are many government programs, women still have not been able to obtain just the government. The government can provide little assistance related to agroforestry. One of the obstacles faced by women farmers is the lack of knowledge in managing forests and increasing the creative economy. For example, basket weaving training, which has already been carried out for several years, but there are still no women who want to sell. Usually, the training activities are carried out in collaboration with Dekranasda, the Office of Women's Empowerment and Child Protection, and other government agencies (result of discussion with Subur Makmur Farmers Group & Makmur Women Farmers Group in Sintang, 23 May 2023).

In addition to that, the village government has recently formed KUPS (Social Forestry Business Group) under the Village Forest Management Agency (LPHD). The LPHD will later collaborate with the Department of Environment and Forestry. According to the Department of Environment and Forestry, KUPS aims to ensure that programs owned by districts under LPHD can be carried out in a more inclusive manner. In reality, there are only three KUPS, where each KUPS must include a gender perspective in the programmes being carried out. Because it had just been formed, the management structure in KUPS still needs to be improved and developed. The reason is that women have not yet been involved because the KUPS must still represent justice and gender equality. It is hoped that in the future, more women will participate in KUPS activities so that they can see that women have many skills that can be explored according to the needs of the programme/activity itself (Abstracted from the results of discussions with LPHD in Kubu Raya Regency, 20 May 2023).

This data shows that women play a role in carrying out forest-based activities but have yet to be involved as stakeholders in the management of the village forests under the Forest Management Institution (LPHD). Women have not been involved as LPHD managers because there is no gender sensitivity of community leaders, and they consider women's primary


---
**Page 133**
---

responsibility to be domestic affairs. Increasing gender sensitivity at the village level, both for village officials, LPHD, and others, needs to be done so that every activity they carry out always takes into account gender equality. This includes identifying women's roles in planning programs/activities, planning, designing budgets, implementing and monitoring and evaluating them. In addition, it is also necessary to ensure that women are involved at the village level to be significantly involved in organizations at the villagesite level (Abstracted from the results of discussions with LPHD in Kubu Raya Regency, 20 May 2023).

# Conclusions for the project

## Women play a significant role in carrying out various forestry activities, but inequalities remain with regard to access to information and training. It is important to ensure that women have equal opportunities as men in forest utilization and forest protection so that women's rights significantly increase.

- Women's individual and collective knowledge and experience in natural resource management are valuable assets for climate change adaptation at all levels (local, national, and international). These skills and experiences at different levels equip women with unique skills to engage in land-based mitigation and adaptation activities. However, women are rarely involved in making forest management decisions or participating in the planning of forest projects. This is one of the reasons why the participation of women in forest management and decision-making, starting from designing, planning, implementing, monitoring, evaluating and reviewing the benefits of land-based mitigation and adaptation activities.

- At the provincial level, West Kalimantan does not have any operational regulations related to gender mainstreaming or gender equality. In the forestry sector, although regulations related to gender mainstreaming and the implementation of environmental and forestry management regulations are still running separately and are not integrated into one regulation, it is necessary for the government to harmonize and to develop gender mainstreaming material for KKLH/CLHK.

- Inequality in access to information and training at all levels reduces the speed of women's adaptation in dealing with climate change. Opportunities to obtain information that are not the same between women and men make vulnerability more pronounced among women than men.

- There are potential actors/agencies involved in adaptation and mitigation actions. However, there is no synchronization regarding providing assistance needed at the site level for women to be able to participate effectively in forest management to strengthen and develop collaboration between related actors and institutions in integrated forest management.

- Cases of sexual violence occur in the field, but their handling is not optimal because they are still considered taboo. For this reason, it is necessary to strengthen gender equality awareness among all parties involved in forest management and implementers. It is necessary to develop SOPs with a gender perspective by taking sides with victims and using young people to campaign against sexual violence based on the principle of zero tolerance.

- No affirmative action regulation requires women's involvement in land-based adaptation and mitigation activities, which results in the minimization of the minimum percentage of women's representation in the Forest Management Plan (FMP) and Local Development Plan (LDPH) and increases female and gender equality in the utilization of forest products.

- The most significant contributor to the gender gap is the economic aspect. Meanwhile, important contributors to gender views are legislative membership and the implementation of affirmative action regulations. It is necessary for the government to create job opportunities for women in Social Forestry Enterprises through minimum policy provisions, to provide leadership training for women who are KWT (Women Farmers Groups) members, to encourage women's participation in forest management and to encourage women's representation as administrators in formal institutions such as Forest Management Units. The government also needs to provide support related to land-based mitigation and adaptation such as Village Forest Management.


---

Groups (KPHG), Social Forestry Business Groups (KUPS), LPHD (Village Forest Management Institute).
• The principle of improving collaboration between organizations responsible for integrating a gender perspective and technical institutions directly accountable for development in the environmental and forestry regarding the same vision regarding gender equality. This ensures that both social and economic activities can run well and are carried out with the principles of justice and gender equality.

### 4. Gender responsiveness and expertise, as well as gender equality in the part-ner organisations(s)

#### 4.1 Political Partners

Following the Presidential Decree Number 9 of 2000 on gender mainstreaming in national planning, the Ministry of Environment and Forestry has imposed gender-responsive planning, implementation, monitoring, and evaluation of its policies, programs, and activities. In order to ensure gender equality in all aspects of its work, the Ministry issued Circular Letter No. P-528/MENHUT-II/PEG/2004 concerning Guidelines for Implementation of Gender Mainstreaming in the Ministry of Environment and Forestry. The circular letter was followed by Circular Letter Number P-569/MENHUT-II/1/CONCERNING GUIDELINES FOR GENDER RESPONSIVE PLANNING AND BUDGETING to provide guidance on gender-responsive planning and budgeting for all units within the ministry preparing gender responsive planning and budgeting. In 2017, The Regulation of the Minister of Environment and Forestry of the Republic of Indonesia Number P-318/MENHUKET-JEN/PER/2017 concerning Guidelines for Gender Responsive Planning and Budgeting in the Ministry of Environment and Forestry was issued. This regulation was issued to encourage, make effective, and improve gender-responsive planning and budgeting in all units within the ministry and forestry and Forest sector. Work units within the scope of the Ministry of Environment and Forestry. The Ministry of Environment and Forestry (KLHK) has received the award of "Anugrah Prabu Eka Pura" which is a national award.

Nevertheless, horizontal and vertical coordination with regional governments at the provincial and district levels in the implementation of gender mainstreaming policies in the environmental and forestry sector has not yet been implemented, so gender mainstreaming (PUG) has not been carried out in an integrated manner in environmental and forestry development at the regional level. The availability of data disaggregated by sex in the scope of environment and forestry is not yet available. This makes it difficult to design a project that can be used as a tool needed in designing a project to monitor and evaluate its results to provide fair and equal benefits for women, men, and groups. Externally, there are still socio-cultural values that prevent women from being involved in strategic roles in decision-making.

Kubu Raya District and Sintang District

Kubu Raya district has the most gender mainstreaming regulations, 4 (four) regulations, in-cluding:

1. Kubu Raya Regency Regional Long-Term Development Plan (2009)-2029
2. Regulation of the Region of Kubu Raya Regency Number 28 of 2018 concerning the Implementation of Gender Mainstreaming


---
**Page 137**
---

3. Regulation of the Regent of Kubu Raya Regency Number 104 of 2019 concerning the Regional Action Plan for Gender Mainstreaming for 2019-2024

4. Regulation of the Regent of Kubu Raya Regency Number 50 of 2021 concerning Synchronized and Integrated Gender Data System (SISGEND)

Sintang District has one gender-related regulation, namely Sintang District Head Regulation Number 8 of 2013 concerning the Establishment of a Special Fund for the Empowerment of Women and Children in Sintang District). However, both Kubu Raya Regency and Sintang District have not yet had gender mainstreaming regulations in the environmental and forestry sectors.

# Implementing partners

Although the policy regarding Gender Mainstreaming in the environmental and forestry spheres has been formulated, at the implementation stage it still experiences internal and external obstacles. Regarding internal barriers, the institutions responsible for integrating gender equality into planning, budgeting, monitoring, evaluation, and reporting of environmental policies, programs, or activities are not yet gender sensitive. The availability of gender-sensitive information on women's participation in decision-making processes regarding natural resource management and other related issues is still limited. Women's representation in participatory groups in the environment and forestry sector are not yet operationalized in regulatory rules of a similar nature as a reference for related agencies in carrying out gender mainstreaming in the environmental and forestry sectors. The availability of information on women's participation in the ecological and forestry scope is also not available in an up-to-date and adequate manner. Meanwhile, existing social norms and cultural values still prevent women from being able to play a strategic role in decision-making.

Understanding gender equality and gender mainstreaming

Such a concept is emerging, although capacity improvements are still needed in the general sense of gender equality and gender mainstreaming. Kubu Raya District, which is a pilot project, has carried out several activities related to gender equality and gender mainstreaming through the KUKPS under the Village Forest Management Agency (LPHP), where the LPHP collaborates with Forestry Department, Environment and Forestry Department, and the Regency Government of Kubu Raya. The establishment of KUKPS is intended so that programs owned by the Regency can be carried out in a more efficient manner. However, the representation of women in KUKPS management is still not optimal. There are 10 members on the core board (the core board is 2 men) and only two female representatives from each village. In addition to creating the KUKPS, Kubu Raya Regency also established a Women's Village Group (KWTA). The KWTA consists of seven groups, each consisting of 140 (one hundred and forty) women. KWTA Jayabera is one of the KWTA that empowers female members for agroforestry activities. KWTA has collaborated with BRIAGA (Bogor Research Institute Agroforestry Agency) in providing training to farmers, which is carried out between KWTA and partners in the form of seed assistance and assistance with agricultural tools. The second activity carried out by the Regency Government through KUKPS working with BRIAGA (stands for Peat and Mangrove Restoration Agency). One of the activities carried out is the empowerment of women's groups related to increasing the creative economy, for example, basket weaving training, which is carried out in collaboration with the Department of Industry and Trade. The third activity is the empowerment of women's groups related to products ready to sell. Usually, the training activities are carried out in collaboration with the Department of Industry and Trade, BRIAGA, and the Women's Beauty Makeup Organization.

The Sintang district government emphasized the need for women's involvement in environmental management through gender mainstreaming. The Regent of Sintang District Government always try to involve women in every activity, including proposing that women participate as members of the local government. In addition, the empowerment activities carried out by the local government to farmer groups in the village is related to rubber. Since 2006, the local government has assisted with superior rubber seeds, which will be carried out until 2031 by

# 4.3 Strategic partners

Many funding institutions went to come to Kubu Raya or Sintang to reduce emissions. In addition, many of these institutions, especially farmers, are already actively supporting the community's welfare. The district government emphasizes the involvement of women in environmental and social safeguarding. However, the project team wants to work with the district government. Therefore, multi-stakeholder collaboration must be built and enhanced. This will ensure that the project is sustainable after the end of the project period. Women must always be considered an integral part by opening access and to participation of women in all project activities. The project team should also consider gender mainstreaming in decision-making. Thus, national and international commitment to "no one will be left behind" can be realized. This means that the project team should ensure that women are provided fair and equal benefits.

# 4.4 Executing Entities

This chapter provides an overview of the Executing Entity's strategies and capacities on promoting gender equity and gender mainstreaming. The project also aims to enhance existing capacities of field facilitators, GESI focal points, and other key stakeholders (see Chapter 3). As per Annex 8b – Call for Session 2 Technical Advisor for Environmental and Social Safeguards, Gender, and Indigenous Peoples (ESGVI Advisor) will be re-named as the Gender Advisor. The ESGVI Advisor is responsible for ensuring that gender measures are implemented according to the GAF, which will mainstream gender positive action in all project activities. In addition, a M&E system that includes all GAF indicators will be developed.

## 4.4.1 BPDHL

BPDHL is committed to monitoring and reporting on gender results and impacts which is part of safeguard implementation monitoring. Gender outcomes and impacts of programs are to be reported annually. Beneficiaries and beneficiaries' entitlements are required to report on gender outcomes and impacts in the annual progress reports. The M&E system will also include all program closure. In addition, they need to provide gender disaggregated data where relevant (that is when there is a possibility to collect outcome or output data that can be disaggregated by gender).

According to lot Environmental and Social Management System (ESMS) Guidelines, BPDHL shall ensure the implementation of the following: i) The provisions in the Forest Protection Chapter No. 3/2022 (Appendix A) which contains BPDHL top management's commitment to provide access to information, participation and justice as well as non-discriminatory treatment for all stakeholders; ii) The provisions on gender-based violence, discriminatory behavior, harassment, bullying, violence and/or intimidation based differences whether physical, written, verbal or virtual. BPDHL provides the following support tools:

i. Policies and regulations/ agreements;

ii. Monitoring and evaluation;

iii. Complaint and handling mechanisms for gender-based violence;

iv. Gender action plan to address the interests of vulnerable groups in the

v. Implementation of BPDHL institutions as well as programs and/or activities that receive funding support from BPDHL.


---
**Page 139**
---

BPDHL’s ESMS Guidelines also demand a social and gender impact assessment study if there is potential for direct, indirect or cumulative impacts on vulnerable communities, including women (Principle 12 – Social quality of society, gender mainstreaming and protection of vul- nerable groups and communities). The BPDHL has developed a stakeholder engagement framework when consulting with local communities, especially women and other vulnerable groups need to be involved in the process. This framework is based on the principles of participation (Principle 10 – Involvement of stakeholders and disclosure of informa- tions). Stakeholder engagement plans are developed, which includes strategies and steps that will be implemented for each stakeholder group. The BPDHL has also recruited a Gender Safeguard Specialist to lead and provide assistance on gender safeguards to the BPDHL, including to provide assistance to effectively develop and implement gender-sensitive policies and procedures within the organization, ensuring that these comply with both local and international requirements.

### 4.4.2 Solidaridad

Solidaridad has a gender policy and strategy which provides guidance in a continuing effort to integrate gender into their work. They have developed a practical implementation framework called the Gender ABC, which consists of the following three main elements:

- Analyzing and addressing barriers to participation
- Building capacity for women
- Creating togetherness based on shared experiences

Solidaridad is committed to social development, including gender inclusion, ensuring trans- formations in society. The organization recognizes that reducing all forms of inequality (SDG 10) and gender equality (SDG 5) can only be a reality when compro- mises are made with the most vulnerable groups. Solidaridad prioritizes addressing underly- ing barriers to inclusion are put in force. Therefore, Solidaridad proactively ensures that those who are more vulnerable have access to opportunities and resources. This is achieved through a solidaridad network’s work. This requires that power imbalances are addressed and that local communities are empowered with the tools for established participation, influence, and the ability to affect change in their respective contexts.

To achieve this, Solidaridad prioritises a rights-based approach to economic development across the Solidaridad Network – particularly with vulnerable civil society actors. In practice, this means that Solidaridad works to ensure that all women have equal access to resources, starting from power imbalances through a localised analysis, which is followed by locally-led design of interventions and programmes. The organization also ensures that all projects it implements, in partnership with programme stakeholders (both rights-holders and duty-bear- ers), will be gender-sensitive and inclusive. This means ensuring that women have equal playing field and equal benefits for all. Additionally, indicator-specific indicators (especially on strengthening the capacity of women) are used to monitor progress against the programme’s commitments to addressing issues of exclusion. This serves the pur- pose of implementing programmes that help make systems and supply chains more inclusive, and therefore more sustainable.

At the network level, Solidaridad has a Gender and Social Inclusion Thematic Learning Group, gender focal points within regional excellence centers and countries who help drive gender equality, and a gender strategy that includes a focus on women’s economic empowerment through a feminization of supply chains model. This model promotes creating an enabling environment

# Conclusions for the project

• However, despite the coordination with regional governments at the provincial and district levels, especially in integrating gender in the development of the environmental and warehouse sectors, has not yet been implemented, so gender mainstreaming (PGU) is still a challenge.

• The availability of data disaggregated by sex in the scope of environment and forestry projects is very limited. Although this data is very much needed in designing a project to monitor and evaluate the results to promote women's participation among other marginalized groups. Meanwhile, externally, there are still sociocultural values that prevent women from being able to play a strategic role in decision-making.

## Gender responsiveness and expertise in the project among project staff in the country

### Equal opportunities in the team

There are no particular barriers between male and female staff to advance their career in GIZ. However, although there is gender diversity on the team was insufficient an imbalance in GIZ. In addition this project is a new project. No staff can be assessed. GIZ Indonesia has a clear statement on gender equality and equal opportunities for all employees. The company adheres to the principle of equal pay for work equal is binding. A National employee committee exists, which, among other things, ensures that the principle of equal pay is applied (GIZ 2019a). Besides supporting the partners in proactive and improved recruitment and promotion processes of female staff, the GIZ project should emphasize the importance of gender mainstreaming. The GIZ team has a good understanding of the importance of gender equality for women but men do particularly well in response to quantified analysis of the costs of gender inequality, the GIZ team is aware that this is an important topic and will be included in communication and capacity development measures.

### Gender expertise of the team members

To promote sustainable development, gender equality must be an integral part of the GIZ approach. In order to achieve these objectives, GIZ has committed itself to considering gender equality in the design of all projects. Promoting gender equality is a stated goal of Germany's Development Cooperation Strategy (BMZ 2014). The German Committee for Development Assistance Committee (DAC) of the Organisation for Economic Co-operation and Development (OECD) has set up a Gender Equality Working Group (GEWG) under the German Committee for Development Assistance (GDA) to determine what effects these measures might have on gender equality and to advise GIZ accordingly. In addition, GIZ is also supported by the German Federal Ministry for Economic Cooperation and Development (BMZ), 2011). general analyses for monitoring and evaluation purposes are carried out in various early stages of developing a project or follow-on-measure. The results of a gender analysis will be included in the project proposals under the cross-sectoral strategy of the BMZ on gender equality in German development policy from 2014 and with the second Gender Action Plan, 2016 to 20


---
**Page 141**
---

Other commissioning parties, such as the Green Climate Fund (GCF) and the European Union (EU), also require a gender analysis.

Everyone involved in the project should participate in gender training. However, there needs to be more specific training offered by GIZ Indonesia. Even though all staff has passed the training, it is still necessary to keep reminding and discussing gender issues in the environment and forestry sector. It would be useful if GIZ Indonesia could provide a manual or guidelines for such orientation on implementing and improving gender relations in the project activities. It would be necessary that this manual should be based on the experiences of different projects already implemented on the ground. This includes adequate knowledge management based on the experience of the different projects (see summary, page 20).

## Conclusions for the project

- An expert needs to be recruited for the project that provides technical support to ensure gender equality is mainstreamed in capacity-building activities and mainstreamed across all project activities (see ESGI expert in GAP).
- GIZ Indonesia has a clear statement on gender equality, as does the project. As well as this, there should be an effective recruitment and promotion process of female staff. GIZ projects must clearly emphasize the importance of gender mainstreaming to ensure that women are not discriminated against.
- Special importance should be given to preventing, detecting, and responding effectively to incidents of SEAH in line with GCF's Revised Environmental and Social Policy (ESPI).
- Gender training for all levels of staff needs to be conducted. Regular gender training for all levels – from management to field staff – is needed. Project staff need to be trained to identify gender issues in the field and how to monitor gender equality in projects.

## Gender Action Plan

to the GCF Funding Proposal

### Land-based Mitigation and Adaptation through a Jurisdictional Approach in West-Kalimantan

23/07/2024  
Version 3.0  

Submitted by:  
Deutsche Gesellschaft für Internationale Zusammenarbeit (GIZ) GmbH

**Page 144**
---

1. **Background and Introduction**

This Gender Action Plan (GAP) is aimed at the Green Climate Fund (GCF) project "Land-Based Mitigation and Adaptation through a Jurisdictional Approach in West Kalimantan". It becomes part of the Gender Analysis and Gender Mainstreaming Assessment (GA, see Annex 8a). This document contains specific elements related to gender that must be considered in project design and during the implementation of the activities. It helps ensure effective gender mainstreaming by integrating gender considerations into all mitigation and adaptation actions and minimize social, gender-related, and climate-related risks in all its actions. The GAP ensures consistent integration of gender perspectives throughout the project cycle and creates an environment that supports drivers of change and positive gender dynamics and manage and mitigate potential adverse risks during the project.

|  |  |  |  |  |  |  | M&E Budget |  |

The resources for the GAP are included in the overall project costs.
